#4142. How Your Imagination Can Work for You or Against You

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

I have a pretty vivid imagination, sometimes for the good, sometimes not. I love it when it helps me to create new stories or songs, or when I can daydream about fun times ahead. But it can also lead to fear or worry, like when I imagine something terrible befalling the people or places I love. Is there some way to nurture the positive aspects of imagination while minimizing the problematic? According to Cassandra Vieten, director of research at the Arthur C. Clarke Center for Human Imagination, there is. In her new book, Imagine That, she explains imagination’s importance in our everyday lives—a way to understand our past, navigate the present, and explore future possibilities. If we learn more about its inner workings, she argues, we can use it more consciously and deliberately to create the kind of life we want. “Imagination is something we all possess, something we can all practice, for free and with equal opportunity,” says Vieten. “When someone develops this capacity, they gain something almost magical: the ability to see multiple futures simultaneously and navigate toward the preferred ones.” Imagination as part of everyday life Many of us tend to conflate imagination with creativity, thinking that being able to imagine new ideas is the same as bringing them to fruition. While the two may indeed be related, this is far too limited a view of what imagination can be, writes Vieten. “Imagination is an essential, foundational capacity that allows us to simulate reality and transcend it to explore possibilities beyond the known,” she says. In our everyday lives, imagination is what helps us make predictions as well as come up with novel (or creative) ideas. For example, we might imagine how stopping for a coffee on our way to work will help us focus (simulation imagination, based on past experience). But we might also use our imagination to transcend what’s known and come up with a totally new innovation or product idea once we arrive at the office (generative imagination). “Imagination weaves throughout the fabric of our cognitive, sensory, and relational lives—you are constantly speculating, visualizing, rehearsing, wondering,” she writes. These types of imagination show up differently in the brain, writes Vieten. Simulation is primarily centered in the default mode network (DMN), a part of the brain that remains active when we aren’t engaging in a specific task. The DMN is where we tend to mull over future interactions, plans, or options, often subconsciously (or even unconsciously). Generative imagination, on the other hand, comes by freeing ourselves from the DMN’s limitations (or, some would say, tyranny). If we can get out of the everyday chatter and let our minds truly wander freely around disparate parts of the brain, making new neural connections, we have a better chance of generating something truly unique. “Rather than simulating or iterating, comparing and contrasting, generative imagination is focused on combining, reconfiguring, or transcending existing knowledge to generate novel outputs,” writes Vieten. Either way, imagination isn’t frivolous, she writes; it’s important for our everyday functioning as well as creative self-expression. It helps us solve problems, while also expanding possibilities of what could be, giving us “the gift of wonder, whimsy, and surprise, the whoas and the wows of our lives,” she writes. Imagination’s darker side Imagination can sometimes be problematic, though. For example, we might imagine that our spouse is mad at us and worry about what might happen when she gets home from work when, actually, she’s not mad at all. This can cause us needless worry, not to mention potentially trigger a fight. We can also imagine that other people are unpleasant or even dangerous based on untested biases. These can be formed in part via our imagination, which tries to prepare us for unknown encounters, often behind the scenes at an unconscious level. “We regularly imagine entire narratives, scenes, and interactions without intending to or even knowing we’re doing it,” writes Vieten. Our imagination is often busy filling in the missing parts of our memories and perceptions, which are by nature incomplete. Memories, rather than being etched in our minds, are recreated (and often changed) each time they’re recalled. Similarly, we can’t perceive everything at once, so our imagination completes the picture for us, adding interpretations, too. This means it’s easy to corrupt memories or miss details of a situation—which can sometimes lead to problems (think eyewitness accounts of crimes, for example). Understanding these darker sides of imagination can help us tame them somewhat, says Vieten. “You can practice bringing greater attention to what you are imagining, recognize it as imagination, and decide whether it holds useful information, is irrelevant, or is actually causing harm.” How to increase your imaginative capabilities While the book covers the neuroscience behind imagination, it’s also about how to harness our imagination more effectively. Your personal imagination style may depend, in part, on your genetics (such as neurodiversity), culture, and life experiences. Understanding this can help you figure out where you might want to try moving the needle one way or the other. To assess your own imaginative strengths and weaknesses, here are some key imagination modes Vieten outlines in her book. 1. Receptiveness. “How willing you are to imagine—and how receptive you are to whatever bubbles up.” If you are high in receptivity, you may find it easy to do guided visualizations, for example; if low in receptivity, you may be less willing to imagine unknown scenarios. 2. Presence. How much do you embody your imagined experiences? If you tend to be absorbed, immersed, and emotionally involved in imagined scenarios, you’re high in presence; if you tend to observe these imaginings from a distance, you’re less so. 3. Embellishment. “How vivid, detailed, and elaborate people make their imaginings.” Those high in embellishment can imagine intricate scenarios with much more detail, clarity, nuance, and complexity. Those low in embellishment might be more “big picture” folks, who may not be able to conjure up details as much. 4. Originality. How much your imagination is fueled by what is or could be, based on reality, versus how much it veers from that and is based more on fantasy and novelty. 5. Temporality. How much your imagination is associated with time (in other words, assessing the past or projecting into the future) versus how much it is agnostic around time (not based on any particular time). (Vieten admits the science on this mode is not completely settled, but says there appear to be some differences in how people engage with time while imagining.) Each of these modes has pros and cons, she argues. For example, being high in receptivity may mean you’re more cognitively flexible and open to new experiences, but can also mean you’re overly compliant and easily manipulated. Similarly, the upside of being high in originality is that it can fuel advancements in art and science, though it can lead to boredom with everyday life. Considering my own strengths and weaknesses, I noticed that I seem to be high in receptivity and embodiment, but low in embellishment and originality (and mixed when it comes to temporality). Recognizing this makes me think about paying more attention to details in my environment and playing around with more fantastical ideas, especially if I’m wanting to fuel my creativity. To that end, Vieten provides avenues for stretching our imagination in different ways. Some ideas for accessing different aspects of imagination deliberately include rehearsing something before doing it, brainstorming ideas, engaging all of our senses when imagining something, reframing an idea from a different perspective, and freewriting. Vieten also suggests creating environments conducive to being more spontaneously creative. Setting time aside to daydream, being in solitude where your mind can wander, taking walks in the woods to de-stress, or, possibly, taking psychedelic drugs (in a safe, controlled environment) can all help us disengage our DMN and allow creative thoughts to enter more freely. “One of the key ways to make imagination welcome is to remove the practical pressures of the world,” she writes. Beyond self-help Imagine That is full of suggestions for unleashing your imagination, all based on the emerging science of imagination that Vieten and her colleagues (and others) are exploring. But while the book reads mostly as a self-help guide, there’s also an important message about why using our imagination is beneficial to society as a whole. Too many of us are experiencing the hopelessness and despair that come with being alive right now, writes Vieten. It’s too easy to get lost in the negative news cycles and focus on all of the problems in the world, making it more difficult to imagine anything better for our collective future. Learning how to engage our imagination more readily can create a sense of hope in our current situation. Not only can it bring more joy and aliveness to our individual lives, countering malaise, it can also lead to new ways of collaborating with others and innovating to solve societal problems, thereby providing a better life for everyone. “So many of the issues facing us locally and globally are rooted in our collective inability to imagine new solutions, alternative futures, creative pathways forward that could build a world that promotes thriving for all,” writes Vieten. “My deepest hope is that [you] take a deeper dive into the science, the practice, and the magic of imagination and [see] how it can re-enchant your life and our world.”

#4132. The Overlooked Ways Love Shows Up in Urban Spaces

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Over the past two decades, my doctoral students and I have focused on studying love, care, kindness, and compassion in urban settings. Why? Because social science research on life in America’s cities is desperately incomplete. Much like the vision of urban life that permeates most of American popular culture, social science research imagines urban areas as sites of overwhelmedness, disengagement, social and moral crisis, threat, and violence. But, as someone who has lived and worked in large urban centers—New York City, Chicago, Detroit, Istanbul—I know firsthand that urban centers are also sites of human virtue. People in urban contexts, including those living in high-stress, oversurveilled, underresourced communities, draw daily from deep wells of love, compassion, empathy, kindness, and generosity in the service of caring for each other. From holding doors open for one another, to offering seats on buses and trains, to stopping to help with directions, to paying unexpected compliments, city-dwellers create environments that are saturated with powerful but easily missed acts of care.  My former students and I have been eager to understand how love, generosity, and care manifest and operate in the hyperdiverse, densely populated, loud, fast-paced complexities of urban spaces. Through surveys and interviews, we have sought to understand how adults residing in big cities define love, how they see love being expressed, and their views of the cascading effects of giving and receiving care, generosity, and love.  We have learned that the decision to express love and care for others in large cities is not simply a moral or emotional decision. We offer two examples of the lens needed to notice the presence of love in cities. First, we have learned that identities matter in experiences of compassion, love, and care between strangers. Urban dwellers’ identity (for example, race or gender), lived experience, context, familial, cultural, and legal systems’ realities work together to shape decisions about whether to extend acts of love to people you do not know. For example, Kehinde, a 26-year-old African American man living in a large, densely populated city, described his experience of giving and receiving kindness, care, and love in his city as shaped by how he navigates other people seeing him as a Black man. “I will see folks all the time that may need a hand with their bags or crossing the street or just little things like that. And I take a second, hesitate,” Kehinde says. “I don’t want to scare them. I don’t want to come off as a threat.” To get through these moments, Kehinde describes engaging in a practice of analysis and shape-shifting. Before he steps in to offer help, he assesses the environment and changes the way he carries himself so that people will perceive him as a possible helper rather than as a threat. He says: “I smile a lot more. My voice might change a bit.” He might shrink his body and keep his head down, to avoid seeming intimidating. After helping someone, he moves along without lingering. Reflecting, he acknowledges not being his full, true self in these interactions. “I have to realize I’ve been socialized—made to believe that this is what I need to do in order to navigate the situation.” Kehinde’s story highlights the point that identities matter in economies of care. The images and stereotypes that we construct about people come at a cost even in moments of compassion. Stereotypes affect who we perceive as deserving of care, concern, and protection. Stereotypes also inform our expectations about who can be trusted to help. Stereotyping is not unique to urban settings. But because urban settings tend to be especially diverse spaces, they create abundant opportunities to study how identities and the social scripts we hold affect the way that strangers care for each other across lines of difference. What Kehinde and others bring to the fore is the reality that the targets of stereotyping are aware of the ways that they are perceived. They learn that to be seen as helpful rather than as threatening, they need to read situations correctly, make adjustments to their approaches (e.g., keep “appropriate” distance), and exit situations strategically. Targets of stereotyping must strike a balance between their own deep-seated motivations to behave caringly and their desire to stay safe. For individuals like Kehinde, miscalculations can be costly. If, in the effort to behave lovingly and compassionately, someone is misidentified as a threat, that can lead to a range of consequences from being rebuffed and embarrassed to becoming the target of violence, arrest, or death. The end result is that people consciously or unconsciously learn the rules of engagement around helping strangers. A second lesson that has emerged from studying love in urban settings is that love lives in the built environment of cities. I highlight this lesson in my Positive Psychology in Urban Settings classes. Cities are often saturated with murals, billboards, and symbols that convey messages of love, solidarity, and belonging. As residents move through neighborhood streets, they may encounter signs prominently posted in shop windows or near the entrances of religious institutions that remind them that “Black Lives Matter,” “Love Is Love,” or “Immigrants Are Welcome Here.” People display rainbow flags and national flags from apartment windows, on cars, and in other spaces, declaring both their own identities and pride for and solidarity with those identities. The architecture of urban spaces may also play a role in expressions of love and care. In many urban neighborhoods, porches, stoops, and front steps serve as places of connection and care. Simple greetings (e.g., “Morning, baby!,” “You doing good?”) voiced by neighbors sitting on their steps serve as ways of spatializing love, care, and connection in urban landscapes.  I return here to my initial point. Our studies of urban areas are desperately incomplete and will remain incomplete until we more fully examine how various forms of human goodness—including love and care—flourish in urban settings. Likewise, our understandings of love and care will remain underdeveloped until we explore how these experiences emerge in the most densely populated, diverse, complex social structures in which people live. We will truly understand love when we understand how it is titrated through place, context, history, culture, religion, structures of power, identities, and a host of individual and relational factors. Urban centers give us powerful opportunities to know love.

#4100. 17 Ideas From Educators to Start the School Year With Joy

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Joy! What a beautiful way to start the school year, not only because it feels good, but also because science shows that joy is linked closely to learning, as well as individual and collective flourishing. We asked Greater Good Education newsletter readers to share a favorite strategy for bringing joy into their school or classroom at the beginning of the year. The responses did not disappoint! Here are a few that span ages, time commitments, and subject areas. Dance party Friday Laura M. (Newark, DE): “Dancing it out” is a strategy that has been shown to reduce stress, release held feelings, and encourage kids to be in the moment. Providing time for activities like dancing is often seen as an “extra.” Building this activity into our weekly schedule has become a favorite stress-relieving activity—adding tremendous joy and connection between children and children and children and teachers. Related Greater Good in Education (GGiE) practices: The World Is a Dance; Getting to Know Each Other Through Dance; We Are Better Dancing Together Finding joy in our lives Josh (New Haven, CT): Every Monday morning meeting, students greet each other by asking, “What brought you joy (made you smile/laugh) over the weekend?” Laura H.: At the end of the day, with second graders, we share the activity from our day that brought the most joy. If I wasn’t there for it (i.e., recess, music, art, PE), I ask them to include details. Related GGiE practices: Noticing Kindness; Giving Thanks; Looking for Gratitude in School; Sharing Gratitude Stories Enchanting joy through surf, science, and storytelling Emi (Lobitos, Peru): Identifying forms of enchantment—I use this approach in our Women & Water Program. It’s an all-girls (ages 3–16) surf, science, and storytelling program based in Lobitos, a tiny fishing village in Northern Peru. We use “enchantment” as a way for young girls to foster their curiosity, creativity, and care for themselves, peers, community, and the coastal environment. We do this through photography (taking “macro” photos of small, wondrous things found in their environment, like a flower petal, a beetle, etc.). Or we do this in surfing—the girls notice the “awe” of riding the energy of a leftover storm until it crashes into the sand. These girls also made this song after an oil spill that rocked our community about two years ago. Related GGiE practices: Meaningful Photos for Students; Mindfully Finding Awe in Nature Sidewalk messages of joy Angela (Pleasant Hill, CA): I am a school counselor and with my students who experience anxiety we use sidewalk chalk to write positive welcoming messages and pictures in front of our school. It makes others feel welcomed and helps my students feel connected to our school. They even told me it helps get them to school the first couple of days knowing they would see their chalk messages. Related GGiE practices: Designing the Classroom to Promote Kindness; Kindness Buddy; Awe-Inspiring Affirmations “Would you rather” joy Lorien (St. Louis, MO): Have a “Would you rather…?” question on the whiteboard at the start of class—the funnier the better. Students put a checkmark next to their choice. It’s a fun way to welcome the “whole student self” into the classroom, not just their “learner selves.” Sometimes we talk about it, but often, we don’t. Related GGiE practice: Superstar Engineering joy through perseverence Pam (Muscogee County, GA): To kick off the year, my eighth graders design and build a cardboard maze that guides three different rolling spheres from start to finish. Working in teams of four, students sketch a plan, build it with cardboard and hot glue, test it, and revise their design. Nothing works perfectly the first time. Students quickly discover that failure is a useful part of the design process. By the end of the week, teams are cheering when a ping pong ball, golf ball, or rubber ball finally makes it all the way through, and that joy comes from earning it through real trial and error. It sets the tone for the whole year: Engineering is a process of building, testing, and improving. Related GGiE practice: Four 4s Discovering joy in little wins Kimberly (Williamsport, PA): Something that I introduce during the first week of classes is our 3 Good Things or 3 Little Wins Padlet. Each week students post about three good things or little wins they had during the week. Before the first post, we discuss why it is important to celebrate these things, no matter how small (e.g., a good cup of coffee!). We also discuss the impact that reflecting on these things has on our physical and psychological well-being. Each week I also post my wins for the week and comment on student posts as well. It creates a wonderful community! Related GGIE practices: Three Good Things for Students; Gratitude Journals Our favorite songs joy Jonah (Duxbury, VT): As a high school English teacher, the first writing assignment I give to students asks them to choose a (clean) song that represents them and tell me why they feel connected to this song. I then make a playlist including all the students’ songs, and play it during work times or use it to play students’ walk-up song on special days like birthdays. Related GGiE practices: Creating Musical Playlists for the Classroom; Prosocial Music Hub (13 practices to bring music into the classroom) Cake-pop stick joy Joe (Bridgeport, CT): Students interact with each other as they navigate around the crowded classroom, connected only by cake-pop sticks balanced between each other using only their index finger. Fosters concentration, cooperation, joyful failures, interconnectedness, and intimacy without actually touching one another. Related GGiE practice: 1-2-3 Clap Geological joy David (San Antonio, TX): For my introductory geology class, I work with the students to develop rules for a “rock-rock-rock” (igneous-sedimentary-metamorphic) version of “rock-paper-scissors.” Students develop the necessary hand signals and the action verbs that say what beats what. Once we have the rules established, the students engage in a best two out of three to crown the class champion. Branded university swag is available for the winners, and everyone gets a candy treat for participating. Related GGiE practice: Making Science Meaningful Joyful lies Susan (St. Paul, MN): I ask students to tell the rest of the class their name and a ridiculous lie about themselves—the more absurd the better. There’s always good-natured laughter and encouragement. Related GGiE practice: Truth or Tale Laughing joy Harry (Hailey, ID): I work to end every “session” (class, administration meeting, faculty meeting, etc.) with laughter, whether incited by me or coming from someone else. That moment becomes the punctuation. Related GGiE practices: Share a Laugh; The SEL 3 Signature Practices The joy of contribution—seeing the good in others Paige (Seattle, WA): About the second or third week of classes (graduate and undergraduate students) we begin doing this ritual that we do a few times during the semester: “The joy of contribution…I see this in you!” Students take turns identifying something they have genuinely observed in another student in the cohort/class: “One leadership capacity I see developing in you is________, because I watched you ________.” The BECAUSE matters. Examples I have heard from pre-certified teachers and principals: “I see courage building in you because I have watched you ask questions the rest of us were afraid to ask.” OR, “I see you really owning belongingness as you consistently notice who is outside the conversation and what is happening around us as you read the room.” Related GGiE practices: I See You. Everyone Matters; Behind Your Back What joy looks like Lorien (St. Louis, MO): I use a guided visualization to prompt students to think about what joyful teaching, joyful learning, and joyful interactions look like, sound like, and feel like. It makes what we’re doing much more explicit, and it helps me know what they want from me and vice versa. Related GGiE practice: Designing the Classroom to Promote Kindness Drawing for joy Angela (McMinnville, OR): A non-dominant hand drawing activity that gets us all out of our “perfection” thinking and into being learners…and laughing. Related GGiE practice: Drawing as a Way to Manage Emotions Joyful greetings Milton (San Diego, CA): As the associate principal, I stand out in front of the school in the morning with a BIG SMILE sign on and greet the students with a “Good morning & have a great day!” Some students like a fist bump or handshake as well. Related GGiE practice: Making Families Feel Welcome Closing the day with joy Donna (Arkansas): “JOY” is the grounding rhythm that closes every day, honoring the joyful moments that JUST happened, recognizing community attunement through something an OTHER person did, and celebrating internal self-efficacy via something YOU did. Each person is free to select whichever letter resonates with them to share. We pass a rainstick around the circle; once it is turned to let its gentle cascade settle into silence, the person holding it speaks. This tactile ritual and quiet sound not only brings a profound sense of calm to the transition from classroom to home, but it also serves as a gentle reminder to carry those reflections outward. J—Just happened (joyful moments): This includes moments of awe through visual art design, the natural world, or quiet classroom breakthroughs. Prompt: “What is a small moment of joy or beauty that just happened today—maybe a bird outside our window, a solved puzzle, or a moment of laughter?” O—Other-centered kindness (noticing moral beauty): This centers on acts of care, inclusion, or helpfulness witnessed throughout the day. Prompt: “Who is someone else you noticed doing something kind, caring, or helpful today—seen or unseen?” Y—Your own contribution (reflective pride): This is anchored in agency and emotional regulation, helping us to recognize how we foster our own joy. Prompt: “What is something you did today that you are proud of, or a time you showed patience and care to yourself or someone else?” Related GGiE practices: Inspiring Virtue With Moral Beauty; Stories of Moral Beauty; Noticing Kindness; Nurturing Students’ Wonder and Curiosity Thank you to all of the educators in the Greater Good community who shared activities for bringing joy into schools and classrooms. We received many, many more than we could share here. Even in the face of incredible challenges, you are bringing so much joy to your students and their families, your colleagues, and yourself. May you all have a joyful start to the new school year! We look forward to our next post in the From Our Readers series that will focus on building community through connection and belonging. Want to share an activity? Fill out this quick form! Not only will you strengthen our community, you’ll be entered into a drawing for a free self-paced course or a spot in our Flourishing Spring community of practice!

#4060. How a Video Game Helped Me Process My Grief

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Become a subscribing member today. Help us continue to bring “the science of a meaningful life” to you and to millions around the globe.

Greater Good Science Center • Magazine • In Action • In Education

In a genre built around sweetness and ease, one “cozy” video game did something far greater: It put me back together after I lost Mary.

Mary was not only my then-boyfriend’s mother, but she also became an important maternal figure when my own family life felt unstable. I accompanied her on weekly grocery store runs that included stops at Marshall’s and Dollar Tree, where I’d watch her study the prices of everything from spices to bath products. When she saw me marveling at her meticulousness, she would share wise tidbits about how she looked for deals.

Our trips often ended with a late-afternoon stop at a Vietnamese restaurant, where she treated me to a warm bowl of beef pho. She’d share what she was excited to make for dinner; I was, of course, welcome to join. In these seemingly mundane afternoons, I felt cared for without the rush of obligations. Mary stopped time for me.

Last November, Mary left behind a world of loved ones after living with cancer for 14 months. From her living room to emergency stays in the hospital, I spent almost every single day of her fight by her side. Yet in my loneliest hours, I agonize over whether I fully cherished our last months together. Did I hug her enough, and did I tell her enough how much she means to me? These questions haunted me in the months afterward.

Funny enough, it wasn’t religion that rescued me; it was Pokémon Pokopia. On the surface, it is a new social simulation game featuring the iconic Japanese creatures who first took the world by storm in 1996. I first heard of its arrival on social media through gamers who raved that it was going to be the best game of the year. I grew up on Pokémon titles, so I recognized it as a distraction from my personal reckoning with abandonment.

But what began as a spur-of-the-moment decision to try what 4 million other gamers were playing turned into a transformative journey exploring what I want my life to mean when it feels as if I have been left behind.

Pokopia falls in the “cozy” game genre, beloved for its warm colors, gentle atmospheres, and easy missions. Released in March, Pokopia stars Ditto, an amorphous purple blob Pokémon that uses its transformative abilities to imitate its human trainer and rebuild a curious post-apocalyptic world devoid of human beings and Pokémon. Ditto first awakens in a cave, and as the story unfolds, the creature looks out at a once-grassy environment of parched earth and litter. It meets the first Pokémon, the hydrophilic Squirtle that teaches Ditto how to spray water across the thirsty soil to bring it back to life; it is also the only other semblance of a survivor in this barren land.

From here the story gains momentum: As Ditto, your mission is to get the environment back to habitable levels and bring back Pokémon creatures that are presumably hiding. Gradually, the unfinished railroad tracks and cracked concrete steps of a staircase stop looking like signs of a catastrophe but rather fixable problems.

I wasn’t alone in trying to find meaning after a loss or trauma. Scholars have explored a variety of constructs around the meaning of life. Psychologists Login S. George and Crystal L. Park articulate a three-part framework in their 2010 article, grounded in comprehension, purpose, and mattering, as a basis for assessing our judgments and feelings as we find meaning after loss.

At first, meaning seemed absent. I sat glued to my Nintendo Switch for three hours on my first day of play. I came up for air only when my console told me that I was low on battery. Yet I did not feel depleted; I had finished restoring the first environment in the Pokopia world to verdant levels with lush berry trees and waterfalls.

As I built new habitats, I met new Pokémon. A Charmander emerged among tall grass. A Cubone spawned from a gravesite surrounded by wildflowers. Every new Pokémon that resurfaced charmed me with their excitement to be back home, scampering across the grass to greet other familiar faces. In my mind, I thanked these creatures for turning to me. Without Ditto’s help—my help—a world of Pokemon creatures ceases to exist, fated to disappear alongside humanity.

After a Pokémon spawns in its revived habitat, it awakens with some surprise. “Huh?” “What?” Some of them startle and put up their defenses in front of Ditto, who then coaxes them into the reality that they are back in a safe, familiar home. Often this realization gives them relief. “Wow, you did all of this? Thank you!”

Some of the Pokémon seem more wistful. They hold on to items or articles of clothing that their trainer used to wear, like the oversized Tangrowth (a large Pokémon with thick, shaggy vines that cover its entire body) that wears a pair of glasses much too small for him that gamers can infer are its trainer’s. Other creatures comment on Ditto’s human-like appearance (“You look like your human!”). These private conversations between Pokémon affirm that Ditto, who is also piecing together where its own trainer has gone, is doing something meaningful.

In 2023, game scholars Karam Eum and Young Yim Doh explored how bereaved adults shaped a meaning-making experience by playing video games with narratives that focus on loss and grief. Subjects spent a week playing two different video games and kept a diary where they shared specific game scenes that were particularly meaningful. While the games did evoke sad memories, participants also recalled positive memories in their pre- and post-loss lives, be it a funny conversation or a serendipitous moment shared with close ones who are still alive.

In the real world, the player behind the console, I, too, wonder where my beloved human has gone. I see Mary in the pink rose bouquets at grocery stores, and I smell her in the fragrant bone broth that wafts from the next-door apartment unit. In some of these most visceral disruptions, I feel my loss painfully.

But even in these heaviest of days, I surrender to the ordinary surprises that come up: a call from an old neighbor asking if I can help make meals for a family in need, or a stray cat mewing at my window to open the building’s front gate. I am startled out of a detached state, and I am moved to help, the way I got to help Mary.

This piece is part of a three-year GGSC project to explore love's role in promoting social well-being, bridging divides, and contributing to a good, meaningful life.

I associate Mary now with the colorful berry trees in Pokopia. When I come across one that needs tending, I remember how easy it is to water it with my Squirtle-like powers. When I clear a path from blocks of beach sand to make way for a new house for my aquatic Pokémon, I think of Mary’s selfless endeavors to nourish and comfort all of her friends and family.

Despite its sad undertones, Pokopia is full of positive quests, eager to help gamers imbue its universe with the belief that it is worth building a positive world in memory of those who shaped us. Even though our loved ones may “mysteriously” leave us behind, we recreate happiness, redefine ourselves, and choose who and what we live for, even if they do not come back.

It’s curious to me that bereavement can feel so isolating, although it is a human experience universally experienced. It helps to have a video game that reminds me of the actual truth. When I start to catch myself drifting into deeper ends of grief, I have something to divert my attention: I wonder how my Ditto is doing? I pick up my Switch 2 that sits at the foot of my bed, and I make a new home for my Pokémon the way Mary had done for me.

Lisa Kwon is an arts and culture writer based in Los Angeles, CA. She has contributed to The Guardian, Teen Vogue, Vulture, Eater, Prism Reports, Cultured Magazine, National Geographic, and many more.

#3989. Should We Look to Hunter-Gatherer Societies as Models of Equality?

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Become a subscribing member today. Help us continue to bring “the science of a meaningful life” to you and to millions around the globe.

Greater Good Science Center • Magazine • In Action • In Education

On the shores of Lake Eyasi in Northern Tanzania, a small number of people still hunt and gather their food. Today a Hadza hunter has taken down an animal—a kudu. He can’t carry it by himself, so he cuts his poisoned arrow from its flank, covers the carcass in dry grass to hide it from hyenas, and heads home to enlist help.

News spreads like wildfire across the camp. Everybody rushes to the kill. They divide up the carcass, carry it back, then divvy it up some more. Every family gets some, and after the meat is cooked, there’s more sharing still. On a good day like today, the hunter and his family retain half, but sometimes they keep much less. People may even walk from other camps, and almost everyone, from miles around, is entitled to a share.

The post-hunt scene above is idyllic. It paints a quintessential picture of egalitarianism and equality. But our experience as anthropologists working with the Hadza people of Tanzania and with the Tsimane people of Bolivia’s Amazon rainforest is that inequalities are part of even the most equal of societies. Furthermore, the equalities that do exist often emerge from selfish motives.

Insights like ours are revising researchers’ understanding of both the past and how people operate today, as inequality grows globally. Hunter-gatherers are often at the center of heated academic battles about what it means to be human, including debates over gender, war, and peace and whether humans are, by nature, egalitarian or hierarchical.

Sometimes these debates oversimplify. So we pulled together hundreds of different sources covering more than a century of ethnography to find out just how egalitarian hunter-gatherer and other subsistence societies really are.

Anthropologists have called hunter-gatherers such as the Hadza “egalitarian societies,” with equalities of power, wealth, and social status. And it’s true that the traditional subsistence communities we work with successfully limit many inequalities.

Most families have similar, modest possessions and store little material wealth. When leaders do emerge, they are highly constrained in how they can direct other people. People often make decisions via consensus, and, if they cannot reach agreement, families will move away.

While the same anthropologists also emphasize that some hunter-gatherers are very unequal and that equality takes deliberate effort, the prevailing impression is that equality characterizes hunter-gatherers, past and present.

A quick trip to your local bookshop will reveal grand claims, including that past hunter-gatherer societies had no permanent leadership and no hierarchy, inequality only spread with the development of agriculture, and humans are noble by nature.

Some scientists are pushing back against these utopian portrayals. The popular bestseller, The Dawn of Everything, calls egalitarianism a “Garden of Eden narrative” that lacks substance. Other scholars have called egalitarian societies a scientific origin myth.

So where does the truth lie? Probably somewhere in between.

In our recent wide-ranging review of the evidence from the early 20th century onward, we found that no society lacked inequality.

Like the Hadza, the Ju/’Hoansi of the Kalahari desert in southern Africa had little property and widespread food-sharing, yet certain families controlled valuable hunting territory surrounding waterholes. The Bayaka of the central African rainforest had little material inequality, yet they bought, sold, and hoarded ritual knowledge. The Philippine Agta controlled and arranged their children’s marriages, hugely discriminating against orphans.

Gender inequalities were commonplace. The Tanzanian Hadza keep certain cuts of meat exclusively for older men, and give more respect to better hunters. Across societies, women have universally done more direct parenting, and men have participated more frequently in community-level decision making.

No society lacks informal leadership. Age was another source of inequality. In many Australian societies, older men controlled ritual secrets and had power over the marriage choices of younger relatives.

Our study also found that although hunter-gatherers are frequently generous, the processes limiting inequality often do not require any generosity. Instead, equality often results from people’s self-interested motives to acquire resources, status, and autonomy.

A great example of this is food-sharing. Although each Hadza family gets a cut of kudu meat, it often happens not because the hunter’s family prioritizes total equality, but because other families request it vociferously. It can involve more taking than giving, often through strongly stated demands or complaints.

Another example is ridicule and mockery of stingy or braggadocious individuals, which keeps greed and bravado in check. Paradoxically, status-seeking can also promote equality, when cooperating to gain status diffuses status throughout the community.

Many of these inequality-reducing processes are familiar to people everywhere.

Ridicule and mockery are ubiquitous in industrialized societies, from political cartoons to social media. Lampooning authority is even common in autocratic societies where governments spend large sums of money suppressing speech. Demands and complaints form the basis of strikes and collective bargaining. And key to social mobility is cooperation among people of different social strata.

Like many great debates, the truth is complex: Our ancient hunter-gatherer ancestors didn’t reach the giddy heights of inequality seen in other societies over the last 6,000 years, but putting hunter-gatherers on a pedestal as paragons of equality is a mistake. Sweeping statements about equality in the past blind us to the tools that probably allowed ancient hunter-gatherers to combat inequality, just as they and people in all societies do today.

Egalitarianism, then, is best seen not as perfect equality but as a set of tools that people use to secure their own autonomy and access to resources. Equality along one dimension or another might be the outcome, but it doesn’t rely on people being amazingly noble or completely lacking in greed or status-seeking. If you consider egalitarianism as equality-creating processes, you can see that it is a widespread reality among hunter-gatherer societies, and beyond.

This article is republished from The Conversation under a Creative Commons license. Read the original article.

Duncan Stibbard Hawkes, Ph.D., is an assistant professor of anthropology at Baylor University.

Duncan Stibbard Hawkes, Ph.D., is an assistant professor of anthropology at Baylor University.

Christopher von Rueden, Ph.D., is a professor of leadership studies at the University of Richmond.

Christopher von Rueden, Ph.D., is a professor of leadership studies at the University of Richmond.

#1104. Weakened US fuel-efficiency rules and promises of cheaper cars could see drivers pay higher running costs while also increasing carbon emissions.

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Weakened US fuel-efficiency rules and promises of cheaper cars could yet see drivers pay higher running costs while increasing planet-heating emissions.

As part of its "Freedom Means More Affordable Cars" initiative, the US Department of Transportation this week rolled back fuel efficiency rules under the guise of lowering vehicle prices.

The Trump administration's transport agency says US taxpayers will save $138 billion (€122 billion) over the next five years — new vehicles will be $1,300 cheaper on average — since automakers will no longer have to invest in more fuel-efficient fleets.

But this does not account for soaring gas prices as Middle East conflicts choke global supply.

"Any savings on the front end get eaten up and spat out by excess costs of fuel… Fuel economy standards have saved drivers hundreds of billions of dollars," said Democratic Senator Sheldon Whitehouse from Rhode Island in a social media post.

Under the revised Corporate Average Fuel Economy (CAFE) rules, vehicle fleets only require an average fuel efficiency of about 35 miles per gallon (14.7 kilometers per liter) by 2031, down around one-third from 50 miles per gallon. Implemented by then-President Joe Biden in 2024, the updated efficiency rules aimed to incentivize the production of low emission electric vehicles and hybrids. The US National Highway Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA) estimates the standards would have saved 70 billion gallons (265 billion litres) of fuel over 25 years.

Meanwhile, nonprofit advocacy group the Union of Concerned Scientists (UCS) says an earlier version of CAFE regulations has saved US consumers about $321 billion at the gas pump since 2010.

As the world's biggest petrol consumer, the US burns through 20 million barrels of oil per day, which is 20% of global consumption, notes Dan Becker, director of the Safe Climate Transport Campaign at the US-based nonprofit environmental group Center for Biological Diversity.

"Transportation is the biggest consumer of that oil, making strong auto standards the most effective way to cut pollution and oil use," Becker said in a statement.

The rules that have been diluted this week would have prevented more than 710 million metric tons of carbon dioxide emissions by 2050 — the equivalent of running 165 million gas-powered passenger vehicles for a year.

"With transportation representing nearly 28% of US greenhouse emissions, rolling back fuel economy standards is a dangerous setback for near-term climate goals," said Ben Scott, head of Energy Demand at UK-based climate think tank Carbon Tracker. "It risks keeping inefficient combustion engines on the road much longer than climate science demands," he told DW.

But following President Donald Trump's elimination of greenhouse gas emissions standards for light- and heavy-duty vehicles last year, Dave Cooke, senior vehicles analyst for the UCS Clean Transportation Program, said the rollbacks merely compound "a much more significant step backwards."

He said the trend sends "a specific signal to automakers," adding that "the United States is seeking to maximize oil usage to increase oil company profits."

This will be to the detriment of climate targets, with high-polluting oil contributing one-third of global fossil fuel emissions that continue to rise — along with temperatures.

The world just had the hottest August on record as severe heatwaves in Europe caused more than 16,000 excess deaths in Germany alone.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Sales of low emission EVs are down in the US in 2026, especially after Donald Trump scrapped the $7,500 tax credit for new electric vehicles.

American automakers also continue to push back against regulations that would incentivize a switch to EVs, arguing there is a lack of consumer demand.

"It's highly unlikely that the United States will suddenly become awash in electric options when neither the auto industry nor regulators seem interested in providing such choices for consumers," said Cooke.

But Ben Scott believes that while softening US fuel economy standards "will create temporary headwinds", it won't stall an inevitable transition to electric vehicles.

"With EVs delivering vastly lower running costs, electrification becomes a clear-cut economic choice," he said of cars running on "cheaper, domestically secure energy that will drive the transition forward, regardless of short-term politics."

As less efficient cars and trucks consume more oil that's more expensive and in shorter supply, "the fragility of oil-dependent transport" is set to be exposed, he said. Edited by: Tamsin Walker

#928. Nobel Prize in chemistry goes to Henri B. Kagan and Kenso Soai

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Henri B. Kagan and Kenso Soai won the Nobel Prize in chemistry for their groundbreaking discoveries related to the origin of chirality and life's building blocks.

The Nobel Prize in chemistry on Wednesday was awarded to Henri B. Kagan and Kenso Soai "for the discovery of non-linear effects and autocatalysis in asymmetric organic synthesis."

Kagan, who is 95 years old and was born in France, is affiliated with France’s Université Paris-Sud, and Soai, who is 76 years old and was born in Japan, is based at the Tokyo University of Science.

The scientists share the Nobel Prize for answering longstanding questions in chemistry about life's origins.

Specifically, the duo solved an age-old mystery that has long puzzled chemists: some molecules, like amino acids, exist as two variants that are mirror images of one another, but just one of the variants out of the two is found in living organisms.

The two answered questions about why life chooses one variant over another.

The Royal Swedish Academy of Sciences announced the winner in Stockholm.

In chemistry, life's building blocks are what scientists call homochiral, after the Greek words for "same" and "hand."

The Nobel Committee said the chemistry prize recognizes discoveries that have enabled chemists to drive chemical reactions that lead to homochirality.

"Henri Kagan and Kenso Soai have provided a solution to a chemical mystery that is over a century old: how homochirality can emerge spontaneously," Heiner Linke, chair of the Nobel Committee for Chemistry, said in a news release. "The chemical reactions they have developed are spectacular."

The duo's discoveries, the news release said, "have been decisive for chemists who design reactions for the manufacture of pharmaceuticals."

#509. An ancient ice shelf’s collapse may hint at what’s coming for Antarctica’s glaciers

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

During the last ice age, a floating ice shelf filled Baffin Bay and helped hold three ice sheets in place. Its collapse may hold a lesson for Antarctica.

An ice shelf floats on the ocean, so it’s easy to treat it as the least important part of a frozen coastline. In practice, it works like a brake on the ice sheets behind it.

During the last ice age, one ice shelf covered all of Baffin Bay, between Greenland and Canada, and helped hold three of them in place.

When that shelf broke up, the ice behind it probably began retreating fast. The researchers see the same setup in Antarctica, where floating shelves still hold back glaciers.

Shannon Klotsko of the University of North Carolina Wilmington’s Department of Earth and Ocean Sciences led the study. The ice melted thousands of years ago, so the team estimated its size from marks it left on the seafloor.

Robert Hatfield, an associate professor of geology at the University of Florida, co-authored the study. He compared the ice to a church.

“These ice sheets are like a gothic cathedral,” Hatfield said. “You have this big, tall cathedral of ice and these flying buttresses of floating ice shelves that support them.”

The team wasn’t looking for an ice shelf. Its members spent five weeks in Baffin Bay aboard the research ship Neil Armstrong, and they wanted sediment cores.

These long tubes of mud pulled from the seafloor help to better understand what made the Greenland Ice Sheet unstable as the last ice age ended.

Sonar mapped the seafloor along the way. The team added older maps from other ships and studied the steep slope that drops into the deep bay.

Around most of the bay, they traced a boundary on that slope. It marks the deepest point where ice touched the seafloor.

On one stretch off Greenland, bare rock lies above the boundary and mud covers the slope below it. Off western Greenland, winding ridges and small pits appear above the line. Scientists read similar features off Norway as marks of a retreating ice edge and of icebergs breaking away.

“The thing that really surprised us was this evidence was over a mile deep into the ocean,” Hatfield said. “That’s at least half a mile thicker than any previously known ice shelf in the Northern Hemisphere.”

The bay isn’t as deep now as it was 19,000 years ago. The ice sheets were heavy enough to press the land down, and sea level was different.

Jacqueline Austermann and Lauren Lewright of Columbia University’s Lamont-Doherty Earth Observatory ran a computer model to show how the ground and ocean responded to that weight. With their correction, the team estimates the shelf was about 4,600 to 6,900 feet (1,400 to 2,100 meters) thick.

The team puts its area at about 58,000 square miles (150,000 square kilometers). That’s roughly the size of Illinois.

Earlier researchers had found signs of something smaller. A 2022 study by a separate team found evidence of an ice shelf about 1,600 feet (500 meters) thick in the bay’s northern part.

The shelf touched three ice sheets. The Greenland Ice Sheet lay to the east, the Innuitian Ice Sheet to the north, and the Laurentide Ice Sheet to the west.

The team concluded that the shelf did more than slow them down. It connected the three ice sheets and helped them grow thicker.

Sediment cores from across the bay suggest the shelf disappeared over about 500 to 1,000 years. The researchers infer that its collapse triggered a rapid retreat of the surrounding ice sheets.

“That’s one of the scary takeaways we didn’t expect, that an ice shelf the scale of the ones in Antarctica could completely disappear in a matter of centuries,” Hatfield said.

Antarctica has already shown a smaller version of this. After its Larsen B ice shelf broke apart, a 2004 study found that the glaciers flowing into it sped up and thinned.

Most of the ice that Greenland and Antarctica have lost since 1979 left through faster-flowing glaciers, not surface melt. Scientists are watching the shelf in front of Antarctica’s Thwaites Glacier for that reason.

In Greenland, a channel of seawater carved into the Petermann Ice Shelf may have weakened it before a large piece broke away.

“One of the biggest concerns in Antarctica right now is how fast will that ice that’s on land flow into the ocean when we lose that buttressing effect,” said Hatfield.

Scientists who build computer models of ice sheets have a true historical case to test against, Hatfield said. That could lead to better predictions of how quickly Antarctic ice loss will raise sea levels.

The thickness range is wide. That range depends on how soft the rock deep inside Earth is and how the ice grew, so the team ran several versions of the model. Some seafloor maps weren’t sharp enough to show whether the boundary existed at all.

Timing comes next. Researchers still need to work out how quickly each of the three ice sheets pulled back once the shelf was gone.

The full study was published in the journal Nature Geoscience.

Photograph the year’s highest tides through the King Tides Project. Volunteers in this international citizen science effort document how far those tides reach. The project offers a preview of everyday high water as sea levels rise.

Support the Antarctic and Southern Ocean Coalition to help shape how Antarctica is protected.  The coalition is the only environmental group invited to observe Antarctic Treaty meetings, and it tracks climate change, fishing, and tourism in the region.

Follow the National Snow and Ice Data Center‘s Ice Sheets Today updates to watch melting on Greenland and Antarctica as it happens. The center posts near-real-time maps and graphs during each melt season.

#518. Two new siphonophores, one fiery red, emerged from 20 years of robot dives, and a biologist named the other after his mother

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Two new siphonophores, Bargmannia marlena and B. pyrosa, were named from MBARI robot dives, bringing the genus to eight species.

A dive by the Monterey Bay Aquarium Research Institute (MBARI) off central California, researchers found two new species of siphonophores, which are deep-sea jellyfish relatives.

The first came from a depth of 6,440 feet (1,963 meters). It had long, bright orange feeding zooids – the units that make up a colony – and 11 swimming bells, and it now sits at the Yale Peabody Museum as YPM IZ 112077.

Maciej K. Mańko, of the University of Gdańsk in Poland, named it Bargmannia marlena after his mother, Marlena.

Mańko and colleagues from MBARI, New York University and Yale University described it this month, with a second new siphonophore.

The team based each species on two preserved colonies, a holotype and a paratype, a second reference specimen. All four came from waters off California.

Related to jellies and corals, siphonophores are gelatinous animals that MBARI counts among the main predators of the midwater, the open water between the surface and the seafloor.

Cat Munro studied their evolution as a graduate student at Brown University and wrote about them in a 2015 MBARI expedition log.

“Siphonophores operate as an integrated unit, and are the most complex of all colonial animals,” she wrote. In her description, each zooid works like an organ that swims, eats or breeds for the colony.

The Portuguese man o’ war, split into four species in 2025, has the same kind of colony but floats at the surface. Bargmannia lives deep, and it swims by firing dozens of bells at once.

In 1954 British zoologist A. K. Totton described the genus and its first species, B. elongata, from swimming bells caught in nets. Gillian Mapstone of London’s Natural History Museum added B. lata in 1998. A year later Philip Pugh added B. amoena and B. gigas.

In a 2019 Zootaxa paper, Pugh named B. profunda and B. stenotes, each from one incomplete specimen. That made six species. The new paper went online on October 16 and makes it eight.

MBARI’s October 22 announcement singled out the orange stomachs of B. marlena, which can stretch. On the holotype these gastrozooids reach about 1.5 inches (3.8 centimeters), and the whole colony measured 4.4 inches (11.2 centimeters).

The paratype came up 18 years earlier, on October 9, 2004, from 4,045 feet (1,233 meters) off California. Yale keeps it too.

Of the known species, B. lata looks most like it, but its bells and bracts (shield-like plates along the stem) differ in shape. A B. marlena colony also carries male and female parts on one stem.

The name Bargmannia pyrosa joins the Greek pyr, fire, with the Latin suffix -osa, full of, so it means fire-colored. MBARI says it has “a fiery red body.”

Most of its zooids are red in life and turn pale orange in preservative. Each colony is either male or female.

Its holotype came from 3,268 feet (996 meters) off California on March 23, 2002. It had 14 swimming bells, and the biggest stood 24.2 millimeters (just under an inch) tall. The paratype came up on May 18, 2014, from 7,451 feet (2,271 meters).

Mańko’s team then reread Pugh’s 1999 account of B. amoena and found it “was a compound one, combining B. amoena and B. pyrosa.”

The bells of related species grow along paths that partly converge with those of B. pyrosa. The authors think that helped the red species slip past earlier workers.

MBARI photographed a live B. pyrosa in 2015 in the Gulf of California at 5,564 feet (1,696 meters). The same year its cameras caught B. marlena in the gulf, at 3,652 feet (1,113 meters).

The team went through 155 specimens kept in formalin, most of them at the Yale Peabody Museum. The Smithsonian loaned its B. profunda and B. stenotes type specimens. For the B. amoena types, the team went to the Natural History Museum in London.

They measured the bells and the stinging cells, and the Yale Center for Genome Analysis sequenced the DNA. From the reads, the team put together mitochondrial genomes.

One genome came from a single bell of a B. elongata colony from 2004 that had sat in formalin since. The authors got the whole mitochondrial genome out of it with a method they describe as new.

Their tree puts B. lata and B. marlena on one branch. B. stenotes, B. profunda, B. pyrosa, B. amoena and B. elongata share the other.

On the lata and marlena side the mitochondrial genes changed order. The authors suggest that those colonies also went from separate sexes to both sexes. Their data cannot establish which change came first, they wrote.

The paper thanks the pilots of Ventana, Tiburon and Doc Ricketts, the MBARI robots that filmed the live colonies, and the crews of the R/V Rachel Carson and the R/V Western Flyer.

MBARI found seven Bargmannia species in its video archive, and the eighth only in museum jars. In its photos, the shallowest is B. amoena at 1,329 feet (405 meters) off central California, and the deepest is B. profunda at 10,712 feet (3,265 meters).

Seven of the eight species turned up off California, where the authors found their ranges structured along depth.

MBARI footage of deep-sea Bargmannia siphonophores, including the two newly named species B. marlena and B. pyrosa. Video: MBARI (Monterey Bay Aquarium Research Institute)

Steven Haddock, a senior scientist at MBARI, runs its Biodiversity and Biooptics Team. In March 2015 he led a Gulf of California leg on the Western Flyer. The ship left on Saturday, March 7, for dives with the ROV Doc Ricketts.

Munro, then in her second year in Casey Dunn’s lab, was on her third cruise aboard the ship. “Many siphonophore species are fragile and fall apart easily when they are collected by trawl nets. The best way to sample siphonophores is by blue-water diving or with an ROV,” she wrote.

A caption in the second expedition log says Munro “very carefully removes a delicate orange physonect siphonophore,” a B. amoena, from a detritus sampler.

Doc Ricketts also spotted the bumpy snailfish, one of three new snailfish species from the deep Pacific. MBARI says its research and technology have helped find more than 300 new species.

Public database records give some of these species very broad ranges. Specimens assigned to B. elongata appear there from near the surface down to 18,766 feet (5,720 meters). They also span tropical to polar seas.

The authors think the broad ranges of B. elongata and B. lata probably hide lineages nobody has told apart yet. They had few data for B. stenotes. The larger stinging cells of B. lata and B. marlena they read only as a possible shift toward bigger prey.

Siphonophores show up as well in deep-sea DNA surveys in the Indian Ocean. The David and Lucile Packard Foundation and the US National Science Foundation paid for the work. Poland’s National Agency for Academic Exchange and the University of Gdańsk also contributed.

At Yale, the colony cataloged as YPM IZ 112077 now carries the name of Mańko’s mother.

The full study was published in the journal Zoological Journal of the Linnean Society.

#526. Crows make decisions twice as fast as humans and scientists say it is not just an automatic reflex

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Carrion crows can make deliberate visual decisions twice as fast as humans or monkeys. This incredible speed is tracked in their brains and likely evolved to help them fly safely.

Calling someone a bird brain is meant to be an insult, but is it really? A carrion crow can tell which way a cloud of dots is moving in about a quarter of a second, roughly half the time a monkey or a human needs.

And it has none of the layered cortex mammals use for that same job, which suggests there’s more than one way to build a brain that makes quick and correct decisions.

Philipp Schmidbauer and Andreas Nieder of the University of Tübingen trained two carrion crows on that test and recorded from the birds’ brains during it.

The crows lost nothing in accuracy for their speed, and the neurons they recorded escalated towards making a choice the way primate neurons do, only faster.

In an interview with Earth.com, Nieder said the result “tells us that bird brains – crow brains, at least – are built for life in the fast lane.” A mammal’s cortex, he added, isn’t required for fast, accurate decisions.

The crows watched a patch of moving dots on a screen. Most of the dots jumped around at random, but some of them moved together: left or right, or up or down.

On different trials, 3%, 9%, 27%, or 81% of the dots moved together. On some trials, none moved, so the crow had to guess.

A trial started when a crow held its head inside an infrared beam in front of the screen, and two targets showed up on either side.

When the dots came on in the middle, the crow gave the researchers an answer by pulling its head out of the beam and pecking the matching target. Schmidbauer and Nieder timed each decision from the moment the dots appeared to the moment the head began to move.

Monkeys and people have done the same task for more than 20 years. In those experiments, neurons in part of the primate cortex add up evidence over a few hundred milliseconds.

The animal commits to a choice once that activity reaches a threshold. The open question was whether a brain with no such cortex does anything similar.

One crow did 55 sessions and the other 102, close to 280 trials in each session. Both responded in about 235 milliseconds on average.

The crows barely slowed down when fewer dots moved together. With none of the dots moving together, the first crow took 254 milliseconds, and with 81% moving together it took 220.

They were right more often when more dots moved together: more than 99% of the time at 81%, and about three quarters of the time at 9%.

The crows also took longer on the trials they got wrong, which is what a deliberate decision looks like.

Monkeys given the same self-paced test took roughly 410 to 600 milliseconds across three earlier studies, and humans took roughly 450 to 820 milliseconds.

Those primates answered by moving their eyes. The crows answered by moving their whole heads, which take more effort to get going, and still finished in about half the time.

The speed, Nieder told Earth.com, was “much faster than we expected.”

The crows stayed just as fast when the researchers changed the number of dots and the way the motion was generated. So the speed factor comes from somewhere in the birds’ brains, the researchers wrote, not in the test.

While the crows worked, the researchers recorded from 248 neurons in a high-level region of the crow’s forebrain.

Birds use that region for flexible decisions, much as primates use the prefrontal cortex. Fifty-eight of those neurons, about a quarter, fired harder for one direction of motion than for its opposite.

Nieder said the speed was only part of the surprise. “But what fascinated me most was the neural activity,” he said.

The direction-sensitive neurons started firing faster for the direction the crow was about to choose, about 67 milliseconds after the dots appeared.

That was roughly 170 milliseconds before the crow moved its head. From there the firing climbed steadily until the head moved, and the clearer the motion, the steeper the climb.

When a crow was slower to answer, the climb was slower too. In the last 50 milliseconds before any head movement, though, the firing reached about the same level on fast trials and slow ones.

That climb to a shared level is the pattern primate researchers have described for more than 20 years: evidence builds until it reaches a threshold, and then the animal acts.

Nieder said that despite “the very different organization of the crow brain, this pattern looked remarkably similar to what has been observed in primates.”

On the trials where no dots moved together, the crows had nothing to judge and could only guess. Even then, the same neurons began firing harder for whichever target the crow was about to peck, about 96 milliseconds after the dots appeared.

The same thing happened when a crow got a trial wrong: the neurons followed the choice, not the dots.

The researchers then trained a computer program on the firing patterns from correct trials. From the firing alone, it picked the target a crow was about to choose 87% of the time on correct trials, 73% on the guesses, and 68% on the mistakes.

Runs on shuffled data, used as a control, scored above 65% about once in every 20 runs.

Asked by Earth.com for a finding worth highlighting, Nieder picked those guessing trials. The result, he said, “suggests that decisions are influenced not only by sensory evidence but potentially also by internal biases and fluctuations in the decision process.”

Birds and mammals last shared an ancestor more than 360 million years ago. That’s long before the dinosaurs died out, Nieder said.

Since then the two groups have evolved different brains: mammals grew a cerebral cortex in layers, the neocortex, and birds grew clusters of neurons instead.

Yet the corvid family, crows and their relatives, has repeatedly shown sophisticated thinking: ravens can plan ahead, and crows can count their own calls.

Nieder said the speed may come from efficient connections between neurons and fast visual processing. Brain areas may also communicate quickly, he said, and a crow’s brain is compact, so signals have less distance to travel.

Bird eyes may also help explain the speed: some songbirds can see a light flickering 145 times a second rather than seeing a steady glow.

A key visual center in a bird’s brain responds within about 5 milliseconds of something appearing in view. The pressure to be that quick, Schmidbauer and Nieder wrote, may come from flight, from spotting predators, and from fast beak movements.

“Studying crows alongside primates allows us to identify principles of decision-making that may be shared across animals, rather than assuming that intelligent behavior depends on having a mammalian neocortex,” Nieder said.

Two crows took the test, and the recordings came from one brain region. The firing on fast and slow trials came close to one level just before the head moved but didn’t match exactly. So something besides the evidence may also be affecting the firing.

Nieder listed three things to test next. He wants to give crows four choices instead of left and right and see whether the same mechanism holds.

He also wants to know whether crows have something like confidence: can a crow tell a strong-evidence decision from a weak one, and how does its brain represent the difference?

The biggest question, he said, is where the speed comes from. The team has recorded from one high-level region, and what the rest of the brain does in that quarter of a second hasn’t been measured.

Nieder wants to follow the information through the whole brain, from the eye to the head movement that gives the answer.

He also wants to learn whether the speed holds for decisions that aren’t about visual motion. Until then, one region of the crow’s brain has been timed, and the rest has not.

The full study was published in the journal Science.

#535. Stranded by a storm in 1873, an Argentine captain built a boat on the beach, and archaeologists may have found his camp

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

In 1873 Luis Piedra Buena lost his ship on Isla de los Estados and built a 33-foot cutter to escape. A camp found 490 feet from old hulls may be his.

On March 10, 1873, the Argentine captain Luis Piedra Buena ran his schooner onto a beach on Isla de los Estados, the island off Tierra del Fuego that English charts call Staten Island.

One anchor was dragging and the other had lost its stock, so he decided to put her on the sand. She went in sideways, and the surf knocked the oakum out of her seams until she sank in the shallows. He and seven men had come to boil penguins for oil.

In January 2026, archaeologists from Argentina and Spain recorded wooden hulls and a camp in a cove there. Their paper in Antiquity says the camp is possibly linked to his crew, but the ship has not been identified.

Born in 1833, Piedra Buena rose to lieutenant colonel in the Argentine Navy, according to CONICET, Argentina’s national research council. The island had been granted to him by the early 1870s. He lived in Punta Arenas, Chile, where he kept a store of nautical goods.

His ship was the Espora, ex-Nancy, a 157-ton brig with one deck built in Maryland. His Spanish diary of the trip survives in a copy by Cándido Eyroa, a family friend and his first biographer.

The Maritime Museum of Ushuaia has put the diary online with notes by C.P. Vairo, in Spanish and in an English version quoted here. The penguins were rockhoppers, the museum says, and the men cooked them in a can for the oil.

They left on February 12 and stopped at Chabunco to collect his tacho, the rendering pot. At the First Narrows a man named José brought a bag of beans, two of biscuit, one of coffee and a letter from his mother, which he answered after work.

On February 22 he wrote, “we found what I expected, the birds on land. With those millions of animals a great deal could be done.” The crew killed 300 the next day and had 200 gallons of oil by February 27.

On March 5 a wave filled the ship’s boat while three men were landing firewood. The boat drifted off and turned up later, broken, in the surf.

After the wreck he tried to caulk the hull with everyone at the pumps, and had to give up. “People give up on the ship, but I don’t. Anyway, we are cutting curves for the boat that we plan to make,” he wrote on March 14. The curves were bent timbers for its frames.

Two men were sick that day, and Don Celestino, left to sharpen axes and cut curves, did neither, so five of the eight were working.

On March 15 he built a hut because the mattresses and clothes were all wet. His aneroid barometer read 73 the next day, the lowest he had seen. That afternoon they laid a keel of 33 feet (10 meters) for a boat with a beam of 13 feet (4 meters).

The planks came out crooked. In his words, “the saw does not help us, because there is no one who knows how to saw with it.”

On March 31 he had 6 barrels of meat, 3 bags of biscuit, about 60 pounds of sugar, 50 of rice and 40 of coffee. By April 5 it was one biscuit per man at each meal. On April 16 a gale and the biggest tide he had seen carried the planks off the beach.

He wrote that the cutter, the Luisito, was finished on April 30. On May 4 Celestino, unhappy with the work he was given, threatened to burn it.

It took until the afternoon tide of May 11 to float her. At noon on May 18 they left what he called their poor Espora, all in pieces. They were in Punta Arenas on May 27.

Vairo went looking for the wreck in April 1997 on Jorge Trabuchi’s ketch Callas, with the diary as a guide. His notes say Franklin Bay was the only bay they found with a sandy beach open to the southwest.

The museum’s page on the island says they later found a hull, a chain and wood matching timber used then in the northern hemisphere.

The latest trip ran from January 15 to 31, 2026, with the expedition Aquí hay dragones (Here be dragons), led by the Falklands War veteran Roberto Ulloa.

The team sailed on the Pampa Mía and the Galileo, helped by the Argentine Navy and the crew of the ARA Indómita, and ran out of water, CONICET said.

Near the entrance of Lacroix Inlet, a shallow cove inside Franklin Bay, in the tidal zone, lie the lower hull of a ship, labeled BF010, and an iron chain, BF009.

Hull BF005 sits just above the high-tide line and still reaches deck height, and a small piece of a third, BF004, lies on the north side.

After recording the remains, the team dug test pits and sampled timbers. The first author is Nicolás Ciarlo of the University of Cádiz and CONICET’s Institute of Archaeology.

Site BF008, the camp, is at the foot of a steep hill beside a small stream. It sits about 490 feet (150 meters) from both BF005 and BF010.

On the surface lay glass beads, four-hole ceramic buttons of the Prosser type, corrugated galvanized sheets and a bottle. There was also a deck prism, a glass block that let light below deck, and a small rudder hinge.

Carlos Landa, a CONICET researcher on the trip, called the spot a possible castaways’ camp in the council’s Spanish-language release of April 1.

The authors say the camp’s place and objects point to the nearby wrecks, and so possibly to the Espora. Old accounts disagree on how far she was dismantled. A hull still standing to deck height, like BF005, does not fit the idea that she was scrapped, they wrote.

Stones from the 1629 wreck of the Dutch ship Batavia have filled gaps in incomplete records. Shipyard drawings and diaries were also used to revisit why Shackleton’s Endurance sank.

The paper does not say which ship the hulls belong to, and no timber results have been published.

By a stream about 100 feet (30 meters) from the Espora remains, the museum’s English page on the island describes a ruin with a saw arch, cables, blocks and bottles.

“Obviously it was the refuge and warehouse that Don Luis built next to the improvised shipyard,” it says. The 2026 paper has not confirmed this and reports no traces of a shipyard so far.

A watercolor by E. Biggeri from about 1965, at Argentina’s National Naval Museum, shows the Luisito on the stocks beside the wreck.

Vairo notes that Piedra Buena’s workers had used a large king penguin colony at Puerto Roca years earlier. Far to the east, on Zavodovski Island, chinstrap penguin nests fell 63% between 2011 and 2025.

Piedra Buena died in 1883. On the last day of the 1873 diary he reached Punta Arenas and “found everything that was most precious, which was my family and my little children, good guys, instantly forgetting everything that had happened.”

The full study was published in the journal Antiquity.
