#2296. Renovation Diaries: The House Envelope and Other Lessons in Efficiency

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

All Gardenista stories—from garden tours and expert advice to hand tools and furniture roundups.

Starting from scratch or upgrading an outdoor space? Look here for advice on plants and hardscape materials. We have 200 guides on everything from fences to foxgloves.

Heading somewhere? Plan your trip with our destination guides to our favorite public gardens, hotels, restaurants, and shops.

Gardenista’s members-only directory of landscape architects and garden designers.

See our obsessively curated catalog of favorite products sourced by the Gardenista editors.

Editor’s note: This is the latest in a series of posts by Sally Kohn— journalist and CNN political commentator, TED talk giver, and design aficionado—chronicling her adventures in remodeling.

Let’s level set. I live in a 1780s barn that was turned into a house in the 1950s, and as far as I can tell the windows and the siding haven’t been changed since. The fact is, many parts of our money pit farmhouse need updating. Some, like the 1950s asphalt roof built directly on top of a much older shake shingle roof, we replaced last year. Other things we’re still waiting on, like the vintage 1950s bathroom tile with vintage crumbling grout. But since we can’t close many of our windows without pushing on them from the outside, and we have an inexplicable leak behind our very ancient siding, we put off the inevitable long enough. It is time for some grand-scale renovations.

Stay tuned for some of the other things we’ll be changing, including a kitchen refresh. But first up, if we’re going to go to all the trouble—and, ahem, expense—of replacing all of our siding and windows, can we make our old home more environmentally responsible in terms of both materials and energy efficiency? Let’s find out.

My guide on this journey is Shannon Pendleton, principal of Sanderson Sustainable Design and co-host of Passive House Accelerator. In addition to being a friend and neighbor, Shannon is a leading expert on how to make homes more sustainable and energy efficient. And while those concepts might immediately make you think of solar panels and the like, Shannon explains that in energy efficiency, an ounce of prevention is worth a pound of cure. In other words, using green energy to power your home is great. But designing your home to need less energy in the first place is even better.

But with apologies to Shannon, when she explained the science of the “building envelope,” all I could think about is that envelope feels like the wrong word. Maybe it should be “building envelop”—as in, how enveloped is your building. Because your “building envelop” is what stops the outside environment from getting in and the inside environment from getting out. That means everything from bugs and dust to wind to heat and cold.

So I’m making two big changes to my “building envelop,” both of which create an opportunity for more energy efficiency. Let’s start with the siding. We have very old, very stained, very falling-apart cedar. I could just replace it with cedar, maybe even sustainably harvested cedar. But the fact is, our old cedar is cupping and splitting. Yes, more maintenance would help, but that’s hard to do with second-story wood. Also I’m lazy. Alternatively, I could have gone with some plastic product or chemically treated wood but, like I said, we care about the environment and also want to preserve the look and feel of our historic home. So we’re going with Thermory siding.

Thermory takes sustainably forested wood—in our case, we’re using pine—and puts it through an intense heat-treating process that makes the wood more stable and rot-resistant. It’s an updated twist on the Japanese shou sugi ban process, where wood is charred to make it water and rot resistant. Thermory is that, without the blackening (though they actually do have a product that mirrors the shou sugi ban look, but with the added benefits of Thermory’s advanced thermal modification). In other words, Thermory gives us everything we like about natural wood plus all these added benefits.

Using a more stable rot-resistant siding definitely envelops our house better (take that, flying squirrels), but the energy efficiency upgrades come from what will be behind the siding. Our contractor—the wonderful, lovable, fantastic-to-work-with John Gemmi and his company, Gemmi Construction—were already planning to weather-seal behind the siding, and of course put new insulation behind that where we find any lacking. But Shannon suggested installing “a rain screen, a continuous air barrier, and continuous exterior insulation” to give us the most enveloped envelope possible. Ultimately, we settled on Zip R sheathing, which does all three of those things in one system. So that’s our plan.

As for the windows, well…. The most energy-efficient option is triple-pane glass, but in our case, since we’re replacing a gajillion windows, we just couldn’t make it work in our budget. We have two walls of windows in our great room, most of which are currently fixed panes that we’re excited to replace with operable awning windows. We looked into doing triple-pane for just those windows, but that would have made the windows too heavy to open. So yes, we’re choosing a little less efficiency there but counting on the fact that having new windows that don’t require a screwdriver and grunts to close will nevertheless up our efficiency. Plus, thanks to Ambiance Design, we’re adding retractable Duette Honeycomb shades over those large window walls, which will give us some extra energy efficiency on super hot days.

Niece Lumber helped us pick out Lincoln Windows, a family run U.S.-manufactured brand with two advantages. First, we could get retractable screens which we’re very excited about. And second, they offer custom colors on their exteriors. You’ll have to stay tuned to figure out what color choice we’ve made. It’s gonna be…dramatic.

Work on the siding and window part of the renovation is starting soon. I’ll be posting updates on my Instagram (@sallykohn), plus more updates along the way here as we get into our kitchen renovations, including the most sustainable and cool countertop and our very locally made and custom color stove. Watch this space!

N.B.: This post was first published on Redemolista on Oct. 22, 2026.

#658. NASA Systems Engineering Team Details HWO Mission Architecture

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Finding an Earth-like world beyond the solar system is the Holy Grail of exoplanet research, and the field has experienced incredible technological advancements in searching for and identifying exoplanets since the first exoplanet was discovered in 1995. This includes both ground- and space-based telescopes, specifically the powerful James Webb Space Telescope. However, directly imaging exoplanets remains elusive, primarily due to the enormous technological challenge of imaging a point of light so far away.

But a proposed mission called the Habitable Worlds Observatory (HWO) could advance progress towards not only observing but directly imaging Earth-like exoplanets. While HWO has been in the works for about 10 years now, itâs still not slated to launch until sometime in the 2040s. In the meantime, researchers are developing new engineering concepts for this highly anticipated telescope. These concepts were discussed in a June 2026 study published in the Journal of Astronomical Telescopes, Instruments, and Systems and hold the potential to continue HWOâs development well in advance of its launch.

For the study, the researchers introduced engineering concepts called Exploratory Analytic Cases (EACs) designed to balance the science goal demands with the engineering needs. The purpose of this study was to discuss the first three of five EACs regarding HWOâs architecture design focusing on telescope type, size, and whether they need to have an on- or off-axis design.

The telescope size includes the mirror diameter in meters, and the type of telescope includes the mirror and aperture design, with the study focusing on inscribed, circumscribed, round, and outer diameter. For example, inscribed includes the inner baseline measurement while circumscribed includes the measured outer region that holds a set of mirrors. A round mirror is how it sounds, while a outer diameter mirror denotes the maximum edge-to-edge measurement. The difference between on-axis and off-axis design is the former has all of the components within the field of view of incoming light, obstructing a portion of the view, while the latter has all of its components outside the field of view, resulting in an unobstructed view.

For the EACs, the researchers designed EAC1 as having a 6-m inscribed / 7.2-m circumscribed, off-axis design; EAC2 with a 6-m round, off-axis design; and EAC3 having an 8-m outer diameter, on-axis design. In the end, the researchers favored the off-axis design to allow greater light-gathering operations, along with focusing on a larger aperture size to enable greater light-collecting area, too. Another design the researchers recommended was the temperature properties, which influence light-collecting and overall telescope operations. This is especially prevalent with the coronagraph instrument (CI), which blocks out the starâs glare, enabling the direct imaging of orbiting exoplanets.

The study notes, âThe SET [Systems Engineering Team], comprised of team members across NASA centers and JPL, successfully completed design architectures for EACs 1 to 3 and developed end-to-end modeling for EAC1. This team was established to support design explorations and formulating technology requirements. Using model-driven practices based on JWST heritage and enhanced by Roman experience, the team developed several tools to perform parametric design studies and trades. In parallel, the models were shared with industry partners to support their technology maturation efforts and independent architecture studies.â

As noted, HWO isnât slated to launch until the 2040s, but its science goals remain unchanged as it is also slated to replace the James Webb Space Telescope as the preliminary space telescope. The primary goal of HWO will be to directly image Earth-like exoplanets, and the designs and objectives outlined today will undoubtedly influence these science goals and the telescope operations in determining whether Earth is the only habitable planet and whether weâre alone in the universe.

What new concepts will scientists conjure up before HWOâs launch in the 2040s? Only time will tell, and this is why we science!

As always, keep doing science & keep looking up!

#450. The asteroid that killed the dinosaurs may have helped ants conquer the world

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Around 66 million years ago, an asteroid struck Earth and brought the age of the dinosaurs to an end. The mass extinction that followed created opportunities for countless other animals to evolve and spread. Ants were among the groups that eventually became remarkably successful, and scientists have now identified a surprising genetic factor that may have helped fuel their rise.

Today, ants are the largest family of social insects, with more than 15,000 species found around the world. New research published in Science Advances suggests that their extraordinary evolutionary success was partly driven by mobile segments of DNA known as transposable elements, or "jumping genes."

The international study was led by Dr. Lukas Schrader of the Institute for Evolution and Biodiversity (IEB) at the University of Münster (Germany).

Jumping genes are pieces of DNA that can move to different locations within a genome and produce additional copies of themselves. Scientists once viewed them primarily as "genomic parasites" because, much like viruses, they can multiply without providing any clear benefit to their host. In some cases, their activity can even contribute to disease.

More recently, however, researchers have begun recognizing that these mobile genetic elements can also create opportunities for evolutionary change. The new findings suggest they played an important role in the remarkable diversification of ants.

When the scientists compared different groups of ants, they discovered that lineages containing the greatest numbers of jumping genes also tended to have the most species today.

Even more revealing was the timing of these genetic changes. The researchers found that several major ant lineages experienced separate surges in jumping gene activity during the early Paleogene (about 66 million years ago). These bursts occurred shortly before the groups began expanding into the thousands of species that exist today.

"The asteroid impact had dramatic consequences for the environment. We have now found the genomic mechanism that connects these ecological upheavals to the subsequent rapid diversification of the ants: transposable elements," explains Lukas Schrader.

Ants are not the only animals showing this evolutionary pattern. Previous studies have also identified periods of increased jumping gene activity associated with the emergence of new species in other groups, including primates and bats.

The researchers also discovered a connection between jumping genes and the expansion of gene families that allow ants to communicate through chemical signals.

Of particular importance were genes responsible for odorant receptors, which enable ants to detect and respond to smells. These receptors are essential to nearly every aspect of ant social behavior. Ants depend heavily on chemical cues to find their way through the environment, identify members of their own colonies, and coordinate activities with other ants.

The findings suggest that jumping genes may have contributed to the expansion of these sensory systems, potentially improving the ability of ants to detect and interpret the chemical messages that make their complex societies possible.

Tracing 100 Million Years of Ant Evolution

To investigate how jumping genes influenced ant evolution, the international research team examined and compared the genomes of 163 ant species. These represented twelve of the 16 ant subfamilies that survive today, providing a broad picture of the genetic diversity found across modern ants.

Using these genomic comparisons, the scientists reconstructed how transposable elements evolved and changed in activity over the past 100 million years.

Their results reveal a striking connection between ancient bursts of jumping gene activity and the later diversification of ants, suggesting that DNA sequences once dismissed as genetic parasites may have helped one of Earth's most successful insect groups flourish.

#366. Curiosity Blog, Sols 5043-5049: Preparing to Drill at ‘Nazca’

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Mars Science Laboratory Mission Team Members

Written by Lucy Lim, Planetary Scientist at NASA’s Goddard Space Flight Center

Earth planning date: Friday, Oct. 16, 2026

Following the Basque Lakes drill campaign, Curiosity has returned to a spot just a few meters away from the "Chiu Chiu" block to conduct our next drill campaign. "Chiu Chiu" itself wasn't a good candidate for drilling but the block just beyond it was much more promising, so it became our drive target during Monday's planning. I was on planning Friday as Geology and Mineralogy Science Theme Lead when we received our close-up views of the neighbor block from the post-drive imaging and our team was happy to see that it was jam-packed with the "disc- and needle-" shaped lumps, just like "Chiu Chiu." We quickly gave the science go-ahead to proceed with preparations for drilling at the newly named "Nazca" site.

One major hypothesis about how the "discs and needles" texture formed is that the characteristic shapes were originally created during the growth of crystals, or "crystal forms." Minerals will tend to grow into specific shapes depending on their microscopic crystal structure, similar to the salt crystals that you can grow in your kitchen as a home science project. (Maybe you have grown one of those for a science class in school!) The relatively large sizes of the crystal forms at "Nazca" and "Chiu Chiu" are providing clues about the temperature at the time when they grew and the availability of salts and fluids. However, we can't necessarily assume that the modern-day composition represents the original mineral because sometimes mineral crystals are replaced by other minerals some time after original crystallization. The new minerals are then known as "pseudomorphs" because they have the same shape as the first minerals but not the same composition or molecular structure.

As we prepared for the drill preload test, we also planned several special observations aimed at highly detailed characterization of the crystal forms. MAHLI will execute a Quantitative Relief Model ("QRM") observation consisting of five overlapping images at slightly different geometries. The combination of these will enable a detailed three-dimensional relief model to be constructed. In addition, MAHLI will also add extra frames to its highest-resolution imaging by doubling the number of focus mechanism steps. Together, these observations will increase the resolution of the 3D models we will have of the crystal forms.

Meanwhile, the ChemCam LIBS will conduct a "depth profile" experiment to obtain compositional information from slightly deeper layers within a nearby crystal-form-rich target, "Toucan". Normally the 150 LIBS laser shots from each sol's observation are spaced out over a five-point raster at 30 shots per point, but this time we are concentrating all 150 laser shots on the same spot on the rock. Since each laser shot vaporizes a very small amount of the rock to form a plasma, the 150-shot profile should penetrate approximately five times deeper than a normal 30-shot point observation. We will thus be able to observe the composition deeper inside the exposed crystal form and see whether it's different from the surface-level composition. There are several reasons this could be the case including exposure of the surface to the modern Martian environment and also the circumstances in which the crystal form developed originally, so it's an experiment worth pursuing.

The LIBS, DRT and APXS will also be applied to the "Nazca" spot before the preload test to characterize its surface composition as thoroughly as possible before drilling. The third LIBS of the plan will go to "Condor," a nearby block in which a finely layered texture is exposed.

We also continued to observe the modern Martian environment as twilight cloud season begins. In this plan we included some nighttime imaging of the larger and brighter of Mars' two satellites, Phobos. Capturing images of Phobos and the nearby sky when Phobos is full can show sunlight reflecting off the moon and scattering in the Martian atmosphere, providing a good way to estimate the particle sizes in the Martian clouds. We can do similar measurements sometimes with solar observations, but the Phobos data allow us to measure cloud particle sizes later in the night. We expect to be measuring high-altitude carbon dioxide (CO2) clouds in this observation.

Mars is the fourth planet from the Sun, and the seventh largest. It’s the only planet we know of inhabited…

#668. Unveiling Starbirth Mysteries

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

In 1985, the physicist Heinz R. Pagels wrote, "Stars are like animals in the wild. We may see the young, but never the actual birth, which is a veiled and secret event." In the decades since then, astronomers have drawn closer and closer to figuring out what actually goes on in the veiled stellar nurseries throughout the Universe. The tale of starbirth we know today invokes the collapse of clouds of gas and dust into prestellar cores, but it still has many unknowns, such as the role of magnetic fields in the clouds where stars are born.

Researchers from Kyushu University and the Max Planck Institute for Extraterrestrial Physics have used radio astronomy techniques to study the magnetic field influence in prestellar cores. Lead researcher Doris Arzoumanian at Kyushu explained her team's findings using the Institute for Radio Astronomy in the Millimetre Range (IRAM) to study a prestellar core called L1544. It lies in a region of the Milky Way called the Taurus Molecular Complex. At a distance of 140 parsecs, it's one of the closest star-forming regions to Earth.

A graphical representation of ion-neutral drift in the L1544 prestellar core. The blue lines represent the magnetic field lines, which are bent due to the gravitational contraction of the core. The red and green dots depict the ion and neutral molecular species, respectively, and the arrows trace their inflow motion towards the core center (the faster they travel, the longer the arrows). While in the outer part of the core both the ions and neutrals are attached to the magnetic field lines, within the inner part of the core the neutrals decouple from the magnetic field lines and infall faster compared to the ions, which remain attached to the field lines. This ion-neutral decoupling, known as ambipolar diffusion is required for the onset of the gravitational collapse of the prestellar core, which will produce a protostar in its center and ultimately a stellar system similar to our own Solar System. Credit: Arzoumanian, et al.

âPrestellar cores are fascinating stellar bodies," said Arzoumanian. "They are dense and cold, and a source of a lot of complex chemistry. The cold environment allows for molecules to assemble into more complex ones like precursors of prebiotic organic molecules. One of the questions we are investigating is the role of magnetic fields in star formation. Strong magnetic fields permeate prestellar cores. If that field is too strong, it can delay gravitational collapse and therefore star formation. We wanted to investigate how prestellar cores reduce the strength of their magnetic field.â

The result of the team's research is the detection of a phenomenon called "ambipolar diffusion". This happens when neutral particles in a plasma decouple from the plasma. In the molecular clouds that are the most likely homes to starbirth creches, there are both neutral particles and some amount of partially ionized gas where the ions are strongly coupled to magnetic fields that thread through the cloud. Neutral particles interact with the embedded magnetic field indirectly through collisions, so their velocities can be measured. Studies such as Arzoumanian's give key insights into the activity of molecules in the core and with its magnetic fields.

Prestellar cores are cold regions, despite the fact that they're coalescing to form future stars. They can be anywhere from 10 to 30 degrees Kelvin (about -263 C to -243 C). That's cold enough to freeze molecules to dust grains, essentially veiling them from deeper analysis. So, the team searched around for other molecules to act as "tracers" of activity. âWe selected Diazenylium-d1 (N2D+), an ion, and para-monodeuterated ammonia (para-NH2D), a neutral molecule, as our tracers because they are generally located in similar high-density regions within prestellar cores,â said team member Silvia Spezzano, group leader at the Max Planck Institute for Extraterrestrial Physics. âWe therefore collected spectral data of the core and modeled the velocity of the two molecules.â

The result was a very clear velocity difference between those molecules of about 0.05 km/second, which the team interpreted as "ion-neutral drift". This happens as the density of the prestellar core increases during accretion. It gets protected from radiation, and that puts a damper on ionization. The end result is that the coupling between the molecules and the embedded magnetic field grows weaker. As the neutral particles fall towards the center of the prestellar core, they speed up. In the meantime, the ions remain coupled to the magnetic field, causing the velocity difference. This process is ambipolar diffusion, and it has been difficult to observe. "Until now, observing this phenomenon in a prestellar core was a major challenge,â said Arzoumanian. âAs ambipolar diffusion continues, the strength of the magnetic field decreases. Eventually, gravity becomes the primary driving force in the core, resulting in its gravitational collapse into a protostar.â

To confirm their finding, the team hopes to observe other prestellar cores using techniques of radio astronomy, as well as principles of dust physics and astrochemistry, according to Arzoumanian. A larger sample of prestellar cores should be examined to study ion-neutral pairs in cores at different stages of evolution. Statistical studies will also provide a way to assess the impact of the magnetic field geometry and density structures in these cores, along with other effects. The team also suggests that future studies could focus on ion-neutral drift velocities in other structures in star-forming regions, such as dense filaments.

Such observations should give new insights, essentially pulling aside the veil on other aspects of star formation and an understanding of the 3D magnetic field strength in cores, and more insights into the evolution of dust size distribution within prestellar cores as they continue on the road to forming stars. âUnderstanding star formation addresses a fundamental question about the origin of life in planetary systems and helps us better understand the universe as a whole," said Arzoumanian.

Capturing the Cosmic 'Drift' Before a Star is Born

Probing the Ion-Neutral Drift Velocity Toward the L15445 Prestellar Core

Heinz R. Pagels (1985). âPerfect symmetry: the search for the beginning of timeâ, Simon & Schuster

#374. APOD: 2026 October 28 – The Mutual Occultation of Ganymede and Io

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Discover the cosmos! Each day a different image or photograph of our fascinating universe is featured, along with a brief explanation written by a professional astronomer.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Explanation: Jupiter is one of the least lonely planets in the Solar System, at least when it comes to natural satellites. 115 moons have been discovered orbiting Jupiter so far! (Saturn has the most with 293, but who’s counting.) Ganymede and Io, two of Jupiter’s Galilean moons, met in the sky on October 20th, 2026. Today’s video captures the meeting, with Ganymede briefly blocking Io from view. This event, when an astronomical object passes in front of another and either completely or partially blocks it, is called a mutual occultation. The mutual occultations of Galilean moons are rare to witness because they require Earth’s edge-on view of their orbits. This occurs once every 6 years when Jupiter reaches its equinox and appears less “tilted”. Ganymede is also casting its shadow on Jupiter’s right side. Any fictional Jovian species within the shadow would experience a solar eclipse!Tomorrow's picture: a planet grows horns

#384. The Land of the Standing-Up Rocks

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

A national monument since the 1920s, Chiricahua is America's newest national park.

Some 27 million years ago, a volcano in what's now southeastern Arizona unleashed a spectacular series of eruptions, strewing more than 1,000 times as much volcanic material across the landscape as Mount St. Helens did in 1980.

The explosive eruptions blanketed the region, part of the Chiricahua Apache Nation’s traditional lands, with ash and pumice. Layered into this were massive pyroclastic flows that hardened into deposits of welded rhyolitic tuff that in some areas were nearly 1,600 feet (500 meters) thick, about three times the height of the Washington Monument. As the magma chamber emptied, the ground above it collapsed, leaving a gaping depression—the Turkey Creek caldera—that was roughly 12 miles (19 kilometers) wide and 4,900 feet (1,500 meters) deep.

An array of geologic processes, including erosion and weathering, gradually sculpted the welded layer of tuff into the collection of dramatic spires, hoodoos, and canyons that visitors to the caldera area encounter today.

The Apache called the area "The Land of the Standing-Up Rocks" for good reason. Hiking through Rhyolite Canyon or driving through Bonita Canyon means encountering a dizzying maze of rock pinnacles, some hundreds of feet tall. Others, like Big Balanced Rock, have surprisingly large boulders precariously perched on top. Meanwhile, those at Heart of Rocks and Massai Point are gathered into groups of hundreds.

The area was designated a national monument in 1924 to preserve its remarkable geology. In October 2026, it was redesignated as Chiricahua National Park (pronounced cheery-cow-ah). The OLI (Operational Land Imager) on Landsat 9 captured this cloud-free image of the new national park on July 2, 2026.

Individual rock spires are too small to distinguish, but the eroding rhyolitic tuff landscape appears gray. Stream valleys dense with trees and other vegetation, including Rhyolite Canyon and Bonita Canyon, snake across the landscape. With four biogeographic regions converging in the park—the Sonoran Desert, the Chihuahuan Desert, the Rocky Mountains, and the Sierra Madre—it is a haven of plant diversity.

Among the 1,000 species growing within the park's varied desert, grassland, chaparral, deciduous forest, and coniferous forest ecosystems are agaves, prickly pear cacti, yuccas, manzanita, pine, fir, cypress, sycamore, juniper, and oak. The park's location within the Chiricahua Mountains puts it among several "sky islands" in Arizona—isolated mountain ranges that emerge from the surrounding desert "sea." According to the National Park Service, the range's highest point (outside the park) rises 2,986 meters (9,795 feet) above sea level, more than 1,800 meters above the surrounding valley.

NASA Earth Observatory image by Lauren Dauphin, using Landsat data from the U.S. Geological Survey. Story by Adam Voiland.

The meandering Congaree River sustains towering forest ecosystems and fashions oxbow lakes as it shifts across its floodplain.

Sites relevant to the start of the American Revolutionary War are interspersed throughout the modern-day Boston metropolitan region.

The colonial communities of “America’s historic triangle” played defining roles in the road to American independence.

#520. Biodiversity hotspots in southwestern Germany are losing their species richness

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Climate change and shifts in land use are affecting habitats and, consequently, plant diversity. To better understand the consequences of these developments, long-term and comprehensive data are essential. The "Floristic Mapping of Baden-Württemberg," coordinated by the Stuttgart State Museum of Natural History, provides a foundation for this. The data collected over five decades as part of this citizen science project document significant changes in the flora of southwestern Germany, drawing on more than 2.5 million plant observations. They also provide insights into changes in plant diversity in Germany and Central Europe.

#674. Dead Planets Orbiting a Dead Star

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

All stars die eventually, but they don't all die the same. A star's death is determined by its mass. Only the most massive stars die in supernovae explosions, and those calamitous explosions announce their death in a burst of pent-up light, an exclamation point marking the end of their lives of fusion.

But our Sun, a more moderately-sized star, will experience a much different end. As it burns its fuel and slowly loses mass, it well eventually lose it gravitational hold on its outer layers of gas. It will expand, and the outer layers of gas will be expelled into the space around star, creating a gorgeous planetary nebula, quite possibly Nature's most beautiful creation. The nebula will last tens of thousands of years, and after that, only a cinder-like white dwarf will remain. Fusion will be a distant memory for this stellar corpse, which will radiate only remnant heat. White dwarfs spend an astronomical eternity in this state, until they eventually cool enough to become a black dwarf. But the Universe hasn't existed long enough for any of those to form yet.

There's another part of this transition to white dwarf, and it's destructive. When a star like our Sun expands on its way to becoming a white dwarf, it engulfs and destroys its inner planets. In our case, the Sun will definitely destroy Mercury and Venus, and almost certainly destroy Earth.

When astronomers gaze out into the heavens, they find many planetary nebulae (a historic name that has nothing to do with planets), lit up by the white dwarfs in their centers. Sometimes, they find something else. Sometimes, they find a debris disk made up of the planets that once orbited the star. For a long time, astronomers have speculated that planets can form out of this debris, a strange, second chance at almost-life. It's highly unlikely that life could once again spawn on one of these resurrected planets orbiting a zombie star. But it's a huge Universe, so you never know.

Astronomers recently found a white dwarf surrounded by a debris disk that's split into two components. This could signal the presence of planets.

These findings are presented in research titled "A double debris disc around a white dwarf sculpted by two planetary bodies." It's published in Nature Astronomy, and the lead author is Nicola Gentille Fusillo. Gentille Fusillo is an astronomer at the UniversitÃ  degli Studi di Trieste, (Universe of Trieste) in Italy.

âThe system is a rare glimpse of the violent, chaotic process by which a planetary system meets its end, and it hints that dead stars can have disc structures far more complex than previously realised.â - Nicola Gentille Fusillo, UniversitÃ  degli Studi di Trieste.

"Many planetary systems survive the metamorphosis of their host stars into white dwarfs, as demonstrated by the detection of dusty circumstellar discs around these stellar remnants and chemical signatures of planetary debris in atmospheres of these stars," the authors write, though survive might not be exactly the right word. In any case, dust is common in these disks, while gas is less common, having been found in only 21 of them.

"All of the 21 known gas discs exhibit double-peaked line profiles characteristic of a rotating flat disc," the researchers write. "The mechanism behind the generation of gas is still debated, but for at least one system it has been linked with the presence of a surviving planetesimal embedded within the dust disc."

Astronomers think that planetesimals in disks around white dwarfs generate gas from the dust through collisions. If the planetesimal is moving fast enough through the disk, then when it collides with dust it could vapourize it into gas. This is the most likely mechanism, though others are also proposed.

So the presence of gas is one indication that a planetesimal is orbiting the white dwarf. Another indicator is the line profile, which is not double-peaked, but quadruple-peaked.

"Here we report the discovery of WD J132800.85â145019.95, a new gas-disc system that exhibits quadruple-peaked instead of double-peaked line profiles," the researchers write. "These emission lines originate from two separate gaseous rings extending from 0.14âRâ to 0.34âRâ and from 0.54âRâ to 1.93âRâ."

The top panel shows the quadruple-peaked line profile for WD J132800.85â145019.95, the subject in this research. The bottom panel shows the double-peaked line profile for SDSS J1228+1040, which is considered the prototypical white dwarf with a gas disk. "The morphology of the emission lines in WD J1328â1450 indicates the presence of two concentric discs around this white dwarf," the authors write. Image Credit: Gentile Fusillo, N.P., Lagos-Vilches, F., GÃ¤nsicke, B.T. et al. A double debris disc around a white dwarf sculpted by two planetary bodies. Nat Astron (2026). https://doi.org/10.1038/s41550-026-03004-3

âThe structure we see in this system is particularly intriguing because the two gaseous rings are remarkably stable and remain separated by a gap,â study co-author Felipe Lagos-Vilches, an astronomer at the University of Warwick, UK, said in a press release.

âThe system is a rare glimpse of the violent, chaotic process by which a planetary system meets its end, and it hints that dead stars can have disc structures far more complex than previously realised,â added lead author Gentile Fusillo, who is also affiliated with the INAF-Astronomical Observatory of Trieste.

There are probably two small planets, or planet-like bodies, responsible for the disk's structure. Each is on its own orbit. The inner one isn't much more than an asteroid, only a few kilometers in diameter. The other is about the size of Pluto, or maybe as large as the Moon.

Astronomers rely on the red-shift and blue-shift of light to detect gaseous disks like the one around WD J1328â1450. Gas moving toward us is blue-shifted, and gas moving away is red-shifted. In a rotating disk, the gas on one side is moving toward us, while the gas on the other is moving away. The shifted light on each side is detected with a spectrograph, which illustrates the shifts as shown in this diagram. In this case, the two disks are rotating at different speeds, creating four peaks. Image Credit: ESO/L. CalÃ§ada

The disks not only move at different speeds, but they appear to have different compositions. The inner ring seems to be enriched in magnesium, while the outer ring is stripped of magnesium. It's possible, but far from proven, that each disk originated from a destroyed planet with different compositions. Another possibility is that each ring formed from a different part of the same planet, the core and the crust, for example.

Adding to the mystery is the nature of the two planet-like bodies. The inner planet must be very dense or have very high internal strength, otherwise it would be torn apart by tidal forces at such a close distance to the white dwarf. If it lacked internal strength, then "it would need to have a density higher than any known material, ranging from â³3,600âgâcmâ3 at the inner edge to â³252âgâcmâ3 at the outer edge," the authors explain. "In the outer disc, the highest required density is â³63gâcmâ3, over three times that of the densest exo-planet."

"Therefore, we conclude that any planetesimals surviving within the discs must possess some degree of internal strength," the researchers write.

This figure shows the double-disk structure around WD J1328â1450 (inner disc A, outer disc B) and the gap to scale. The colour gradient reflects the brightness profile of the discs. Image Credit: Gentile Fusillo, N.P., Lagos-Vilches, F., GÃ¤nsicke, B.T. et al. A double debris disc around a white dwarf sculpted by two planetary bodies. Nat Astron (2026). https://doi.org/10.1038/s41550-026-03004-3

"The detection of this stable double-disc presents a new challenge for the current models of debris disc formation and evolution," the authors write. Astronomers have known about these gaseous debris disks around white dwarfs for two decades, but how they form, the architectures they develop into, and how they evolve over the long-term is not well understood. "The discovery of a stable double debris disc around WD J1328â1450 provides additional observational constraints for the development of physically more detailed models for these discs."

This system could offer a glimpse into our Solar System's future, where the Sun expands and vapourizes Earth and the other inner planets. It's possible that some time in the future, billions of years from now and long after humanity has gone extinct, this is all that remains of our home.

âThe system therefore offers a remarkable glimpse of what the future of planetary systems, including systems like our own, might look like,â said Lagos-Vilches.

#3935. The 3-Minute Rule for Having a Hard Talk

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

We’re all holding onto something we’re sure we can’t give up on, but research shows that letting go can boost our happiness a lot more than we might think.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted October 27, 2026 | Reviewed by Margaret Foley

While we might absolutely love our partner, sometimes knowing that we have to have a hard conversation makes us feel really negative. Maybe you lie awake working out exactly what you will say to your partner, and about a minute into the actual conversation, it has gone sideways. Maybe it was about the kids, or a furtive glance at a stranger. Maybe it was about money. Maybe it was about whose turn it is to empty the dishwasher, again. Experience has shown me that putting the talk off does not help much either.

The heart of these hard discussions is surprisingly direct. From an evolutionary perspective, conflict between partners is not a sign that love itself has failed. Buss (1989) proposed that conflict arises when one partner’s strategy interferes with the other’s goals, and that anger (among other feelings) is an emotional alarm signalling that interference. While the results of Buss’ study are mixed, it suggests a very simple insight, which is that the issues we find hardest to raise are often the ones where our interests and our partner’s do not really overlap.

In other words, the emotions you feel before a hard conversation provide valuable information and should not be ignored. They tell you that something matters to you. Is it your partner’s opinion? The outcome of the chat?

The challenge is delivering the information without setting off your partner’s alarms in return. The first three minutes are really going to matter. Carrère and Gottman (1999) filmed 124 newlywed couples arguing about something. Six years later, 17 of those couples had divorced, and they were couples who had started their arguments with more negativity and less warmth than those who stuck together did. Those critical three minutes at the start were enough to predict how the marriages turned out. What this means, at least to me, is that the opening for the hard discussion sets the tone. A conversation that begins with criticism or contempt leaves little room for anything positive to happen, whereas one that begins with some warmth, interest, or humor gives both people a chance to be positive. There is room for something other than negativity.

When having a hard discussion, most of us know that we should try to be present and open for our partner. Most of us also know that we have to be wary of the classic demand-withdraw pattern, where one partner pushes for change, often with criticism or blame, while the other avoids the topic, becomes defensive, and pulls away. A meta-analysis of 74 studies with more than 14,000 participants linked the pattern to lower relationship satisfaction, less intimacy, and poorer communication (Schrodt et al., 2014). What might be missed, though, is what this pattern is saying, which is that the partner who wants more change on the topic under discussion is more likely to demand, while the partner who wants less change may be more likely to withdraw (Holley et al., 2010).

We also know that emotions can be modified by food, and prepping for a hard conversation is particularly susceptible to hunger. Bushman and colleagues (2014) tracked blood sugar in married couples over three weeks. In one of my all-time favourite study designs, each evening, participants stuck pins into a voodoo doll representing their spouse, depending on how angry they felt. (Does science get any better?) More pins were correlated with lower evening glucose levels. Lange and Kurzban (2014) have questioned whether this is really about self-control, but in any case, the practical lesson seems low risk, which is do not open a difficult topic when either of you is hungry, or worse, “hangry.”

One final consideration that might be worth a thought is to imagine how an objective observer would view your discussion. Over two years, Finkel and colleagues (2013) followed 120 married couples. In year two, half of the couples spent just seven minutes, three times over the year, writing about their most recent disagreement from the perspective of a neutral third party who wants the best for everyone involved. While average marital quality declined for both groups in the first year, the decline stopped for those who did the writing, but not for the other couples.

Now, for some tips. Let’s start with remembering that, above all else, the first three minutes matter when starting a hard conversation.

While I would personally love to think otherwise, difficult conversations will never be easy, and perhaps they should not be, given that they tend to arise where two people’s interests are not aligned. When they do happen, a warm and positive first few minutes, a full stomach, and a little distance from the fight may help the person you love actually hear you.

Bushman, B. J., DeWall, C. N., Pond, R. S., Jr., & Hanus, M. D. (2014). Low glucose relates to greater aggression in married couples. Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, 111(17), 6254–6257. https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1400619111

Buss, D. M. (1989). Conflict between the sexes: Strategic interference and the evocation of anger and upset. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 56(5), 735–747. https://doi.org/10.1037/0022-3514.56.5.735

Carrère, S., & Gottman, J. M. (1999). Predicting divorce among newlyweds from the first three minutes of a marital conflict discussion. Family Process, 38(3), 293–301. https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1545-5300.1999.00293.x

Finkel, E. J., Slotter, E. B., Luchies, L. B., Walton, G. M., & Gross, J. J. (2013). A brief intervention to promote conflict reappraisal preserves marital quality over time. Psychological Science, 24(8), 1595–1601. https://doi.org/10.1177/0956797612474938

Holley, S. R., Sturm, V. E., & Levenson, R. W. (2010). Exploring the basis for gender differences in the demand-withdraw pattern. Journal of Homosexuality, 57(5), 666–684. https://doi.org/10.1080/00918361003712145

Lange, F., & Kurzban, R. (2014). Sugar levels relate to aggression in couples without supporting the glucose model of self-control. Frontiers in Psychology, 5, 572. https://doi.org/10.3389/fpsyg.2014.00572

Schrodt, P., Witt, P. L., & Shimkowski, J. R. (2014). A meta-analytical review of the demand/withdraw pattern of interaction and its associations with individual, relational, and communicative outcomes. Communication Monographs, 81(1), 28–58. https://doi.org/10.1080/03637751.2013.813632

#990. The Best Beauty Minis to Try Before Committing to Full Size

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Small but mighty picks to save space, reduce waste, and test before committing to full size.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Size matters—especially when it comes to your most-used beauty essentials like shampoo, conditioner, curl creams, and other stylers. Those are the products that you’ll happily take in jumbo size. But there’s something so cute (and eco-conscious, too) about shrinking some of your other favorites like lipstick, eye shadow palettes, and even fragrance down to tiny, travel-friendly versions. Mini mania has taken over the beauty world, and not only are some of the most beloved products now available in mini versions, but there are newly opened retail spaces in a few major cities dedicated to baby-sized beauty. Sure, minis are a lot easier to tote around, but on a more practical level, it’s less consumption; you’re more likely to finish it before it expires, and in formats like fragrance or skin care, you can try it with less commitment before deciding if you want to go all in on a full-sized option. Here are some of our favorites.

Tangle Teezer’s breakage-reducing, two-tiered teeth technology and easy-grip handle remain the same on this mini metallic version of its versatile detangling brush.

Size: 6.1 inches (vs. 8.7-inch regular, 9.3-inch large)

Opposites attract in this tiny, sweet-meets-tart duo. Henry Rose’s vanilla gourmand, Dave, sits on one side with its notes of whipped Chantilly vanilla, citrus, neroli, cocoa shell, and tonka bean. The second half of the pair is the brand’s new Rhu Berry, featuring notes of rhubarb, sugared fruits, osmanthus flower, choux pastry, and silky musk.

Size: 0.33 oz each (vs. 1.7 oz/50 mL regular size)

If you’ve tried the full-size version of this antioxidant-rich, deeply moisturizing, gourmand-scented body lotion, you’ll immediately see why you must carry one in your bag at all times.

Size: 1.6 fl oz (vs. 10.1 fl oz full size)

#683. Massive Stellar Collisions Could Explain Early Supermassive Black Holes

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Astronomers are in an ongoing scientific wrestling match with some of the JWST's observations. The telescope can gaze back in time by sensing red-shifted light from the early Universe. One of its surprising findings is that supermassive black holes (SMBH) existed long before theory says they should. Some of the SMBHs it detected were found only 400 million and 500 million years after the Big Bang.

So far, there's no solid, widely-held explanation for this. But one contender involves stellar mergers.

New research suggests that in the dense stellar environment in the early Universe, repeated stellar mergers could've lead to eventual SMBHs. It's titled "A Cosmological Framework for Stellar Collisions at High Redshift in Proto-globular Clusters, Nuclear Star Clusters, and Little Red Dots," and it's published in The Astrophysical Journal. The lead author is Claire Williams, a doctoral student in the Department of Physics and Astronomy at UCLA.

"Observations and cosmological simulations indicate that the early Universe hosted numerous compact, high-density stellar systems, where close encounters and physical collisions between stars were likely common," the authors write. "We develop a bottom-up framework for stellar dynamics in such environments, spanning systems with and without intermediate-mass and supermassive black holes, and covering regimes where stellar collisions may or may not dominate the evolution."

The work connects stellar collisions in dense environments with different outcomes. In this work, the researchers started with initial conditions from the AREPO cosmological simulations. They used these to set the parameters for early star clusters, along with the JWST's observations of early star clusters and high-redshift galaxies.

The authors note that these clusters are very similar to Nuclear Star Clusters (NSC) like the one found near Sgr A*, the Milky Way's SMBH. "There, collisions can lead to unusual outcomes, such as the apparent regeneration of an old population with merger products that appear as young massive stars," the authors write.

The researchers point out that almost all galaxies host a SMBH and about 80% of early galaxies also host an NSC. That "suggests that collision-rich environments exist across the Universe," they explain. "As massive black holes and their host star clusters formed, collisions shaped their dynamical and physical evolution."

The team ran simulations to try to understand all of this. The simulations included a variety of different cluster environments with different parameters like velocity dispersions and mass density profiles. "We initialize 1000 stars for each test case, located within 1 pc of the center," they write. This is fewer stars than are within 1 parsec of the Milky Way's black hole. Estimates for that stellar population vary, but some sources say there are more than 6,000.

The results show that stellar collisions are prevalent in a range of star cluster and NSC environments. Not only that, but runaway sequential collisions can eventually create very massive stars (VMS) very early in the Universe. There's no universal definition for VMSs, but something above 100 solar masses is often used.

âPerhaps the strangest outcome of our simulations is that stars can merge repeatedly within the cluster until they form one gigantic star with a mass equal to that of an entire cluster,â said Williams. Universe Today readers know what that means. âWhen a star this huge reaches the end of its life, models suggest that it may have no option but to collapse into itself and form a massive black hole.â

âOne place in the nearby universe where collisions between stars are common is the galactic center,â Williams said. âIn our model, once these very massive stars turn into intermediate-mass black holes, the system resembles our own galactic center, which is a black hole surrounded by a dense star cluster.â

This isn't the first time that astrophysicists have wondered if stellar collisions could be behind the early SMBH. In fact, this idea precedes the JWST by several decades. But this research takes it a step further by showing how the JWST's intriguing Little Red Dots could be connected.

Little Red Dots are compact objects that appear to be black holes surrounded by ample gas. The black hole is rapidly feeding on this gas and growing very quickly, perhaps at a super-Eddington rate.

This image has become famous in astronomical circles. It shows the JWST's famous Little Red Dots, of which hundreds have been observed in the early Universe. They could be ancient black holes surrounded by gas, which masks the x-rays that would give away their nature. If they are black holes, they could be growing quickly due to super-Eddington accretion. This research supports that idea, by showing that plentiful stellar collisions could be generating all the gas, which is fuel for the black hole's growth, but also masks x-rays. Image Credit: NASA, ESA, CSA, STScI, D. Kocevski (Colby College)

Not all of the stellar collisions in the simulations resulted in more massive, merged stars that were doomed to explode as supernovae, leading to black holes. In some of the collisions, both stars were destroyed, and reduced to gas. This could have created a situation where a black hole is surrounded by not only a dense stellar population, but also a dense gas environment.

"Finally, we show that high rates of destructive collisions can rapidly build up extremely dense gaseous environments around massive black holes, potentially providing an analog to the observed population of Little Red Dots," they write.

These simple schematics show two separate pathways in the dense clusters. The left shows how stellar collisions in a cluster without a black hole are constructive and lead to very massive stars. The right shows how collisions in a cluster with a black hole are generally destructive, leading to the build-up of thick gas near the black hole, which is then accreted. This could be how Little Red Dots are created. Image Credit: Claire E. Williams et al 2026 ApJ 1012 1

âLittle red dots are a hot topic in astronomy. You can compare studies of people trying to understand LRDs to the tale of blind people who are trying to describe an elephant by touching it, and each person describes it differently based on what part theyâre touching,â UCLA astrophysicist and co-author Smadar Naoz said. âOur research connects several of those perspectives together and may help us see more of the elephant.â

Intermediate-Mass Black Holes (IMBH), an elusive type of object predicted by the theory of hierarchical black hole growth, is also part of this. IMBH are more massive than stellar-mass black holes, but less massive than SMBH. There are some firm IMBH candidates, but none that everybody agrees are IMBH. The best example might be in the Omega Centauri globular cluster. Others have been detected via gravitational waves emitted during the mergers that created them.

This work shows that the dense clusters common in the early Universe could be the environment that spawned early IMBH. These could have merged, and in the past, those mergers would've been more common, generating the SMBH we see around us today.

âDense star clusters provide a natural birthplace for massive black-hole seeds in our model,â Williams said. âThe same environment that forms the black hole can also provide stars and gas that influence its subsequent growth.â

Evan Gough is a science-loving guy with no formal education who loves Earth, forests, hiking, and heavy music. He's guided by Carl Sagan's quote: "Understanding is a kind of ecstasy."

#589. Quantum particles never completely forget where they came from

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

A quantum birthmark keeps a chaotic particle at least twice as likely to return to its starting state, even after a very long time.

Stir a drop of milk into a cup of tea, and within seconds there’s no telling where the drop first landed.

Chaotic mixing like that erases any record of a starting point. In the quantum world of atoms and electrons, new research suggests, the erasing never finishes.

Physicists at Tampere University in Finland, Harvard University and TU Dresden in Germany call what’s left behind a quantum birthmark.

Even a particle bouncing around chaotically stays at least twice as likely to be found back in its starting condition as in any comparable one.

That holds when its motion is averaged over a very long time. The work is theory, built on math and computer simulations rather than a lab experiment.

Joonas Keski-Rahkonen, a co-author, works in the Quantum Control and Dynamics group at Tampere’s Computational Physics Laboratory.

“In the everyday world, chaos wipes the slate clean. What we found is that quantum systems can’t hide their origin, even in the middle of chaos,” he said.

The team worked with one of physics’ oldest test cases: a ball bouncing forever around a billiard table with rounded ends.

Physicists call that shape a stadium, and waves in oddly shaped spaces like it tend to turn chaotic.

A real ball on that table bounces off the curved walls in a new direction every time. Track it long enough and it visits every spot equally, with no hint left of where it began.

Particles as small as electrons don’t behave like billiard balls.

They spread out like ripples on water, and the most a physicist can say is how likely a particle is to be found in one spot rather than another.

A small, bunched-up ripple of that kind is called a wave packet.

The researchers simulated wave packets inside a stadium with soft walls the particle could seep into slightly, closer to what electrons meet in nanoscale devices.

Within a few bounces, each wave packet looked like random noise spread across the whole stadium.

At any single moment, that noise looked featureless. The memory showed up only when the team averaged the motion over a very long stretch of time.

Averaged that way, a wave packet stayed at least twice as likely to be found back in its starting state as in a typical state with the same energy.

For some kinds of starting states, the minimum rises to three times. The advantage didn’t belong to the first moment alone.

Every shape the wave packet passed through on its way kept it too, and the result held no matter when the team began averaging.

Classical physics predicts no advantage at all. By the team’s math, every sealed-off quantum system that doesn’t begin in a settled state carries a birthmark of its own.

To see what makes a birthmark stronger, the team launched four wave packets.

Three left the center of the stadium along paths that repeat: straight up and down, straight side to side, and a bow-tie shape. The fourth started off-center on an ordinary chaotic path.

The ordinary launch came out close to the minimum, at about 1.8 times. The team noted that a single wave packet can land slightly below two, since two is an average.

The up-and-down launch, which kept bouncing back to where it began, reached about 6.6 times, and about 8 in a stadium with hard walls.

Early bounces mattered most. Ripple patterns from the wave packet’s second hit on the wall still showed up in the long-time average.

Even the most ordinary wave packet reached only about half of the combinations of position and direction open to it. A classical ball would eventually have covered them all.

In 1984, Eric Heller, a Harvard physicist and co-author of the new study, found that some quantum states keep sharp imprints along repeating paths, a pattern called quantum scarring.

Scars were long treated as a rare exception to chaos. The team now treats them as one case of something broader: a scar is a birthmark made stronger by a path the particle keeps returning to.

Keski-Rahkonen tied the result to a larger question.

“That matters beyond textbook physics. It speaks to how quantum systems settle into equilibrium, and how our familiar classical world emerges from the quantum-mechanical rules,” he said.

He also pointed to a practical side. “For instance, it matters that a system which quietly remembers its starting point behaves differently from one that forgets,” Keski-Rahkonen said.

In tiny electronic devices holding even thousands of electrons, several statistical measures commonly run three or more times higher than random behavior would predict.

The authors read that as a sign of a birthmark. The American Physical Society’s magazine Physics highlighted the study in a feature article.

The math assumes a quantum system sealed off from its surroundings. Once such a system leaks information into the world around it, the universal part of the birthmark fades.

The particle then starts behaving more like a classical ball, though marks left by early bounces may still remain.

So far, the team hasn’t tested the idea in a lab. How strongly a birthmark shows up also depends on what physicists choose to measure, and the researchers call that an important question for future work.

They suggest looking with quantum simulators, machines related to quantum computers that are built from carefully controlled atoms.

In those machines, physicists can set how many states a system has room for, so they could test whether a birthmark holds as the system grows.

For now, the birthmark exists on paper and in simulations. The next step is measuring one in a real device kept isolated well enough to hold on to it.

The full study was published in the journal Physical Review X.

#692. This Oddbal Reflective Lava World is Challenging to Understand

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Among Kepler and TESS's many surprising exoplanet discoveries are the ultra-short period (USP) planets. These planets move very quickly and complete an orbit in less than 24 hours. Ancient astronomers thought that Mercury was so fastâit goes around the Sun every 88 daysâthat they named it after the Roman god Mercury, who was the fast-travelling messenger of the Gods. They couldn't have known how sluggish Mercury is compared to the USPs.

Their speed is just one of their remarkable qualities. Since they're so close to their stars, USPs are exposed to the full, blazing force of their stars' powerful radiation. This means these planets should have lost their atmospheres to space.

But one USP named TOI-561 b seemed to be different. Early observations detected hints of an atmosphere, and a 2025 paper baed on limited JWST observations suggested it had an atmosphere made of volatiles like water and carbon dioxide. Now, some of the same researchers revisited the exoplanet. The new work used the JWST to examine the exoplanet's atmosphere in more detail.

An artist's illustration of TOI-561 b. In 2025, JWST observations showed that the exoplanet had an unexpected atmosphere. The researchers thought it had a thick atmosphere above a molten surface. Image credit: NASA / ESA / CSA / Ralf Crawford, STScI.

The research is "A Highly Reflective Atmosphere on the Lava World TOI-561b Revealed by JWST/NIRSpec Phase Curves," and it's published in The Astronomical Journal. The first author is Samuel Boucher from the Department of Physics and Trottier Institute for Research on Exoplanets, at the UniversitÃ© de MontrÃ©al, Canada.

"Our discovery challenges current theories. We would expect a heavily irradiated rocky planet like TOI-561 b to have lost its atmosphere billions of years ago." - Professor Lisa Dang, University of Waterloo.

"One of the most pressing questions in exoplanet science today is whether small rocky exoplanets can host atmospheres," the authors write. If they do, scientists expect these atmosphere to be secondary rather than primordial. Some portion of the atmosphere could come from off-gassing or other exchanges with the exoplanet's interior, and that is evidence of processes that are not visible in remote observations. That has implications for understanding how rocky planets evolve, and how habitability may arise.

In a twist, rocky USPs may be the best place to look for secondary atmospheres and the clues they hold to deeper planetary processes. That's because they're probably magma oceans, at least on their daysides, and they can "be in continuous exchange with and fuel an atmosphere," the researchers write.

"Ultrashort period (USP) rocky planets are expected to be depleted of any substantial gaseous envelope due to the intense irradiation they receive from their host star, making the recent detection of an atmosphere around TOI-561 b particularly surprising," the authors write. "That finding was based on the planetâs bulk density and dayside emission spectrum, but full-orbit phase-curve observations offer a more comprehensive way to constrain the presence and characteristics of a planetâs atmosphere."

In this work, the authors observed TOI-561 b for 37 hours, and because it completes an orbit in about 10.5 hours, 37 hours of observing time was equal to more than three complete orbits. That means the JWST got a really good look at the planet. This generated more fullsome data to understand the exoplanet, its atmosphere, and what that can tell planetary scientists about hidden processes.

This graphic shows the JWST's NIRSpec spectrum for TOI-561 b from the 2025 research. Though the data was less complete than the new observations, it showed that the exoplanet likely had a thick volatile-rich atmosphere. Image Credit: Illustration: NASA, ESA, CSA, Ralf Crawford (STScI), Science: J. Teske (Carnegie Science Earth and Planets Laboratory), A. Piette (University of Birmingham), T. Lichtenberg (Groningen), N. Wallack (Carnegie Science Earth and Planets Laboratory)

The observations of TOI-561 b in previous research showed that the exoplanet's dayside was much cooler than it should be for a bare rock world this close to a star. There could only be two explanations for this: either the heat it was reflected back into space, or it was carried to the nightside by an atmosphere. These new full-orbit observations gave researchers their first look at the exoplanet's nightside.

They found that the nightside wasn't warm enough to have acquired the missing heat. About three-quarters of it must have been reflected into space. To try to understand what was happening, the researchers turned to a General Circulation Model (GCM) and compared it to their observations. "We conclude from the GCM simulations that heat redistribution alone cannot match the observed dayside fluxes or amplitude of the phase curve, and a high-albedo source (Ab = 0.6) indicative of clouds or haze," they write.

âWe measured how the planetâs brightness and heat changed throughout its orbit," said Professor Lisa Dang, project lead at the University of Waterloo, Ontario, Canada. "This technique, known as a phase curve, revealed that the planet reflects a significant amount of light from its star, and that heat is being redistributed around the planet instead of only on the sunlit side. Together, these point to the presence of an atmosphere.â

They found that "multiple possible cloud species can form on the dayside including MgSiO3 and SiO2," and that SiO2 in particular could play a role. SiO2 "could help to explain the high albedo required by the GCM to match the observed dayside emission," the researchers explain. Basically, these clouds are like a sunscreen, protecting the planet from intense heat.

These results are a confirmation of earlier work showing that, against all expectations, TOI-561 b has a significant atmosphere. This challenges the current understanding of how rocky super-Earth USPs evolve.

"Our discovery challenges current theories," Professor Dang said. "We would expect a heavily irradiated rocky planet like TOI-561 b to have lost its atmosphere billions of years ago. Instead, the evidence suggests it still has a significant atmosphere today."

"Atmospheric-escape theory suggests that strongly irradiated super-Earths should be devoid of any volatile envelopes," the researchers explain. "This is particularly astonishing given that the current JWST census of rocky exoplanets has been interpreted as suggestive of a population of bare-rock planets..."

They note that most of those planets are orbiting M-dwarfs, while TOI-561 b orbits a late G/early K dwarf, a more Sun-like star. These red dwarf planets are less irradiated than TOI-561 b, so it's strange and unexpected for it to host an atmosphere. "If rocky exoplanets at intermediate irradiation are completely desiccated, even-more irradiated exoplanets such as TOI-561 b should be, too," the researchers write.

The authors suggest a couple possible explanations for this. It's possible that TOI-561 b started out with a massive amount of atmospheric volatiles, meaning it began as a volatile-rich sub-Neptune and has had its atmosphere whittled away over time. It's also possible that we're seeing its secondary atmosphere, and that primary H/He atmospheres are more easily stripped away, while secondary atmospheres persist.

This graphic compares Earth, Super-Earths, Sub-Neptunes and Neptune. Some sub-Neptunes may have started out with more voluminous atmospheres like Neptune, but had them whittled away by their stars' powerful radiation. It's possible that's what TOI-561 b is one of these. Image Credit: By Keith Miller (Caltech/IPAC/SELab) - https://www.ipac.caltech.edu/news/finding-patterns-in-planets-researchers-explore-the-demographics-of-alien-worlds-in-k2-data, Public Domain, https://commons.wikimedia.org/w/index.php?curid=193443844

Migration could play a role, too. If the planet was further from its star while the star was younger and emitting more powerful radiation, then moved closer as the star calmed down, it's been exposed to less radiation overall, so hasn't lost its atmosphere. Lastly, in what they call option d, the authors write that "the originally accreted atmospheric volatiles were sequestered into the interior and later outgassed."

"Why TOI-561 b has an atmosphere may be due to a combination of more than one of these options," the authors write.

Whatever the case, these results are extremely interesting. The fourth possibility, that the exoplanet's original atmsophere was sequestered into its interior during its magma ocean phase and released later, is particularly interesting. "It is an exciting prospect that constraining the composition of a rocky planetâs secondary atmosphere could provide insights into its geochemical evolution (option d), such as the redox state of the magma ocean," the researchers write.

Whether one or more or all of the outlined explanations are responsible for TOI-561 b's atmosphere can hopefully be explained in future work. Part of that work will be comparing the exoplanet to bodies we're more familiar with.

âThe next big question we will seek to answer is whether TOI-561 bâs atmosphere resembles Venus or an extreme version of lo," Dang said. "Observing the planet at longer wavelengths with JWST could give us insights and help explain how a planet this hot has held on to an atmosphere for billions of years.â

#627. Giant Antarctic iceberg approaches remote island after three-year journey

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

A massive iceberg is slowing near an underwater shelf that has trapped and broken apart other Antarctic icebergs in the past.

An enormous iceberg that broke away from Antarctica more than three years ago has traveled thousands of miles across the Southern Ocean and is now approaching a remote island in the South Atlantic.

Known as Iceberg A-81, the massive block of ice has followed a route taken by many Antarctic icebergs. But its journey could soon face an unexpected obstacle.

A new satellite image captured by NASA’s Terra satellite on October 20, 2026, shows the iceberg nearing South Georgia, an island surrounded by underwater terrain that has trapped other giant icebergs in the past.

Iceberg A-81 began its journey in January 2023, when it separated from Antarctica’s Brunt Ice Shelf.

At the time, the iceberg covered approximately 600 square miles (1,550 square kilometers), making it an enormous floating mass of glacial ice.

Despite its size, the iceberg did not immediately head into the open ocean.

Instead, it spent more than three years moving through the Weddell Sea, where powerful ocean currents carried it along the Antarctic coastline.

The movement was largely driven by the Weddell Gyre, a massive clockwise circulation of ocean water that influences how icebergs travel through the region.

Some Antarctic icebergs remain close to their original ice shelves for decades. Others eventually escape into warmer northern waters, where they begin to melt and break apart.

A-81 followed the second path, although its departure was anything but quick.

By May 2026, Iceberg A-81 had reached the northern tip of the Antarctic Peninsula.

From there, it moved into the Scotia Sea, leaving the waters surrounding Antarctica and continuing toward the South Atlantic.

By late October, the iceberg had traveled approximately 1,400 miles (2,300 kilometers) north-northwest of the Brunt Ice Shelf.

Its destination was the vicinity of South Georgia, a remote island surrounded by cold, nutrient-rich waters.

The island lies along a route frequently traveled by icebergs escaping the Weddell Sea.

However, the underwater landscape surrounding South Georgia can dramatically change their movements.

As Iceberg A-81 approached South Georgia, its progress began to slow.

Satellite observations suggested that the iceberg was interacting with the edge of the island’s underwater continental shelf.

This shallow underwater terrain can interfere with the movement of enormous icebergs, particularly when their submerged portions extend deep below the ocean surface.

Jan Lieser of Australia’s Bureau of Meteorology and Christopher Shuman, a retired researcher from the University of Maryland, identified the apparent slowdown.

Their observations suggest that A-81 may have been bumping against the edge of the underwater shelf.

South Georgia has created similar obstacles for other giant icebergs.

In 2025, Iceberg A-23A became grounded on the island’s underwater shelf after its own long journey from Antarctica.

The enormous iceberg eventually broke into smaller pieces, demonstrating how the region can influence the final stages of an iceberg’s life.

Whether A-81 will experience a similar fate remains uncertain.

Although A-81 spent years traveling away from Antarctica, the process that created it began much earlier.

For approximately 35 years, a major fracture known as Chasm 1 remained relatively stable within the Brunt Ice Shelf.

Around 2012, however, the fracture began expanding again.

By 2019, it was growing rapidly enough that scientists expected a large section of the ice shelf to break away.

Yet the ice remained attached for several more years.

The final separation occurred in January 2023, when the expanding fracture produced Iceberg A-81.

This process, known as calving, occurs naturally when sections of floating ice shelves break away and become icebergs.

Although calving is a normal part of Antarctic ice dynamics, scientists closely monitor major fractures because they can reveal important changes in ice shelf stability.

The Brunt Ice Shelf has attracted considerable scientific attention because several enormous icebergs have separated from it in recent years.

In 2021, a fracture called the North Rift produced Iceberg A-74. Two years later, Chasm 1 released A-81.

Then, in 2024, another major fracture known as the Halloween Crack produced Iceberg A-83.

These events have significantly reshaped portions of the floating ice shelf.

The region is also home to the British Antarctic Survey’s Halley Research Station, which has supported research into Earth’s atmosphere, climate, and space environment since the 1950s.

Monitoring the surrounding ice is therefore important for understanding both natural changes and conditions affecting scientific operations.

A-81 is not the only iceberg moving northward from the Weddell Sea.

Iceberg A-74 has already broken apart, while A-83 remains intact and is following a similar route toward the north.

Several other icebergs also appear to be leaving the region, carried by the ocean currents that shape their movements.

Their journeys illustrate how Antarctic ice can travel enormous distances before eventually melting or fragmenting.

For A-81, the next chapter may depend on the underwater terrain surrounding South Georgia.

The iceberg has already survived more than three years at sea.

Now, the same ocean currents that carried it so far from Antarctica have brought it to a place where other giant icebergs have met their end.

NASA astronaut Jessica Meir took a tiny fingerboard for a spin on the space station and asked Tony Hawk for tips.
