#4186. What Should a Dog’s Name Sound Like?

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

We’re all holding onto something we’re sure we can’t give up on, but research shows that letting go can boost our happiness a lot more than we might think.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted October 27, 2026 | Reviewed by Michelle Quirk

Perhaps the most important sound that a dog can learn is its name. Our domestic dogs live in a sea of spoken human language from which they must pick out the sounds that are relevant to them. Thus, if you happen to say to your spouse or housemate, “Come over here and sit down,” how is your pet dog able to know that you haven’t just issued three commands aimed at them, namely: “Come!”; “Sit!”; and “Down!”? The answer is that, if a dog has been correctly trained, they understand that sounds that are relevant to them are usually preceded by their name. Thus, the specific canine syntax that the dog is looking for is “Lassie come!”; “Lassie sit!”; or “Lassie down!”

Some new research from Eötvös Loránd University in Hungary suggests that all dog names are not equal. Certain linguistic sounds seem to have the ability to capture canine attention better and allow them to pick out their name from the flow of language more easily.

Human speech is a continuous stream. For example, say:

out loud. Notice that there are no gaps between the written words, but as you speak these sounds, your brain adds the breaks between the words and syllables. This is a process known as speech segmentation, which is used to identify the boundaries between words or phonemes in continuous spoken language. In normal written text, we help the process by using spaces to separate words more easily and clearly.

Similar to that written example, most often human spoken speech is a continuous audio signal without specific pauses between the words. You can see this from the speech spectrogram below (where the sound frequencies are on the vertical axis and time is measured on the horizontal axis). This particular recording represents a measure of the sounds produced by a person speaking the phrase “I owe you.” Notice that although we would hear this as three distinct words, there are no explicit physical markers or gaps in the actual audio signal. The psychological question is “Which aspects of the stream of sound allow us to pick out the specific words?”

An important clue comes from the fact that words are made up of two major types of speech sounds, namely vowels and consonants. These are treated differently by the brain. Despite the fact that vowels are louder and more noticeable, you can say that consonants generally form the skeleton of words. So, if we have an uninterrupted stream of speech sounds, it is easier to detect specific words when we concentrate our attention on consonants. This strategy, called the “consonant bias,” is what human brains have adopted, and this mechanism is apparent even in infancy.

A recent report by a team of researchers led by Boglárka Morvai (a biologist) and Kinga Tóth (a psychologist), with the cognitive neuroscientist Attila Andics as the corresponding author, attempted to determine if dogs have a similar consonant bias when listening to human speech sounds. This study used 20 adults and their companion dogs. The participants (both human and dog) separately listened to a stream of speech made up of nonsense words while they were connected to an EEG monitor that measured the activity of their brains.

The signal the participants were listening to was a continuous flow of contrived three-syllable sounds. There were no pauses to indicate where one meaningless “word” ended and the next began. Three types of language streams were used. In one, consonant patterns formed the repeating three-syllable words while the vowels changed; in another, the vowels repeated and the consonants changed, while in the third there was no repetitive pattern.

The researchers were looking for a phenomenon called “neural synchronization,” which occurs when brain activity takes on the rhythm that we detect in the sounds we hear. The more clearly we detect the cadence or tempo of the words, the more distinctly that pattern appears in the electrical brain response.

The differences in responses to the speech signals were quite significant. In humans, the response to the consonant-patterned words was around 25 percent higher than to the vowel-based rhythm. Dogs showed a similar consonant bias, with their neural responses around 42 percent stronger for consonant-structured words compared to vowel-structured words.

An interesting additional finding was that human brains could also track words built from vowel patterns, but considerably less strongly than the consonant-based words. However, dog brains did not show a similar response to vowels. This was interpreted by the researchers as indicating that people may simply be better at detecting unexpected or atypical patterns of speech.

All of this brings us back to the matter of which sounds might be best for a dog’s name. It is important for the dog to be able to detect its name from whatever flow of speech sounds are going on around him. Taking the results of this new research at face value, it would seem to suggest that it would be more efficient and effective if you gave your dog a name with more consonant sounds in it than one with more vowels. Therefore, names like Ruby, Wendy, Merlin, or Barrett would appear to be preferable to names like Ava, Iris, Arlo, or Owen. At least such consonant-rich names would be easier for a dog to distinguish or detect when they are embedded in the ongoing stream of human speech. Of course, whether your dog actually responds to the commands that they hear associated with their name is not just a matter of whether they can hear and recognize what is said, but it is also a matter of how well you trained them.

Copyright SC Psychological Enterprises Ltd. May not be reprinted or reposted without permission.

Morvai, B., Tóth, K. G., Boros, M., Rácz, D. S., Iotchev, I., Szabó, K., & Andics, A. (2026). Neural evidence that dogs segment the speech they hear with a humanlike consonant bias. Science, 393(6818), 1354–1358.

#1125. Inside L’Oréal Paris’s Science-Forward Tinted Balm Foundation

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

The formula is designed to give mature skin its glow back.

If you’re over 40 and your current foundation is starting to settle into fine lines and wrinkles and make your skin look dry and dull, it’s time to rethink your approach. Instead of a thick formula that focuses on covering up, look to a buildable, hydrating option designed to bridge the gap between makeup and skin care.

L’Oréal Paris True Match Hyaluron Tinted Balm Foundation is one such innovation that was specifically crafted for mature skin and women who are demanding more from their beauty basics. “As skin matures, its biological and aesthetic needs evolve—dryness can become more visible, texture changes, and traditional makeup formulas are no longer flattering,” says Balanda Atis, L’Oréal Paris Assistant Vice President, Scientific Communications. “There are consumers who have had to compromise: Choose a skin care product that lacks coverage or wear a traditional foundation that masks the skin and accentuates fine lines and wrinkles. We wanted to eliminate that compromise entirely.”

In order to create a complexion product that works with mature skin instead of against it, the brand formulated the Tinted Balm Foundation with a base of 80 percent skin care ingredients.

Topping this list is moisture-magnet hyaluronic acid. Known for its ability to bind water to the skin, this naturally occurring humectant has been a staple in the skin care aisle for decades.

“To pack in all the amazing skin care benefits, our scientists had to do something completely innovative with the texture,” says Atis. “Our labs spent countless hours perfecting a creamy texture that melts on skin. It instantly delivers weightless comfort while offering completely customizable coverage, allowing the wearer to go from sheer and glowy to a buildable, radiant finish.”

Available in 12 shades and housed in a bag-friendly compact with a built-in mirror, the Tinted Balm Foundation features an antimicrobial applicator with a unique, tear-drop shape that allows for precision application in those tough-to-finesse areas around the nose and eyes.

From a shade range and ease-of-use perspective, the Tinted Balm Foundation has plenty going for it. But it’s what it delivers in the short- and long-term that separates it from the rest of the complexion pack.

The Tinted Balm Foundation melts onto skin, delivering instant hydration, a more visibly even skin tone, enhanced natural radiance, and a comfortable feel that lasts all day. A consumer study conducted by L’Oréal Paris found that “91 percent of Hyaluron Tinted Balm users said imperfections, including fine lines and wrinkles, looked minimized” after two weeks of use.

“It completely reimagines how we approach complexion products for mature skin—with its hyaluronic acid-infused formula, it doesn’t just provide the benefits of a foundation, it actively nurtures and seals in vital moisture all day long,” says Atis. “For L'Oréal Paris, it marks an important shift that moves away from heavy anti-aging camouflage and toward embracing a modern, skin-first approach that celebrates healthy, radiant skin.”

Formulated with skin care ingredients including hyaluronic acid, the Tinted Balm Foundation is designed to address the unique concerns of mature skin, such as dryness, texture changes, and the onset of fine lines and wrinkles.

Prep skin by cleansing, moisturizing, and applying SPF. Then, sweep the wide, rounded base of the teardrop applicator over the compact, and deposit balm directly on skin, targeting the larger areas of the face (i.e., cheeks and forehead). Next, use the tapered tip to apply balm around the nose and under the eyes. For a flawless, radiant finish, build coverage in light layers as needed.

#662. Fish are appearing where they’ve never been recorded before in Japan

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Scientists analyzing seawater DNA across Japan have uncovered surprising changes in where fish species are appearing, raising new questions for fisheries.

Go fishing off the Japanese coast today, and you might pull up something your grandfather never encountered. Fish typically found in warmer southern waters are turning up farther north.

The people whose livelihoods and cuisines depend on the sea are already noticing. The ocean is reshaping itself around them.

Yet, most of the world’s tracking tools have been too slow, too expensive, or too patchy to keep up.

A new study from the Okinawa Institute of Science and Technology (OIST) offers the most detailed picture yet of this shift.

Using environmental DNA, the researchers identified more than 100 fish species appearing further north than any previous record suggested.

The work is the largest marine eDNA study ever conducted in Japan. It draws on 2,672 water samples collected at 316 locations along the Japanese coastline between 2017 and 2024.

Environmental DNA, or eDNA, works on a simple premise. Every organism sheds genetic material into its surroundings – through skin, scales, mucus, feces.

Collect a sample of seawater, extract and sequence the DNA, then compare it against a reference database.

The result is a snapshot of the species whose genetic traces are present in the water.

This procedure uses a fraction of the time and cost of traditional survey methods.

Citizen science networks, fieldwork surveys, and specimen collection all have their place. Still, each comes with its own blind spots.

Observations can be difficult to verify, traditional surveys can be expensive, and predictions do not always reflect conditions in the water.

Japan is one of the few places where this approach could be applied at a national scale.

The method relies on MiFish primers, genetic tools developed by Japanese researchers to identify fish DNA in water samples. Extensive reference DNA databases help researchers match those sequences to individual species.

Study lead author Ayşe Haruka Oshima Açıkbaş is an OIST PhD graduate.

“Researchers have put tons of effort into sequencing the local marine fauna, increasing the coverage and accuracy of species detection within Japan,” Açıkbaş said.

The team combined nearly 1,000 newly collected eDNA samples with data from previous sampling efforts.

They built a seven-year dataset that allowed them to map fish distributions across Japan’s coastlines with unprecedented resolution.

Among the fish species detected, 126 appeared at latitudes farther north than previously recorded.

The Japanese scad (Decapterus maruadsi) and the blacktip grouper (Epinephelus fasciatus) are both important to local fisheries. Others carry ecological consequences that go beyond the catch.

The black rabbitfish (Siganus fuscescens), also appearing further north, is associated with “isoyake.” That’s a phenomenon in which grazing pressure destroys coastal seaweed beds, with cascading effects on other ecosystems.

“Fishermen across Japan are already finding differences in the types of fish they reel in compared to a few decades ago,” said senior author Timothy Ravasi, the head of the Marine Climate Change unit at OIST.

“This is a problem because the people in these areas may not be used to buying, cooking, or eating these new kinds of fish, and at the same time, they can’t make the dishes that might be culturally or historically significant because those fish aren’t there anymore.”

“And a sudden influx of new types of fish means you need new techniques, tools, and knowledge to catch them, which is especially hard on small, family-based fishing businesses.”

Japan’s combination of a highly developed marine science infrastructure, a dense network of coastal monitoring stations, and decades of fisheries data made this kind of study possible.

The eDNA technique scales well and the researchers expect the value of the dataset to compound as collection continues.

For now, the study provides something that policy discussions around fisheries management and coastal conservation have largely lacked: a fine-grained, empirically grounded map of where fish actually are, rather than where models predict they should be.

The 126 species detected beyond their previously recorded northern limits offer evidence of changes already underway in Japan’s seas, although individual detections do not necessarily establish permanent range shifts.

The authors expect these findings to shape future coastal and fisheries policy in Japan.

The longer-term goal is to move from tracking species presence to modeling the processes driving their movement.

That means understanding not just that fish DNA is appearing in new places, but why it ends up where it does, and where it is likely to turn up next.

As the database grows and predictive modeling catches up with the descriptive work, the technique could become a standard tool for monitoring range shifts in marine ecosystems far beyond Japan’s coastline.

The study is published in the journal Diversity and Distributions.

#444. Scientists Studied 23,000-Year-Old Human Footprints—and Reconstructed a Lost World Around Them

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

The footprints in what is now White Sands National Park were left when Earth’s environment was remarkably different, scientists learned.

The ancient human population that traversed what is now White Sands National Park in New Mexico encountered a shockingly different environment when they were there between 23,500 and 21,700 years ago.

Back in the present, the same research team that pioneered the dating of preserved footprints in the sand in 2021 took the study a step further, analyzing the sediment surrounding the prints to discover a rapidly changing environment, one the team called “an abrupt environmental change at White Sands,” in a new study published in Science Advances.

By analyzing 20 stratigraphic horizons with in situ human footprints, megafaunal trackways, and linear features created by historic wheels, the team used radiocarbon dating of conifer pollen, combined with ages of aquatic seeds, to refine their dating timeline. Researchers believe the evidence indicates a “complete overturning of the environment from a perennial lake to a playa [dry lake bed]” from the height of the ice age.

“This well-dated and highly resolved paleoclimate record,” the study authors wrote, “provides compelling evidence that humans in North America experienced marked environmental changes during the Last Glacial Maximum.”

The researchers now believe that their data, published in both the new Science Advances study on climate and a Fossil Studies study on the footprint record, not only strengthens the dating of the footprints but provides snapshots of life around the lake through information on temperature and rainfall. “Together, these allowed us to reconstruct the landscape through which people were walking,” researchers Matthew Bennett and Sally Reynolds wrote in a post published by The Conversation.

Lake Otero occupied much of the basin at the start of the timeline, with the mountains around it full of conifer forests and temperatures about 4 degrees Celsius colder than today. Then temperatures began to rise; the lake shrunk, forests declined, and oak and sagebrush crept in. “As a result of this warming, the basin became progressively drier as evaporation increased,” they wrote. “Superimposed upon this longer-term change were much sharper climate swings.”

A drought impacted things heavily and “this dry period coincides with a rapid climatic change recorded thousands of miles away in Greenland ice cores.” The earliest footprints occur around the climatic transition, with footprints continuing at intervals as the lake retreated. “Our new evidence therefore shows that people visited this landscape repeatedly while experiencing substantial environmental change,” the team wrote.

The researchers used agent-based computer modeling to explore what the footprints represent in human movement, revealing complex behavior patterns. “Our virtual world consisted of people moving around a fluctuating lake,” the team wrote. “When they crossed mud, they left footprints. Those footprints gradually disappeared unless changing water levels crated the right conditions for them to be buried and preserved.”

In other words, sometimes human activity around the lake left virtually no fossil record, and other times, plenty of preserved footprints remained. “The footprint surfaces occur as irregular pulses that tend to cluster around periods of climatic and hydrological change—just the pattern predicted by the model,” the team wrote.

That data now gives a much fuller view of ancient human history and some of the oldest in North America.

Tim Newcomb is a journalist based in the Pacific Northwest. He covers stadiums, sneakers, gear, infrastructure, and more for a variety of publications, including Popular Mechanics. His favorite interviews have included sit-downs with Roger Federer in Switzerland, Kobe Bryant in Los Angeles, and Tinker Hatfield in Portland.

#698. Hubble Will Give the Roman Space Telescope a Helping Hand

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

The Nancy Grace Roman Space Telescope (NGRST) is on its way to its station at the SunâEarth L2 point, where it will begin its projected 5-year mission. It has several goals, including measuring dark energy's effect on the Universe, the growth of structure, and general relativity's consistency over time. Gravitational lensing plays a big role in its work.

There are two kinds of gravitational lensing, strong and weak. In strong gravitational lensing, a massive foreground object like a galaxy cluster lines up with a background object and produces magnified and distorted images of the background object, making the background object open to observations. It can produce Einstein rings, arcs, and multiple images of the background object.

These distortions let scientists measure the mass of the foreground galaxies. Since visible matter isn't enough to cause the distortion on its own, scientists can do some fancy math to calculate the amount of dark matter that must be present. That, in turn, will help them understand dark energy, the growth of structure, and general relativity.

In this image, the red foreground galaxy produced two arcs of light that are distorted, multiplied images of a background galaxy; a large arc on the top and a smaller one on the bottom. With images like this, astronomers can measure the total mass in the foreground galaxy and the degree of magnification of the background galaxy. By comparing the two, they can determine the amount of dark matter present in the foreground galaxy. Image Credit: NASA, ESA, and X. Huang (University of San Francisco); Processing: Gladys Kober (NASA/Catholic University of America)

The Hubble is the Roman's older brother, in a way, and it works differently. It has something to offer the NGRST's mission. The Roman sees mostly in infrared, with a little bit of visible light. The Hubble sees in mostly visible and UV light, with a little infrared. But the pair of telescopes differ in another important way, too. While the Hubble can examine individual objects, that's not how the Roman works. It has a much larger field of view.

This image compares the Hubble's small, focused field of view and the Roman's much larger one. The difference is stark, but it lets Hubble do preparatory work that will help the Roman's weak gravitational lensing observations. Image Credit: Background image: Digitized Sky Survey and R. Gendler; Moon image: NASAâs Goddard Space Flight Center and Arizona State University

That means that while Hubble can examine individual gravitational lenses, the NGRST cannot. That's because the Roman is a wide-field survey telescope. Here's how the Hubble will help its more modern counterpart.

The Hubble examines a single strong gravitational lens at a time. Astronomers can use Hubble's detailed observations to understand the lenses better. That model will be used to help make sense of the Roman's gravitational lenses.

It comes down to what's called the point-spread function. Telescopes don't image a point source as a point. The source produces a pattern across the detector, and the PSF pattern always has aberrations, diffraction spikes, and other problems. Since the Hubble looks at each gravitational lens separately on a single detector, the PSF is easier to understand and account for.

The problem the Roman will have also concerns the point spread function. (If there's an astronomy paper that doesn't mention PSF, Universe Today hasn't seen it.) The Roman will detect most of its gravitational lenses in its High Latitude Wide Area Survey (HLWAS). The HLWAS will cover about 5,000 degrees2 of the sky (about 12% of the sky).

The Roman's High-Latitude Wide-Area Survey should detect about 160,000 gravitational lenses. But only about 500 of them are predicted to be good for follow-up observations that will reveal their substructure. Image Credit: NASA's Goddard Space Flight Center

That means Roman's images are 200 times larger than Hubble's, and spread across multiple detectors, each with a different focal-plane position. In a 2025 paperr, researchers forecast the number of strong gravitational lense the NGRST will detect. "For a fiducial case of single 146-second exposures, we predict around 160,000 detectable strong lenses in the HLWAS, of which about 500 will have sufficient signal-to-noise to be amenable to detailed substructure characterization," they wrote. The HLWAS is expected to detect 15 strong lenses per exposure.

That number, 15 strong lenses per exposure, makes understanding the PSF more difficult. Its images are detected across multiple detectors, each with a different focal plane. So it's harder for the Roman to deal with the PSF and do its job: measuring dark matter.

Astronomers are preparing for the Roman's gravitational lens detections by building a computer model with Hubble's observations. Hubble's PSFs are being used to help decipher the Roman's PSFs.

Hubble will help in another way, too, by performing follow up imaging of the lenses that the NGRST discovers.

This shows that even though the Hubble is technologically outclassed by newer telescopes like the JWST and the NGRST, it still plays an important role. It is, after all, still a powerful space telescope. It's as if the venerable space telescope is passing the baton to a new generation, even though it's still part of the team.

But while the Roman's mission is just about to begin, the Hubble Space Telescope is approaching its finish line. The Hubble is gradually losing its stabilizing gyroscopes, and with no repair mission planned, the telescope will probably die with its last gyroscope.

Nobody can say for sure when that will happen, but up until then, it still has important work to do by supporting its more modern counterparts.

#452. Archaeologists Found a 3,200-Year-Old Warhorse—Buried as If It Were Still Charging Into Battle

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Egyptians valued their beasts of battle so much that they gave them ceremonial burials and posed them just right for the afterlife.

At a site now known as Tell Al-Abquain in Egypt’s Beheira Governorate, ruins of mud brick houses and other remnants from 3,200 years ago are scattered around an area that used to be an ancient fortress. In its day, it appears to have been a flourishing society. Along with weapons and barracks for the soldiers, archaeologists have uncovered what is left of grain silos, wells, and pottery ovens that also baked bread, a reminder of what daily life was like during the New Kingdom. Something unexpected had also been buried among these artifacts.

As archaeologists were excavating the fortress, they unearthed the intact skeleton of what appeared to be a warhorse. Whomever the young male horse had belonged to must have been a military official of high rank, as the horse had been buried with honor, still wearing its alabaster and limestone chariot harness. Even in death, the horse had been positioned as if it was galloping into battle, further indicating elaborate funeral rites at the time. Most likely used to pull war chariots similar to those interred with Tutankhamun, who ruled as pharaoh during the same time period, the horse is thought to have been bred for fighting. Preliminary studies led by Mohamed Abdel-Badie, head of the Egyptian Antiquities Sector at the Supreme Council of Antiquities, found it to be a medium breed whose strengths were the speed and agility needed for war.

“Among the most significant findings of the excavations was the complete animal burial of a young male horse,” the Egyptian Ministry of Tourism and Antiquities said in a recent press release, adding that “This breed is likely used for training, military activities, or pulling chariots during the New Kingdom. The horse's complete body position, the direction of its head, and the regularity of the burial suggest a deliberate and organized burial, likely a ceremonial one.”

Who the horse belonged to is unknown. Thought to have crossed over into Egypt form the Levant with the Hyksos at the dawn of the New Kingdom, domestic horses were usually a luxury reserved for pulling chariots or carrying pharaohs. Most of what is known about the Egyptians’ relationship with horses is depicted on stelae, tomb murals, and temple wall hieroglyphics, but few actual remains of warhorses have been found. Most horses that appear in temple and tomb murals are decorated with military regalia and as they pull chariots carrying archers. These chariots were modified versions of heavier Levantine models. With the advantage of chariots that were lighter and faster than their predecessors, there are few existing images of riders, though some New Kingdom depictions that suggest riding survive.

Tell Al-Abquain arose to protect Egypt’s western border as a defense from the Libyans and Sea Peoples. This is the same fortress that revealed a bronze sword inscribed with the cartouche of legendary pharaoh Ramesses II. Faience scarabs and a bronze ring, engraved with the names of deities, along with necklaces strung with beads of faience and carnelian in the shape of pomegranate blossoms, may have also belonged to him. Horses often accompanied royals and became a symbol of royal power. In the retelling of the Battle of Kadesh, in which Ramesses II prevailed against the Hittites, the names of his horses are mentioned: “Victory-in-Thebes” was named for the capital city of Egypt then, and “Mut is content” revered the mother goddess Mut. This is just one example of how highly regarded horses were in Egypt.

Ramesses himself is said to have had 24,000 horses in his army. Other kings such as Thutmose III and Amenhotep II are pictured riding gloriously into battle, often compared to gods, and some, such as Amenhotep II and Tutankhamun were buried with their chariots. While they were associated with royalty, especially in royal propaganda that emphasized the king as a fierce warrior, horses were not exclusive. New Kingdom elites such as the royal family and high officials also owned horses and chariots. One relief shows the sons of Ramesses II riding, and horses in another transport Queen Nefertiti and the royal princesses of Amarna. Some officials were given titles such as “Master of Horses” or “Stable Master” to emphasize their rank. The burial of this warhorse is a reminder of how valued the animals were in Egyptain society.

Also discovered at the site were more artifacts to add to the trove that had been exposed in the last excavation—jewelry, amulets, looms, and pottery vessels in the image of everything from lotus flowers to fish and baboons. Some were covered in sculpted crocodile scales. The archaeological team even came upon kernels of unhusked grain, which had stayed intact for millennia in the shadows of crop warehouses. Fragments of limestone stelae also memorialized events and royal figures. Carved into one is a prisoner of war, possibly a Libyan, bound and bent in submission. The others glorify Ramses II with his multiple names and liken his son Merneptah to the formidable god Seth.

“This discovery adds a new dimension to understanding the system of military fortifications established by the ancient Egyptians to protect the country's borders,” stated Sherif Fathy, Minister of Tourism and Antiquities. “The residential structures, storage facilities, and diverse artifacts uncovered by the mission provide a comprehensive picture that extends beyond the military aspect of the fortress.”

#610. Scientists Discover Mysterious 150,000-Year-Old Bone From an Extinct Human Relative in Chinese Cave

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

A search through more than 60,000 bone fragments uncovered the first confirmed Denisovan radius, a forearm bone offering rare clues to how these ancient human relatives were built.

A forearm fragment from a cave in southwest China combines features resembling those of Neanderthals and modern humans, yet it belonged to neither. Proteins preserved in the bone identify its owner as Denisovan, part of an ancient human group recognized through genetic evidence.

The fragment is the first, and so far only, confirmed Denisovan radius, one of the two long bones in the forearm. Denisovans are thought to have lived across much of Asia, but their fossil record is sparse, particularly for bones below the skull. That scarcity has left researchers with little evidence of the physical traits that helped them adapt to different environments.

The radius is among five remains from Bianfu Cave in Yunnan Province that a new study identified as Denisovan. Dating to the late Middle Pleistocene, the fossils fill a longstanding gap in the group’s known distribution in southwest China. Professor Qiaomei Fu of the Institute of Vertebrate Paleontology and Paleoanthropology (IVPP) of the Chinese Academy of Sciences (CAS) led the research in collaboration with multiple institutions. The findings were published in Nature on September 9.

Cataloged as BFD771, the radius fragment preserves the end nearest the elbow. Its large dimensions at that end resemble Neanderthal radii, as does the likely inward orientation of its radial tuberosity, the bony projection where the biceps muscle attaches. Its overall external shape, however, is closer to that of modern humans. So is the geometry of a cross section through the middle of its neck, the narrowed region near the elbow end. The researchers interpret this combination as evidence of distinctive mechanical demands and behavioral adaptations in Denisovans, differing from those inferred for Neanderthals and modern humans.

Two skull fragments, BFD767 and BFD769, provided another anatomical comparison. Both are pieces of parietal bones, which form part of the top and sides of the skull. Their skull vault thickness patterns most closely resemble those of Homo heidelbergensis and late Middle Pleistocene archaic Homo from East Asia.

Finding these bones required narrowing a collection of more than 60,000 fragments. Researchers increasingly use Zooarchaeology by Mass Spectrometry, or ZooMS, to screen large fossil collections by examining protein signatures. The technique requires minimally invasive sampling and is simple, fast, and relatively inexpensive. Even so, hominin fossil recovery rates at sites such as Denisova Cave and Baishiya Karst Cave have been just 0.1%. Applying ZooMS to every fragment from Bianfu Cave would have been costly and inefficient.

The team instead developed a “morphological pre-screening + ZooMS identification” strategy, first examining bone shapes to select 22 potential hominin remains. ZooMS then confirmed that the two parietal fragments and the radius fragment belonged to hominins, the group that includes humans and our extinct close relatives. The combined approach substantially improved screening efficiency and provides a repeatable method for searching other Paleolithic sites with abundant bone fragments.

BFD767 and BFD771 came from Layer 7, dated to approximately 148,000 to 134,000 years ago. That layer also contained four hominin teeth, two of which the researchers included in the study: a lower fourth premolar, YHB3518, and a lower second molar, YHB3075. The other skull fragment, BFD769, came from the older Layer 9, dated to approximately 167,000 to 150,000 years ago.

Identifying the bones as hominin remains did not reveal which ancient human group they belonged to. To find out, researchers analyzed proteins preserved in three bones and two teeth, recovering 6 to 16 proteins from each specimen. Protein fragments in the tooth enamel also indicated that both teeth belonged to males.

All five fossils carried the Denisovan-specific protein variant COL1A2 R996K. Additional variants in the teeth supported this identification and helped distinguish the fossils from other ancient human groups, including Homo erectus.

The researchers also constructed evolutionary trees using the recovered protein sequences. All five specimens clustered with the Denisovan specimen Denisova 3, with 100% posterior probability, providing strong support for their identification.

These findings make Bianfu Cave the richest known source of Denisovan fossils outside Denisova Cave. Dating to a glacial period known as Marine Isotope Stage 6, the remains suggest that the Yunnan-Guizhou Plateau’s relatively warm climate and abundant food resources may have provided a favorable habitat for Denisovans.

Reference: “Ancient proteins identify various Denisovan remains from Southwest China” by Huiyun Rao, Song Xing, Qijun Ruan, Fan Bai, Yuhao Zhao, Zhongyou Wu, Siyuan Tang, E. Andrew Bennett, Xiaotian Feng, Sungui Lin, Qingfeng Shao, Xiong Yang, Zitao Li, Kai Zhou, Feng Liu and Qiaomei Fu, 9 September 2026, Nature. DOI: 10.1038/s41586-026-10976-9

This work was supported by the National Key R&D Program of China (2023YFF0905700), the Archaeological Talent Promotion Program of China (2024-278), the National Natural Science Foundation of China (L2424324), the New Cornerstone Science Foundation and the Open Research Fund of TPESER (TPESER202210).

#3630. RAMSA Unveils Plans for New Welcome Hall at Chicago’s Griffin Museum of Science and Industry

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

RAMSA (Robert A.M. Stern Architects) has unveiled the design for a new Welcome Hall at the Kenneth C. Griffin Museum of Science and Industry (Griffin MSI) in Chicago, Illinois. The project involves reopening the building's historic North Portico as a year-round entrance and introducing a new glazed volume connecting the portico to the museum's exhibition spaces. The proposal is part of a master plan completed by RAMSA in 2024 that examines the use of the museum's existing exhibition and public areas.

RAMSA (Robert A.M. Stern Architects) has unveiled the design for a new Welcome Hall at the Kenneth C. Griffin Museum of Science and Industry (Griffin MSI) in Chicago, Illinois. The project involves reopening the building's historic North Portico as a year-round entrance and introducing a new glazed volume connecting the portico to the museum's exhibition spaces. The proposal is part of a master plan completed by RAMSA in 2024 that examines the use of the museum's existing exhibition and public areas.

The museum's current entrance route passes through largely subterranean spaces and requires visitors to navigate more than 100 stairs and two elevators to reach the exhibition areas. According to the project team, this journey takes approximately 18 to 25 minutes. The proposed entrance is expected to reduce the arrival time to four to seven minutes by establishing a direct route at ground level, without stairs or elevators along the main path.

The design takes as its starting point the building's original role as the Palace of Fine Arts during the 1893 World's Columbian Exposition. At the time, the North Portico functioned as an entrance in direct relation to the surrounding landscape and public spaces. The new Welcome Hall is positioned as an extension of the North Lawn and remains below the historic plinth across much of its length. Its curved form and glazed walls are intended to maintain views toward the existing portico and its Ionic columns. The proposal also includes Science Gardens, accessible lawns, restrooms, and other amenities that will be available without museum admission. These facilities will provide public access to parts of the museum grounds independently of its exhibition spaces.

#492. Do spiders dream? Scientists just found a surprising clue

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Scientists at the University of Bonn have discovered that spiders experience distinct stages of sleep, including a REM-like state that shares surprising similarities with human sleep. During this stage, the spiders move their eyes and twitch their muscles, behaviors also associated with REM (rapid eye movement) sleep in humans and other vertebrates.

The findings provide the first comprehensive experimental confirmation that spiders experience genuine, regulated sleep rather than simply remaining inactive. They also raise intriguing questions about how sleep evolved and whether animals with very different nervous systems might share some of its fundamental features.

Researchers at the University of Bonn's Institute of Organismic Biology made the discovery while studying the Australian jumping spider Portia fimbriata, a small but remarkably sophisticated predator.

A Tiny Spider With Remarkable Hunting Skills

The Australian jumping spider Portia fimbriata is only about the size of a one-euro coin when fully grown, but its hunting abilities make it particularly interesting to scientists.

Unlike many spiders that primarily capture insects, members of the genus Portia specialize in hunting other spiders. They use a variety of sophisticated strategies to catch their prey, including imitating the vibrations made by struggling insects trapped in spiderwebs.

These deceptive signals can lure web-building spiders toward what appears to be an easy meal, only for them to encounter the hunting Portia instead.

Such complex behaviors suggest that these spiders have unusual learning abilities, making them valuable subjects for investigating the relationship between sleep, nervous system function, and cognition.

Scientists Discover Two Distinct Stages of Spider Sleep

Although sleep has been extensively studied in humans and other vertebrates, scientists still know relatively little about how it works in animals with very different nervous systems.

To investigate whether spiders experience genuine sleep and whether that sleep includes different stages, the researchers closely monitored Portia fimbriata over several days and nights.

They discovered that the spiders exhibit two clearly distinguishable sleep stages. One of these resembles REM sleep, the phase in humans associated with rapid eye movements, muscle activity, and vivid dreaming.

During this REM-like stage, the spiders displayed characteristic eye movements and muscle twitches. These observations suggest that some features of sleep previously studied mainly in vertebrates may also occur in spiders.

The researchers emphasize that identifying REM-like movements does not establish whether spiders dream. However, the discovery provides important evidence that sleep in these animals is more complex than previously understood.

How Researchers Confirmed That Spiders Really Sleep

To study the spiders' sleeping behavior, the team placed individual Portia fimbriata spiders in plastic dishes measuring 12 centimeters in diameter. They monitored the animals under different lighting conditions to examine their daily activity and rest cycles.

Infrared cameras allowed the researchers to observe the spiders throughout the night without relying on visible light. Detailed recordings captured their sleeping positions, periods of quiet rest, and episodes of REM-like movement.

The team also conducted experiments to determine whether the spiders were genuinely asleep rather than simply sitting motionless.

Using sound stimuli, the researchers measured how quickly the spiders responded to disturbances. These tests helped distinguish sleep from ordinary inactivity.

Another important part of the investigation involved sleep deprivation. The researchers examined whether the spiders displayed "sleep rebound," meaning they compensated for interrupted sleep by increasing their subsequent sleep.

Together, the behavioral observations and experimental tests provided strong evidence that Portia fimbriata experiences regulated sleep with distinct stages.

Studying Spider Sleep Presented Unusual Challenges

Determining whether a spider is truly asleep is considerably more complicated than observing a sleeping human.

Because spiders sense their surroundings differently from people, the researchers first had to identify a sound stimulus that would produce a consistent response during their experiments.

They also needed to avoid assuming that the observed eye movements and muscle twitches necessarily represented the same processes seen during human REM sleep.

One major limitation was that the researchers could not measure the spiders' brain activity. Instead, they relied entirely on carefully documented behavior to identify different sleep states.

This required extensive high-resolution video recordings collected over many days and nights, producing enormous amounts of data for analysis.

Despite these obstacles, the combination of behavioral monitoring, response testing, and sleep disruption experiments allowed the researchers to build a detailed picture of how these spiders sleep.

What Spider Sleep Could Reveal About Evolution

The discovery has implications that extend well beyond spiders.

Most scientific knowledge about sleep comes from studying vertebrates, particularly mammals and birds. Finding comparable sleep features in animals with radically different nervous systems could help researchers understand when and why different types of sleep evolved.

The results also open new opportunities in behavioral science, neuroscience, and chronobiology, the study of biological rhythms.

By investigating sleep in a wider variety of animals, scientists hope to identify features that are shared across species and uncover the fundamental biological functions that sleep may have served early in evolutionary history.

The researchers argue that future investigations should move beyond traditionally studied animals and include species with unusual adaptations or much simpler nervous systems.

Such comparisons could reveal whether certain sleep mechanisms are widespread throughout the animal kingdom or developed independently in different evolutionary lineages.

Even after decades of intensive research, many basic questions about sleep remain unanswered, including important mysteries surrounding human sleep.

Could Spiders Dream? Scientists Have More Questions

The University of Bonn team is now expanding its research to investigate how sleep, particularly REM-like sleep, varies across different spider species.

Scientists are also comparing spiders sleeping in natural environments with those observed in laboratories to determine how surroundings influence their behavior.

Another major goal is to investigate the relationship between sleep and cognitive abilities. Researchers want to understand what happens inside the brains of spiders and insects during sleep, and whether different sleep stages contribute to learning and other mental processes.

Environmental changes are also becoming an important focus. The team plans to examine how climate change and human activities, especially artificial light at night (ALAN), may interfere with the sleep patterns of spiders and other arthropods.

For now, the findings establish that even a tiny jumping spider can experience a surprisingly complex form of sleep. Whether spiders actually dream remains unknown, but their nighttime behavior is offering scientists new clues about one of biology's most enduring mysteries.

Materials provided by University of Bonn. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#712. Asymmetric space waves map pressure on Earth's magnetic shield

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

The Earthâs magnetic field is responsible for shielding us from harmful space weather, including solar radiation from the Sun but also cosmic rays originating from outside our solar system. Cosmic rays can include deep space supernovas, so the magnetic field takes the majority of the brunt from solar radiation. Without our magnetic field, the solar wind would slowly strip away our atmosphere, making life on our small, blue world impossible to exist. But where is the magnetic field doing the heaviest lifting regarding how it filters out incoming space radiation?

Now, a team of researchers from the United States and Finland might be one step closer to solving this conundrum, as they discuss new evidence that certain regions of the Earthâs magnetic field experiences greater levels of stress from space weather compared to other regions. These findings were revealed in a recent study published in Geophysical Research Letters and holds the potential to help scientists not only better understand the interaction between space weather and Earthâs magnetic field, but magnetic fields on other worlds, too.

For the study, the researchers used a series of computer models to simulate how the interaction between the Earthâs magnetic field and solar wind produces a longstanding phenomenon called the Kelvin-Helmholtz instability (KHI), which are wave-like patterns produced at the boundary of the magnetic field when the solar wind strikes it. The solar wind consists of high-energy particles from the Sun and comprises the brunt of the energy impact our magnetic field shields us from. The primary motivation of this study comes from a continuing drive to understand space weather and specifically develop better space weather prediction models.

In the end, the researchers found that the KHI waves exhibit asymmetric, or non-mirror-like behavior, at certain regions of the Earthâs magnetic field. Essentially, the wave activity doesnât match along the northern and southern hemispheres, indicating the magnetic field experiences different levels of impact and interaction from the incoming solar wind. The researchers note this stems from several reasons, including whether itâs the dawn or dusk side of the planet, winter and summer hemispheres, and the magnetic fieldâs orientation. For example, the researchers found that the winter hemisphere experienced the strongest wave activity, with the solar windâs magnetic field determining if the dawn or dusk side experiences stronger activity.

âThis finding grew out of a long process of thinking through what I was seeing,â said Dr. Lauri Holappa, who is an Academy Research Fellow at the University of Oulu and lead author of the study. âIn 2020, when I was visiting NASA as a postdoctoral researcher, I noticed unusual simulation images of the magnetosphere. I later realized that they showed KelvinâHelmholtz waves at the edge of the magnetosphere, and that the different behavior of the waves on different sides was part of the same phenomenon.â

Studying space weather, and specifically the solar wind, has become essential in recent decades to safeguard satellites and electronic ground stations. However, scientists have actually been studying space weather since the 1600s with Galileo Galilei using his new telescope to observe sunspots. However, it wasnât until 1843 that the German astronomer Heinrich Schwabe discovered the now-well-known 11-year cycle of the Sunâs activity. This theoretical research continued until the Space Age kicked off in the mid-20th century with satellites being used to obtain direct measurements of the solar wind, with the term âspace weatherâ being coined sometime in the 1990s.

What new insights into Earthâs magnetic field interacting with the solar wind will researchers make in the coming years and decades? Only time will tell, and this is why we science!

#413. A-81 Reaches South Georgia

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

The Antarctic iceberg made a relatively quick exit from the Weddell Sea, only to slow at the edge of a remote island’s underwater shelf.

Some Antarctic icebergs linger close to home for decades, running aground near the ice shelves that spawned them or circling partway around the continent on coastal currents. Others, like Iceberg A-81, make a faster exit, heading north out of the Weddell Sea along a route well-traveled by the region’s bergs.

Fast is relative, though. After breaking from the Brunt Ice Shelf in January 2023, the roughly 1,550-square-kilometer (600-square-mile) berg spent more than three years in the Weddell Sea, carried along the coast by the clockwise-flowing Weddell Gyre. By May 2026, it reached the tip of the Antarctic Peninsula. It then moved north into the Scotia Sea, and by late October 2026, it approached the remote island of South Georgia in the South Atlantic Ocean, 2,300 kilometers (1,400 miles) north-northwest of the Brunt Ice Shelf.

This image, acquired by the MODIS (Moderate Resolution Imaging Spectroradiometer) on NASA’s Terra satellite, shows the berg on October 20. In the days before, its progress slowed noticeably, and it appeared to be bumping along the edge of the island’s underwater shelf, according to Jan Lieser of Australia’s Bureau of Meteorology and Christopher Shuman of the University of Maryland (retired). South Georgia has slowed and even stopped icebergs before. In 2025, for instance, Iceberg A-23A ran aground on the shelf and later broke apart.

Though Iceberg A-81 was a steadfast traveler once it broke free, it took its time in becoming an iceberg in the first place. For about 35 years, one of the Brunt Ice Shelf’s rifts known as Chasm 1 was relatively stable. Then, around 2012, it began growing again, and by 2019, it was advancing quickly enough that a break seemed imminent. But the floating glacial ice held on for several more years until finally, in January 2023, the berg let go.

Calving is a normal part of the life cycle of ice shelves, but scientists keep a close eye on Brunt, which has recently shed several large icebergs in quick succession and is home to the British Antarctic Survey’s Halley Research Station, a major base for Earth, atmospheric, and space science research since the 1950s. Besides A-81, the ice shelf calved Iceberg A-74 from the North Rift in 2021 and Iceberg A-83 from the Halloween Crack in 2024. A-74 has since broken up, while A-83 remains intact and is trailing A-81 northward, along with a handful of other icebergs that appear to be clearing out of the Weddell Sea.

NASA Earth Observatory image by Michala Garrison, using MODIS data from NASA EOSDIS LANCE and GIBS/Worldview. Story by Kathryn Hansen.

#718. A Brief-ish History of METI. Part I: Early Ideas and Experiments

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Long before modern astronomy, humans looked to the stars and were absolutely astounded by what they saw. Even by the most ancient reckoning, the night sky offered enough to inspire awe and wonder, make us feel insignificant, fire our imaginations, and foster hope and a sense of connection. Yet, for as long as humans have surveyed the heavens, they have seen no indication that life as we know it exists elsewhere - what has come to be known as the "Great Silence."

For many, the disparity between the assumed prevalence of life in the Universe and the lack of evidence for it raises a troubling incongruity often erroneously referred to as "Fermi's Paradox" (in truth, it was neither Fermi's nor a Paradox). It seems like a valid question when you consider the facts. Given the age, size, and density of the known Universe, shouldn't we have found unambiguous evidence of extraterrestrial life (and intelligence) by now?

Sixty years after the first Search for Extraterrestrial Intelligence (SETI) experiment (Project Ozma), scientists are still asking the question: "Are we alone in the Universe?" In addition to listening to the cosmos for radio signals and other signs of technological activity ("technosignatures"), efforts have been mounted to send messages to space for the purpose of making contact with an extraterrestrial intelligence (ETI).

This practice has come to be known as Messaging Extraterrestrial Intelligence (METI) to distinguish it from its more "passive" SETI cousin. Previously, we chronicled the history of SETI by examining some of its greatest milestones. Today, we will begin looking at the brief(-ish) history of METI and how it came to be.

As Universe Today's own Paul Patton addressed, Fermi's Paradox is named for famed Italian-American physicist Enrico Fermi. In 1950, during a lunchtime conversation at the Los Alamos National Laboratory, where he had worked a few years prior on the Manhattan Project, Fermi and his colleagues were discussing the recent spate of UFO sightings. It was here that Fermi posed his big challenge, saying out of the blue: "Where is everybody?"

After some quick calculations, Fermi and his colleagues concluded that, if interstellar travel were feasible and desirable, Earth should have been visited several times already. This led to the Hart-Tipler Conjecture, named after physicists Michael Hart and Frank Tipler. In a series of papers that appeared between 1975 and 1982, they argued that this "paradox" can be resolved only by asserting that extraterrestrial life does not exist.

Since then, many scientists (including Carl Sagan) have challenged this logic, arguing that there are innumerable reasons why humans have not heard from intelligent life yet (yet being the key word). Or as Sagan and William Newman famously put it: "the absence of evidence is not evidence of absence."

From this, the modern definition of the Fermi Paradox emerged. It states that the disparity between the assumed prevalence of life in the Universe and the lack of evidence for it can be resolved by one of two postulates: either extraterrestrials do not exist, or special circumstances exist that prevent us from detecting them. That certainly makes sense when you consider the observable facts:

Before we get into the history of this discipline, a little note on nomenclature is necessary. Traditionally, scientists made no distinction between SETI and METI, and pursued both projects concurrently. For instance, Cornell astrophysicist Dr. Frank Drake led the first SETI experiment (Project Ozma) in 1960, and was also vital to the first true METI project roughly fourteen years later (the Arecibo Message). Similarly, famed science communicator Carl Sagan, who became integral to early SETI efforts, later collaborated with Drake to create the Arecibo Message, the Pioneer Plaques, and the Voyager Golden Records.

The term "Active SETI" didn't emerge until 2005 to describe sending messages into space intended for extraterrestrial recipients. The following year, Russian scientist Alexander Zaitsev coined the term "Messaging Extraterrestrial Intelligence" to differentiate SETI and METI. As he wrote in the proposal paper, released in 2006:

Those who propose, or oppose, sending Messages to Extra-Terrestrial Intelligence (METI) must contemplate the Hamlet-like question: "To send or not to send?" The science known as SETI deals with searching for messages from aliens. METI science deals with the creation of messages to aliens. Thus, SETI and METI proponents have quite different perspectives.

SETI scientists are in a position to address only the local question "does Active SETI make sense?â In other words, would it be reasonable, for SETI success, to transmit with the object of attracting ETI's attention? In contrast to Active SETI, METI pursues not a local, but a more global purpose â to overcome the Great Silence in the Universe, bringing to our extraterrestrial neighbors the long-expected annunciation "You are not alone!"

In a follow-up paper released in 2011, "Rationale for METI," Zaitsev laid out METI's mission statement. In it, he quoted famed American radar astronomer Steven Ostro's unpublished paper, which proposed creating a powerful omnidirectional radio beacon on the far side of the Moon. As Oslo said in his paper, "We might conclude that it is better to give than to receive, and that the war on Great Silence must begin at home."

Among METI's benefits, Zaitsev highlighted what he coined the "SETI Paradox": advanced civilizations hear only silence from space because everyone is listening and no one is transmitting. He also noted that previous SETI programs spent hundreds of times longer listening to space than METI experiments did transmitting. But most important were the possible benefits interstellar communication could bring, something that was being avoided with passive SETI efforts. This, he wrote, could very well lead to civilizations living in isolation and eventually going extinct:

Isolationism as a possible cause of extinction of civilizations I do not know definitely, but it seems to me that Sebastian von Hoerner was the first, who in the 1960s indicated that "apathy" or "Loss of Interest" represents real cause of extinction of advanced Civilizations... I think that it is not interesting for inhabitants to live in such en- forced self-isolation, in such lurker-like Civilization! Civilizations, which are forced to hide and tremble because of farfetched fears, is doomed to extinction.

Mars as conceived by astronomer Percival Lowell (1855-1916) in his books, Mars (1895), Mars and Its Canals (1906), and Mars As the Abode of Life (1908). Credit: H. Seppings Wright (1850-1937).

For fans of Liu Cixin's Remembrance of Earth's Past (or The Three-Body Problem) trilogy, these sentiments likely bring to mind the Dark Forest Hypothesis. And they would be correct! Whether it's safe to broadcast is at the heart of the debate over the ethics of METI (more on that later).

The first example of an "active SETI" proposal came from the 19th-century German mathematician and astronomer Carl Friedrich Gauss, though debate continues over who first proposed the idea. In 1820, Gauss proposed drawing a petroglyph representing the Pythagorean theorem (a right triangle and three squares) on the surface of the Earth that would be large enough to be seen from the Moon or Mars. This would signal to extraterrestrial beings using the language of mathematics.

Several experiments were attempted in the late 19th and early 20th centuries, which were heavily inspired by astronomers like Giovanni Schiaparelli (1835-1910). While observing Mars, he noted several faint linear features, which he designed "canali" (Italian for "channels"). He further named these features after ancient seas and rivers derived from Classical Antiquity. The name "canali" was misinterpreted by scientists and the public to mean "canals," fueling belief in a Martian civilization.

Percival Lowell popularized the idea in the late-19th century, building an observatory in Flagstaff, Arizona, to map these supposed canals. Henceforth, people tried to message the civilization they believed was there. As Hugo Gernsback wrote in a 1909 opinion piece published in the journal Modern Electrics:

Every time our neighbor Mars comes in opposition to the Earth, a host of inventors and others begin to turn their attention to the great up-to-date problem, "signaling to Mars." It is safe to say that all the proposed, possible as well as impossible, projects would fill a good-sized volume, especially the ones invented in this country, which by far leads the world in number of projects and "inventions."

That same year, Harvard astronomer Professor William Pickering proposed building a massive $10 million system of motorized, synchronized mirrors in the desert to flash signals to Mars. Known as the Mirror Scheme, the goal was to see whether any sentient life and a Martian civilization existed by looking for return signals. In 1928, Dr. Hugh Mansfield Robinson, a British lawyer who believed he had been telepathically connected to a Martian woman (Oomaruru) since the early 1920s, launched what became known as the Martian Telegram.

The experiment consisted of a telegraph sent from London's Rugby Radio Station at a wavelength of 18,240 meters (ft). The message contained three words: "Opesti, Nipitia, Secomba," which Robinson believed were derived from the Martian language, followed by "Mar la loi de Earth" ("Love to Mars from Earth"). The station also kept a receiver antenna tuned to a wavelength of 30,000 m (ft), which Robinson insisted on, stating it was what the Martians preferred.

Like the earliest SETI efforts, the first METI experiments were humble and aimed at the nearest planets in the Solar System. They also reflected the then-accepted idea that Mars and Venus were inhabited and hosted civilizations. As our knowledge of the cosmos grew and technology improved, METI efforts would expand and direct their messages towards the stars. The transition from interplanetary to interstellar communications will be the subject of our next installment. Stay tuned!

#1243. Frank Lloyd Wright’s Only Planetarium Reopens in Florida After 6-Year Restoration

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

The Florida Southern College landmark is open for the first time in more than two decades.

The only planetarium that Frank Lloyd Wright ever built shut down in 2004, more than four decades after the famous American architect completed the dome-topped structure on the grounds of Florida Southern College (FSC). The projector died, according to the Lakeland-based school, rendering the building functionally obsolete.

Now, the entire planetarium—with a new, state-of-the-art projector—just emerged from a six-year restoration and is once again welcoming students and the public.

Led by campus architect Jeff Baker of MCWB Architects, the long-awaited project is part of a larger effort by FSC to revive all 13 of its buildings by Wright, who conceived a master plan called “Child of the Sun” for the Lakeland college between 1938 and 1958. Complete with academic buildings, esplanades, chapels, and a library, the 113-acre campus is home to the largest collection of Wright-designed buildings anywhere in the world. In 2012, the National Park Service granted National Historic Landmark status to the school.

The two-phase renovation of the planetarium, which is located in the Polk Science Building, began in 2021 with the exterior. The team updated the roof, flashing (weatherproofing), and stucco cladding, and replaced 280 custom textile-block tiles on the facade. Wright developed his signature textile-block system in the 1920s, applying geometric patterns to hand-cast concrete tiles and then sheathing both exteriors and interiors of his buildings to create a tactile, woven look. The planetarium’s tiles are among the architect’s most straightforward, defined by simple horizontal bands and a vertical “stitching” motif.

By 2023, the project moved indoors. The main objective with the interior was to restore Wright’s original vision, which had been augmented through the years with awkward additions, like a computer closet from the 1980s.

Now, formerly deep-blue walls are a calming neutral hue, while the flooring is back to “Cherokee Red,” another Wright signature found throughout many of his Usonian designs. Local artisans also collaborated on restoring the structure’s 60-seat theater, reviving the original circular floor plan with a seating system crafted out of plywood and red vinyl upholstery.

Capping off the project is the addition of a new Cosm projector and Bowens sound system, both of which provide state-of-the-art functionality befitting a modern planetarium. Perhaps best of all, it’s not just FSC students who will benefit: The public can experience the planetarium as intended through a series of four rotating shows each week. Among the scheduled offerings is “Destination Mars: The New Frontier,” which focuses on humanity’s epic endeavors to the Red Planet.

“Reopening the planetarium allows us to bring an important part of Florida Southern’s history back to life while creating new opportunities for our students and the community,” said Tory King, director of tourism operations and visitor experience at FSC, in a statement. “The architectural restoration brought the space back, and the new technology now allows us to return it to active educational and public use.”

Tickets are $20 per person and $15 per person for families and groups of four or more.

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#650. Scientists Discover a Strange Insect in the Himalayas Where No One Expected It

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

A newly identified Himalayan wormlion challenges the idea that its genus lives only in the tropics, raising the possibility of undiscovered relatives on nearby mountain slopes.

In rain-sheltered patches of sand and soil on the southern slopes of the Himalayas, wormlion larvae build funnel-shaped pits to catch prey. Larvae belonging to this group were first collected in southern Xizang in 1978, but without adult flies to examine, scientists could not confirm their identity.

Adult specimens collected during a 2025 expedition to Yadong County have now helped resolve that 48-year mystery. A team led by researchers at Dali University in China has described the new species, Vermitigris tsangyanggyatso, in a recent paper published in the open-access journal ZooKeys.

Wormlions belong to Vermileonidae, a family of brachyceran, or short-horned, flies. They are the only known family of flies whose larvae capture prey by constructing these pits. Their hunting strategy evolved independently of the similar traps built by antlions, an example of evolutionary convergence.

The researchers’ search for adult wormlions had already taken years of patient work with another species. Asked what first drew them to the family, the team recalled:

“Well, it was probably an accidental discovery that first motivated us. In 2015, while searching for pit-building antlions on Cangshan Mountain in Dali, China, we discovered an unfamiliar larva that we found particularly interesting. However, because the adults have an extremely short lifespan, we failed for many years to determine its identity.

It was not until 2024 that we finally obtained adults through artificial rearing in the laboratory. It turned out to be a new species, which we named Vermiophis cangshanensis. This first discovery therefore took nine years. Since then, we have paid close attention to wormlions wherever we encounter them.”

The adults collected in Yadong revealed a species that is unusually large for its group. Their bodies measure up to 19.5 millimeters, about three-quarters of an inch, with wings nearly 16 millimeters, or 0.63 inches, long. A uniformly yellowish-brown to reddish-brown abdomen distinguishes Vermitigris tsangyanggyatso from its closest relatives, which have light and dark rings or longitudinal stripes.

Its mouthparts also offer a clue to how it lives as an adult. Some related lineages diversified during the Late Mesozoic while developing elongated mouthparts for feeding on nectar. The new species instead has a markedly shortened rostrum, or snout, suggesting that it may not feed at all during its final life stage.

Fully grown larvae are larger than the adults, reaching 20 to 28 millimeters, or about 0.8 to 1.1 inches, in length. They have a single semicircular proleg, a fleshy, leglike appendage, bearing a distinct row of five to seven spines. Yet their anatomy has not fully explained how they construct traps in the different materials available to them.

The team found larvae in three types of rain-protected shelter: fine-grained soil beneath giant rocks, wood debris under a pavilion, and fine-grained river sand accumulated beside a river. Field observations indicated a strong preference for the river sand. These sheltered sites provide the stable conditions the larvae need to maintain their pitfall traps, although the physical features that allow them to work across such different textures remain uncertain.

“We honestly do not know yet. Perhaps future behavioral experiments will help answer this question,” the team said when asked which features make that possible.

Wet forests have traditionally been shown to act as barriers to wormlion dispersal. Finding this species in protected pockets within such an environment shows how members of Vermitigris can live there despite the surrounding wet conditions.

The adult observations offered another piece of the dispersal picture. On sunny days, most flies rested quietly on leaves near the places where their larvae lived. One specimen, however, was found farther away around agricultural fields, which the researchers interpreted as evidence of strong flight and dispersal abilities.

The Himalayan location also broadens the climates in which researchers might expect to find the genus. As the team explained:

“The previously known species were first discovered in tropical Southeast Asia, and these insects were therefore thought to be restricted to tropical regions. The discovery of this species suggests that they may also be capable of adapting to subtropical climates. We hope that more unknown species will eventually be discovered in mainland Southeast Asia, such as Thailand, as well as in southwestern China, including Yunnan, Guizhou, and Sichuan Provinces.”

The species name honors Tsangyang Gyatso, the sixth Dalai Lama, a celebrated 17th-century Tibetan poet-monk from Xizang known for his romantic verse and unconventional life. Its description raises the number of Vermitigris species recorded in China from one to two and the genus’s worldwide total from four to five. There are now 67 known living species of wormlion flies.

Many neighboring areas along the southern Himalayan slopes remain unsampled, leaving room for further fieldwork to uncover additional members of this poorly documented group. For co-author Ji-Shen Wang, the work also reflects a curiosity that extends beyond his original research specialty:

“From the perspective of science communication, I would say that although my primary research interest is the insect order Mecoptera (scorpionflies), I have gradually expanded my interests into Diptera (flies) as well.

As a naturalist, I find the diversity of life on Earth endlessly fascinating, and this curiosity about nature continually motivates me to explore and wonder. I would like to quote Herman Melville from Moby-Dick: ‘As for me, I am tormented with an everlasting itch for things remote. I love to sail forbidden seas, and land on barbarous coasts.”

Reference: “After forty-eight years: An enigmatic new wormlion fly from Xizang, China (Diptera, Vermileonidae)” by Li-Xia Shan and Ji-Shen Wang, 7 April 2026, ZooKeys. DOI: 10.3897/zookeys.1276.184675

This research was financially supported by the Starting Foundation for the High-level Talents, Dali University (KY2096124040), the project of the Cangshan Forest Ecosystem Observation and Research Station of Yunnan Province, Yunnan Provincial Department of Science and Technology (202305AM070003), the project Exploration and Practice of the “One Innovation, Two Integrations, and Three Breakthroughs” Talent Training Model in the Biological Sciences Major at Dali University (JGSX2024201), and the First Cohort of Core Curriculum Cluster Teaching Teams at Dali University: Macrobiology Teaching Team (KCTD04).
