#735. Scientists Can Now Spot Hidden Sunspots on the Solar Far Side

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Unlike the Moon, the Sun is constantly rotating from our perspective - but we still only see half of it at any given time. Since it takes 27 days to complete a full rotation, that leaves plenty of time for changes to happen to the side we canât see that could directly impact Earthâs own space weather a few weeks later. Now a team of physicists led by Bibhuti Kumar Jha of the Southwest Research Institute has published a paper in The Astrophysical Journal Supplement Series that uses probability to map out what might be happening on the far side of the Sun, even if we canât see it directly.

In the past, space weather forecasters have struggled with this critical cosmic blind spot. Coronal Mass Ejections, one of the most dangerous forms of space weather, can fry satellites or overload ground-based power grids. And the conditions for them could form on the side of the Sun facing away from us so that we only get warning of them once they turn the corner of the Sun itself.

Sure, we had computer simulations of what might be happening around the Sunâs back, but they were mainly just guesses. So the authors turned to a technique that, though it has been around for a few decades, is still an evolving field of research. Helioseismology is similar to traditional seismology on Earth, where geologists use seismic shockwaves from earthquakes to map the interior parts of Earth that we canât see from the outside.

Solar physicists took that same general concept and modified it for use on the Sun. It may sound impossible to âseeâ through a giant ball of burning plasma, but it's possible with high energy acoustic waves. The churning motions that power the convective zone of the Sun itself also create sound waves that both bounce inside the Sun itself and reflect off its surface. Crucially, if those sound waves happen to pass through an active region, the magnetic fields concentrated there will slightly shift their travel time.

While that doesnât sound like much to go on, instruments like the Helioseismic and Magnetic Imager aboard NASAâs Solar Dynamics Observatory and the ground-based Global Oscillation Network Group are able to measure these slight ripples on the near side of the Sun, and use some fancy math to figure out where the magnetic knots representing sunspots are gathering on the far side. The problem has been reliability.

Fraser talks about the Carrington Event - the strongest solar storm directed toward Earth on record.

The signals of these acoustic waves have been notoriously noisy, creating âghostâ active regions that can pollute solar forecasts with fake sunspots - essentially making the heliophysicists look like theyâre crying âwolfâ every time they see a potential sunspot on the far side in the data. But the solution isnât just to ignore the messy data - then weâd leave ourselves open to the risk of a dangerous sunspot developing and having even less warning on how to deal with it.

To solve the noise problem, Jha and his co-authors turned to historical data from a mission called STEREO. This involved two NASA spacecraft that were placed around the far side of the Sun, but still within direct communication lines of Earth. Between 2010 and 2014, the mission collected extreme ultraviolet images of what was happening on the far side - the exact type of data needed to build a validation model of what the far side would look like - assuming we have acoustic data that was synchronous with those observations.

Luckily, SDO was operating at the same time, so the researchers were able to cross-correlate the ultraviolet images from STEREO with the magnetic readings from SDO, but it wasnât a direct correlation - the authors had to scale the signal into proper magnetic units. Once they did, though, they were able to plug their calibrated data in a model called the Advective Flux Transport model, which accurately simulates the plasma flows and magnetic fields that are critical in heliophysical models.

https://music.youtube.com/podcast/R8_XwBxWsn0 Fraser discusses how bad a solar storm would be for a moon base.

What they found was a stark contrast between the regular models and the reality represented by the data from STEREO. Their updated method smoothly updates the locations of the sunspots even while theyâre still hidden on the other side of the Sun, which was much more accurate than the jagged âjumpsâ other models attribute to the same sunspots. Also, when they zoomed out to the entire star, they realized that conventional models had been underestimating the total unsigned magnetic flux by 10-20% of the Sun's total magnetic flux budget. That is a huge underestimation, especially for something as critical to the successful operation of an entire industry as space weather.

This is only another step in the process of truly understanding our solar neighbor. Turning sound waves into verified magnetic maps of the far side of our star is an excellent step towards coming up with a fully integrated model of how sunspots form, but weâre still a long way off from a fully functional early warning system for these potentially dangerous events. Hopefully weâll get that figured out without having to experience a wake up call of only a few days' notice before weâre blasted with solar radiation.

SwRI - SwRI-led team develops improved model for mapping the Sun

UT - How Flare Ribbons Reveal Hidden Solar Explosions on the Sun

UT - Ringing the GONG: New Details About the Sun's Far-side Activities

#751. A Merger Solves a Puzzling Paradox in Orion

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

When the Hubble Space Telescope was launched in 1990, an eager astronomy community had a massive list of targets they hoped the new telescope would observe. One of them was the well-known Orion Nebula, visible with the naked eye. At about 1400 light years away, Orion is the closest massive star-forming region to Earth. For its first few years, the Hubble suffered through a flawed mirror problem, but once that was fixed, the telescope examined Orion.

One of its discoveries was the unusual, dark objects that appeared to have tails. They'd been seen before, but not in such detail. In a 1993 paper, researchers determined that they were protoplanetary disks, and coined the term proplyd. But these proplyds were different than others because of their tails. These tails are part of a paradox in the Orion Nebula.

Some early Hubble images of proplyds in Orion. Image Credit: O'Dell, Wen, and Hu 1992. ApJ.

Why do the disks and their extended gas shells appear to be so much older than the stars themselves?

New research in Astronomy and Astrophysics has an answer. It's titled "A triple-induced merger in Î¸ 1 Ori C solves the proplyd lifetime problem and the curiously young shell of M42." The lead author is Maite Wilhelm, an astronomer at Leiden University in the Netherlands.

"A longstanding tension exists between the stellar ages in the Orion Nebula Cluster (ONC) and the rapid external photoevaporation of its protoplanetary discs," the authors write.

This research focuses on Orion's most massive star: 1 Ori C1 (Theta-1 Orionis C1). 1 Ori C1 is also the most massive star in the famous Trapezium Cluster, dominated by a group of four stars in the heart of Orion. Its powerful radiation is stripping material away from protoplanetary disks in the region. The disk material streams away, forming a tail-like shape.

The Trapezium Cluster in the heart of the Orion Nebula. 1 Ori C1 is the brightest star in this image. Image Credit: Data: Hubble Legacy Archive , Processing: Robert Gendler

But the amount of material stripped away so far doesn't match the age of 1 Ori C1 and other stars. The stars are one million years old, so the material should've been stripped away long ago.

There's an additional puzzle. "More recently, the shell of the Orion Nebula has also been found to be dynamically younger than its stars," the authors write.

We've learned a lot more about Orion since the Hubble's early days. Many things have been observed more deeply and measured more accurately, including how rapidly Orion's shell is expanding.

"More recently, the expansion velocity of the shell component of the Orion Nebula was measured to be â¼13 km/s," the researchers explain. The shell has a radius of about 2.5 pc, so this indicates an age of only about 0.3 million years. Since the shell's expansion is mostly driven by the stellar winds coming from the stars in the Orion Nebula Cluster, this creates another dilemma.

"It is striking to observe that two processes powered by the ONCâs massive stars both appear to have younger dynamical ages than the inferred stellar age," the authors write.

The large image of the Trapezium cluster in Orion is from the Hubble. The inset image is a closeup of one of the stars in Orion, showing how the stellar wind is eroding its proplyd. Image Credit: NASA/STScI/Rice Univ./C.O'Dell et al / O. BernÃ©, I. Schrotter, PDRs4All)

Other researchers proposed an explanation for these discrepancies. Maybe there are different populations of stars with different ages, driven by the ejection of massive stars that disrupted and triggered different episodes of star formation. But that explanation didn't stick.

In this work, the authors propose that a stellar merger explains Orion's puzzle.

"We propose a new scenario aimed at resolving both age issues," the researchers write. "In our scenario, the most massive star of the ONC, Î¸ 1 Ori C1, formed from the merger between two binary stars..."

1 Ori C1 has a current binary partner, 1 Ori C2, and its presence triggered the merger between 1 Ori C1 and another partner star, according to the authors.

This created the 1 Ori C1 we see today. It's a massive and hot O-type star, the most massive type of star on the main sequence. 1 Ori C1 has more than 33 solar masses, and its UV radiation provides most of Orion's illumination.

Lead author Wilhelm and her co-researchers tested this idea against simulations. They showed that the merger scenario is plausible. Prior to the merger, the radiation from the stars was much weaker, and the proplyds were better able to withstand them. That means that the dusty disks survived for most of their stars' lifetimes. But after the merger, the outpouring of radiation from 1 Ori C1 changed the game.

This image shows part of Orion. The star LL Orionis, though not part of this work, illustrates the power of stellar winds. It's just up and to the left of center, highlighted by its bow shock. The star is emitting a powerful stellar wind that creates the shock, as its wind slams into the gas in Orion. The complex gas morphology in Orion is created by winds from different stars. Image Credit: NASA, ESA and the Hubble Heritage Team

"This merger, taking place between the last episode of star formation and today, resulted in an increased luminosity, leading to stronger external photoevaporation and accelerated expansion of the shell," the authors explain.

But this can't explain how the Orion Nebula's shell appears to be younger than its stars. "However, the required increase in the stellar wind luminosity would be greater than a simple merger would be equipped to explain," the researchers write. The gas shell's rapid expansion required an additional element. The merger alone couldn't have driven all of the expansion.

The researchers say that cooler gas from the evaporation disks played a role. By mixing with the hotter gas inside the expanding shell, it could've generated the additional force needed to explain the shell's expansion.

This 3D model image shows Orion's expanding gas bubble in blue. The stellar merger isn't enough on its own to explain the bubble's expansion. Image Credit: NASA, ESA, Frank Summers (STScI), Greg T. Bacon (STScI), Lisa Frattare (STScI), Zolt G. Levay (STScI), K. Litaker (STScI) Acknowledgment: Axel Mellinger, Robert Gendler, Rogelio B. Andreo

This work also explains a couple other curiosities: 1 Ori C1 has a tilted magnetic field and excessive nitrogen, both of which can be explained by a merger.

"This is the first time we have witnessed the consequences of a major stellar collision in a our own cosmic neighbourhood," co-author Simon Portegies Zwart said in a press release. "It also shows that stellar collisions may be much more common than we previously thought, and that they can have far-reaching consequences."

"Our idea shows that subtle effects in triple-star systems can have a major impact on the environments in which stars form, and potentially even on planets that are still taking shape," said lead author Wilhelm.

"We propose a novel scenario that can help explain both the proplyd lifetime problem in the ONC and the Orion Nebula shell lifetime problem. Our scenario involves the merger of a stellar binary into the present-day massive star Î¸ 1 Ori C1, mediated by vZLK oscillations induced by the present-day binary companion Î¸ 1 Ori C2," the authors conclude.

Evan Gough is a science-loving guy with no formal education who loves Earth, forests, hiking, and heavy music. He's guided by Carl Sagan's quote: "Understanding is a kind of ecstasy."

#663. Scientists Uncover a Hidden Secret That Could Transform Chemical Manufacturing

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

A hidden variable in chemical reactions could hold the key to cleaner and more efficient manufacturing.

Scientists have identified a hidden factor that can determine whether a chemical reaction succeeds or fails. By controlling tiny changes in acidity near electrodes, researchers dramatically improved the production of valuable chemical ingredients, raising the proportion of the desired product from approximately 16% to 97%.

The breakthrough, led by Professor Jaeyune Ryu of the Department of Chemical and Biological Engineering at Seoul National University College of Engineering, could help make chemical manufacturing safer, cleaner, and more efficient. The researchers developed a method that uses oxygen and electricity to produce compounds used in plastics, medicines, adhesives, and other everyday materials without requiring the separate addition of peroxide oxidants.

Their discovery centers on a surprisingly overlooked factor called “local pH,” which describes the acidity or alkalinity in the immediate surroundings of an electrode. By redesigning a chemical reactor to control the distance between electrodes and the movement of liquid through the system, the team found a way to manage these conditions and greatly improve the reaction’s performance.

The work focused on two important families of chemicals: lactones and epoxides. Lactones are essential starting materials for biodegradable plastics and polyurethanes, while epoxides are used to manufacture adhesives, protective coatings, and epoxy resins found in electronic materials. Both also play important roles in producing pharmaceuticals and other specialized chemicals.

The findings were published in the Journal of the American Chemical Society (JACS), a leading chemistry journal published by the American Chemical Society (ACS).

The research builds on a technique known as organic electrosynthesis, which uses electrical energy to drive reactions that produce carbon-based chemical compounds.

This approach has attracted growing interest because it may reduce the need for some of the chemical oxidizing and reducing agents commonly used in traditional manufacturing. Such agents help reactions proceed by accepting or donating electrons, but some can be hazardous to handle and generate unwanted waste.

Until now, much of the effort to improve organic electrosynthesis has centered on familiar factors, including the electrical voltage, the materials used for electrodes, and the composition of the liquid in which reactions occur.

However, the researchers suspected that something important was being overlooked: the microscopic chemical environment directly next to each electrode.

This is where molecules exchange electrons and undergo chemical transformations. Although the surrounding solution may appear uniform, conditions within this tiny region can be dramatically different.

One particularly important factor is pH, a measure of how acidic or alkaline a substance is.

During an electrochemical reaction, hydrogen ions, also known as protons, can be produced or consumed at the electrodes. If these ions are generated or removed faster than they can move through the liquid, the pH near the electrode may become very different from the pH measured in the rest of the solution.

The researchers found that these local differences can influence several critical parts of a reaction. They can change how reactive oxygen species are produced, alter the behavior of the starting molecules, and even affect whether the finished products remain stable or begin to break down.

In other words, a reaction can fail even when the overall solution appears to have the right chemical conditions.

To determine whether this phenomenon might extend beyond their own experiments, the researchers examined more than 600 scientific papers on organic electrosynthesis published since 2010.

They found that approximately 89% of the studies involved reactions at one or more electrodes in which both electrons and protons were transferred.

This matters because the movement of protons can change local acidity. The finding suggests that overlooked pH differences near electrodes could influence a wide range of electrochemical reactions, rather than just a few specialized examples.

The team also measured how extreme these differences could become.

Even when the overall solution was essentially neutral, the researchers estimated that conditions near the cathode, the electrode where reduction occurs, could reach approximately pH 11, making the area strongly alkaline.

Meanwhile, the region around the anode, where oxidation takes place, could reach approximately pH 2.5, creating a strongly acidic environment.

These changes can have major consequences. Under such conditions, the reactive oxygen species needed to drive a reaction may become less available. Starting materials can change chemical form, and newly formed products may break down more rapidly.

Simply adjusting the acidity of the entire solution therefore may not solve the problem. What matters is the environment directly around the electrodes, where the chemistry actually happens.

To demonstrate their discovery, the scientists investigated a well-established chemical process called Baeyer–Villiger oxidation.

This reaction adds an oxygen atom to a type of organic molecule known as a ketone, converting it into an ester or lactone. Lactones are ring-shaped compounds that serve as important building blocks for materials such as biodegradable plastics and polyurethanes.

Traditionally, Baeyer–Villiger oxidation often requires highly reactive oxidizing chemicals, including peroxyacids. Although effective, these substances can create difficulties involving safe handling, chemical waste, and the treatment of unwanted byproducts.

The Seoul National University team developed a different approach.

Instead of supplying these oxidants from outside the system, the researchers used electricity to activate oxygen gas at an electrode. This produced reactive oxygen species capable of transferring oxygen atoms into organic molecules.

To establish where the oxygen in the final products came from, the team conducted isotope-tracing experiments. These tests confirmed that the incorporated oxygen originated from oxygen gas rather than water.

The scientists also discovered that the two electrodes could work together in an important way.

At the cathode, oxygen reduction generates reactive oxygen species that initiate the oxygen-transfer reaction. Hydrogen peroxide formed during this process can then be converted back into reactive oxygen species at the anode.

This cooperation helps sustain the supply of reactive chemical species necessary for the reaction.

However, the entire process depends heavily on the acidity near the electrodes. If local pH becomes too extreme, the chemistry can become much less effective.

Once the researchers understood the importance of local pH, they investigated how to control it.

Using computer simulations and electrochemical measurements, they developed a reactor design that places the electrodes closer together while allowing the electrolyte, the electrically conductive liquid carrying the reaction ingredients, to flow between them.

The idea was to prevent extreme acidity differences from developing.

Because the area near the anode tends to become acidic while the area near the cathode becomes alkaline, bringing these regions closer together and circulating the liquid helps counterbalance their effects.

Under conditions where local pH differences were not effectively controlled, approximately 16% of the relevant products formed were the desired target compound. With the improved reactor design, that selectivity rose to approximately 97%.

This increase in selectivity means the reaction became much better at producing the intended substance rather than unwanted products.

The researchers also discovered that changing the flow of the electrolyte had a powerful effect on production.

Across the flow rates they tested, the amount of product generated per unit time increased by approximately 14 times from the lowest to the highest flow rate.

Moving the electrodes closer together also made a difference. When the distance between them was reduced from 20 mm to 4 mm, the production rate increased by approximately 2.2 times.

Perhaps most revealing was what happened when the liquid was not allowed to flow.

Even though the applied voltage and the solution’s composition remained unchanged, the researchers obtained almost none of the desired product.

This showed just how important the immediate chemical environment can be. The success of an electrochemical reaction depends on more than the ingredients, voltage, and electrode materials. The physical design of the reactor and the microscopic conditions it creates can be equally critical.

The researchers then explored whether their approach could be used to manufacture different kinds of useful chemicals.

Using their optimized conditions, they successfully synthesized lactones from 18 different ketone compounds.

One important example was ε-caprolactone, which they produced with an 82% yield.

This compound is a starting material for polycaprolactone, a biodegradable polymer used in medical applications such as surgical sutures and drug-delivery systems.

The researchers also applied their local pH control strategy to the production of epoxides, successfully obtaining products from six different alkene starting materials.

Epoxides are important ingredients in adhesives, surface coatings, and epoxy resins used in electronics. They are also valuable intermediates in the production of pharmaceuticals and fine chemicals.

Together, these experiments showed that controlling the microscopic environment around electrodes could have applications beyond a single reaction.

The findings suggest a potentially versatile way to improve the production of many commercially important chemical ingredients.

One of the most promising aspects of the technique is that it uses oxygen and electricity while reducing the need for externally added peroxide oxidants.

The reactions also take place at room temperature and normal atmospheric pressure, avoiding the need for separate equipment to heat or pressurize the system.

These characteristics could make certain chemical processes simpler and potentially safer.

Because electricity provides the energy needed to drive the reactions, the approach could eventually be combined with renewable energy sources to support more sustainable chemical manufacturing.

The researchers believe that pharmaceutical ingredients and other high-value specialty chemicals could be particularly promising applications.

These products often require careful control of reaction conditions and strict safety measures. Electrochemical methods may offer advantages by limiting the need to handle hazardous oxidizing agents and potentially allowing production in relatively compact equipment.

However, the technology is not yet ready for widespread industrial use. Further optimization and testing at larger production scales will be needed to determine whether the laboratory results can be reproduced economically and reliably in commercial manufacturing.

Beyond its immediate applications, the discovery offers a different perspective on how electrochemical reactions should be designed.

Traditionally, researchers have concentrated on choosing the right starting materials, catalysts, electrode surfaces, voltages, and solution compositions.

The Seoul National University team’s findings indicate that controlling the tiny environment where molecules actually react may be just as important.

This shifts attention toward “designing the environment in which molecules actually react.”

Professor Jaeyune Ryu, who supervised the research, said, “This study transforms ‘local pH’ near the electrode from a hidden variable into a controllable design principle.”

He added, “To improve the efficiency of organic electrosynthesis, we need to design not only the reaction substrates and catalysts, but also the environment in which the molecules actually react.”

The researchers believe this principle could reveal opportunities to improve other electrochemical processes, particularly those involving the movement of both electrons and protons.

Rather than treating local acidity as an unavoidable side effect, future chemical reactors could be deliberately engineered to maintain conditions that favor the desired reactions.

Reference: “Local pH Control Promotes Oxygen-Atom Transfer Electrosynthesis” by Seonghyeon Min, Junghoon Lee, Hanju Kim and Jaeyune Ryu, 4 September 2026, Journal of the American Chemical Society. DOI: 10.1021/jacs.6c12994

The study’s co-first authors were Seonghyeon Min and Junghoon Lee, both M.S. students in Seoul National University’s Department of Chemical and Biological Engineering. Hanju Kim, an integrated M.S./Ph.D. student, participated as a co-author, with Professor Ryu overseeing the research.

Min and Lee plan to continue investigating how local reaction environments influence organic electrosynthesis, with the goal of extending the approach to a broader range of chemical reactions and manufacturing processes.

The research was supported by Seoul National University, the National Research Foundation of Korea, and the Institute for Basic Science (IBS).

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

#542. Archaeologists Just Found the Oldest Dwelling in the Western Hemisphere

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

This 13,800-year-old Alaskan base camp is rewriting how the Americas were settled.

Archaeologists uncovered something more than a hunting stopover just east of the Bering Land Bridge. Far more. An excavation team at Shég’ Xdaltth’í’—in what is now Alaska, roughly 34 miles southeast of Fairbanks—discovered the earliest known Nenana Complex occupation east of the Bering Land Bridge. It isn’t the oldest archaeological evidence of humans in the Western Hemisphere, but it may be the oldest spot where people actually settled in. It also delivered some intriguing news about domesticated dogs.

In a new study published in the journal Science Advances, the authors date the early base camp to 13,800 years ago, when it was inhabited by the Late Pleistocene people of Alaska.

While researchers believe human dispersal across the Bering Land Bridge helped populate the Americas, we still don’t fully understand the region’s archaeological record, in part because most known Beringian sites are scattered remains of small seasonal hunting camps. The Shég’ Xdaltth’í’ site in interior Alaska—a name that translates as “They Were There”—offers a rare focal point. The study’s authors wrote that the oldest array of cultural remains found there includes a “unique hafted-biface form, osseous tools, diverse fauna, and well-preserved activity areas, a record unparalleled for this age in Beringia.” The findings may all be a part of what is ultimately a historic discovery.

“The complex feature at Shég’ Xdaltth’í’ represents the earliest potential case of a habitation structure in North America,” the authors wrote. “Early Alaskans knew their surroundings well.”

Even though Shég’ Xdaltth’í’ has been studied for more than a decade, the experts weren’t expecting to unearth tens of thousands of artifacts. But that’s exactly what they found, highlighted by stone tools, bone needles, and an antler pendant—along with a hearth, a small pit, and a soil stain marking where a wooden post once stood, likely the remains of a lean-to or early tent. Nearby, the team also uncovered an earth oven filled with charcoal and animal bones, probably used to slow-cook meat for a large family group.

Hunting camps aren’t uncommon in the region, but this is something far beyond that, instead showing people “settling in” to a location they returned to intermittently over roughly 1,000 years. “I expected we’d find hunting tools and knapping [stone tool making] debris surrounding a hearth or two, but I had no idea that we’d stumble into a base camp with tens of thousands of artifacts,” Kelly Graf, the study’s first author and an archaeologist at the University of Kansas, told Live Science. “It was a total surprise.”

The latest archaeological and genetic research has the ancestors of today’s Indigenous Americans entering the continent around 21,000 years ago, so finding an occupied site in eastern Beringia—the Alaskan half of the landmass that joined northeast Asia and northwest North America during the last ice age—with cultural links running both west and south fits the narrative.

Those westward links show up in the stone and the stone sources. The team said the obsidian artifacts match a geological fingerprint hundreds of miles to the northwest, meaning people carried the volcanic rock a long way to the site. The hafted bifaces, meanwhile, were knapped from rhyolite and basalt into a distinctive diamond shape unique to central Alaska, yet they closely resemble tools from sites far to the west in Beringia, in present-day northern Siberia.

Then there are the finds that point south instead. Eight bone needle fragments tie the camp to sites farther south in North America, where the only other eyed needles had previously been recovered, making the Shég’ Xdaltth’í’ needles the oldest discovered in the Western Hemisphere. The antler pendant, with a hole drilled at one end and a point at the other, resembles personal adornments found elsewhere in North America as well.

“The co-occurrence of these cultural features at the single site of Shég’ Xdaltth’í’ is truly notable,” the team wrote, adding that the finds “highlight the strong cultural connection between early Beringians and Americans.”

Whether the residents themselves made those journeys remains unclear. “It would be hard to pin down whether or not they were moving back and forth during a lifetime,” Graf said, “but at least we can say there is a connection.”

The research uncovered one additional surprise. While bones from more than a dozen wolf-like animals create additional links to Asia, an analysis of one animal’s jaw showed it came from neither a dire wolf nor a Pleistocene gray wolf, but from a population of wolf-like canids unique to central Alaska. Because current genetic evidence suggests early domesticated dogs in North America descended from Siberian wolves, the site’s timing and location offer circumstantial evidence that these people may have been dog owners.

Still, further work is needed, both at Shég’ Xdaltth’í’ and elsewhere, to understand the dispersal and timing of population shifts east of the land bridge. “Alaska is a big place and so remote,” Graf said, “so there are really just a few small areas that have been intensively investigated.”

Tim Newcomb is a journalist based in the Pacific Northwest. He covers stadiums, sneakers, gear, infrastructure, and more for a variety of publications, including Popular Mechanics. His favorite interviews have included sit-downs with Roger Federer in Switzerland, Kobe Bryant in Los Angeles, and Tinker Hatfield in Portland.

#550. New 3D thermal invisibility cloak makes objects disappear from infrared cameras

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Scientists have developed and tested the first three-dimensional thermal invisibility cloak, a device that can conceal objects from infrared cameras while protecting them from extreme temperatures. The breakthrough could lead to new ways to cool microchips, safeguard delicate electronics, and prevent equipment from being detected through its thermal signature.

The cloak can conceal objects with a wide variety of shapes, regardless of the direction from which heat approaches. Earlier thermal cloaks were limited to two dimensions or worked only when heat traveled in a particular direction. The new design overcomes these restrictions by directing heat around a hidden object rather than simply blocking it. As a result, infrared imaging shows a temperature pattern that makes the object appear to be absent.

The research was conducted by University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign civil and environmental engineering professor Shelly Zhang, postdoctoral researcher Weichen Li, and graduate student Yibo Wang, in collaboration with professor Ole Sigmund of the Technical University of Denmark. Their findings were published in Nature Communications.

"A real thermal cloak should work no matter where the heat comes from," Zhang said. "Our device can hide a complex 3D object in an infinite number of directions while keeping the temperature inside stable and protected."

A New Material Makes 3D Thermal Invisibility Possible

Creating a thermal cloak that functions in three dimensions has been a longstanding challenge. Previous experimental devices could redirect heat only across a flat surface or along one direction of heat flow, leaving them far short of the capabilities needed for a fully three-dimensional cloak.

To overcome these limitations, the researchers revisited the foundational principles of transformation thermotics, a field that explores how heat can be redirected through specially designed materials. They focused on a central question: "What kind of material structure could cover almost all the thermal properties needed for a perfect cloak?"

The solution was a new lattice-based material whose structure can be independently adjusted along three directions. These changes allow the researchers to control how efficiently heat passes through different parts of the cloak. Compared with previous designs, the material can achieve a much broader range of thermal conductivities, closely approaching the properties predicted to be necessary for ideal thermal cloaking.

Importantly, the researchers went beyond computer simulations and constructed a working device. They used 3D metal printing to produce an intricate aluminum lattice, which provides pathways for efficient heat conduction. They then used mold casting to fill the remaining spaces with a rubber-like material that conducts heat poorly. Together, these materials create a structure that can carefully control the movement of heat.

Infrared Camera Tests Confirm the Cloak Works

To evaluate the device, the team positioned it between hot and cold regions, establishing a temperature difference that caused heat to flow through the surrounding environment. They monitored the resulting temperature patterns with an infrared camera to determine whether the cloak could successfully redirect heat around the concealed area.

The experiments showed that the device could guide heat around the hidden region while preserving the surrounding temperature pattern. To an infrared camera, the area appeared much as it would if no object were present. At the same time, temperatures inside the cloak remained uniform, shielding the concealed space from the extreme conditions outside.

The researchers also tested whether the cloak could accommodate unusually complicated objects. They successfully concealed intricate three-dimensional geometries, including detailed shapes resembling human heads. According to the team, earlier experimental thermal cloaks have not demonstrated a comparable combination of geometric complexity and cloaking performance.

Potential Applications in Electronics and Stealth Technology

The ability to control heat in three dimensions could have important implications across several industries. In electronics, the technology could help engineers direct heat away from sensitive components, improve thermal management in microchips, and protect devices operating under demanding conditions.

The researchers also see potential applications in security and defense. Future versions of the technology might help conceal people or equipment from thermal detection systems or shield valuable assets from severe temperature conditions. These uses would build on the device's demonstrated ability to redirect external heat while keeping the protected region thermally stable.

"Any field that needs precise control of heat or needs to protect something from being detected thermally could benefit from this work," Zhang said. "But we also see it more broadly: it's about hiding and protecting information that is carried by heat."

The Next Challenge Is Hiding Objects That Generate Heat

Although the current cloak represents a major advance, the researchers want to develop more sophisticated versions capable of performing additional functions. One particularly important goal is to conceal objects that produce heat themselves, rather than only protecting them from heat originating outside the cloak.

Achieving this would require materials that can actively control heat within the concealed region. Instead of simply redirecting incoming heat, a future cloak would need to concentrate, distribute, or guide thermal energy as conditions change. Such capabilities could expand the technology's usefulness in electronics, thermal protection, and applications involving infrared detection.

"We've shown that a true 3D omnidirectional thermal cloak is possible," Zhang said. "The next step is to make cloaks that don't just hide and protect, but also actively manipulate heat in useful ways."

The research received support from the National Science Foundation, the Villum Foundation, and the Air Force Office of Scientific Research. Zhang is also affiliated with mechanical science and engineering and the National Center for Supercomputing Applications at the University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign.

#667. Scientists overcome a hidden obstacle to more powerful quantum computers

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Silicon spin qubits could play a major role in the future of quantum computing because they can be produced using the same semiconductor manufacturing methods that make today's computer chips. However, researchers have faced a significant challenge. When multiple silicon qubits are controlled at the same time, their operations tend to become less accurate. Building practical, large quantum computers will require scientists to control many qubits simultaneously while keeping errors to a minimum.

Researchers at the RIKEN Center for Emergent Matter Science and their collaborators have now made important progress toward solving this problem. In a study published in Nature Physics, the team demonstrated highly accurate control of several silicon spin qubits operating simultaneously. Their experiments used an array of five qubits connected to a shared microwave control line. By identifying a major cause of errors during simultaneous operation, the scientists developed a technique to reduce those errors and maintain precision.

Leon Camenzind, a RIKEN research scientist and co-first author who supervised the experiments, explains the significance of the results: "This shows that high-fidelity control can be maintained even when several qubits are operated at the same time and represents an important step toward scaling up silicon-based quantum processors."

Scientists Identify a Hidden Source of Quantum Computing Errors

Unlike conventional computers, which store information as bits representing either 0 or 1, quantum computers use quantum bits, or qubits, that can exist in combinations of both states. Silicon spin qubits store this information using the spin of individual electrons confined within silicon. Performing reliable quantum calculations requires extremely precise control over these electron spins.

Microwave signals provide that control, but operating multiple qubits simultaneously can create unwanted effects that interfere with their accuracy. The researchers traced much of this problem to a phenomenon called the AC Stark shift.

This effect occurs when microwave control signals alter a qubit's effective energy levels, producing changes in the phase of its quantum state. Those phase shifts can disrupt the intended operation of individual qubits, particularly when several are being controlled at once.

To counteract the problem, the team created a calibration method that compensates for these unwanted shifts. Rather than separately calibrating every possible combination of qubits, the technique relies on calibrations performed between pairs of qubits. This makes the approach more practical for systems containing larger numbers of quantum bits.

"Rather than requiring an entirely separate calibration procedure for every possible combination of qubits, our method uses calibrations between pairs of qubits to compensate for the unwanted phase shifts," explains Yi-Hsien Wu, the first author who carried out the experiments as part of his PhD research.

Quantum Qubits Achieve Accuracy Above 99.99%

The researchers combined their calibration technique with an improved device design and carefully adjusted microwave control pulses. Their results showed that exceptionally high accuracy could be maintained even when multiple qubits were operating simultaneously.

When the five qubits were tested individually, each achieved single-qubit gate fidelity well above 99.99%, with the highest approaching 99.999%. Fidelity describes how closely a quantum operation matches its intended result.

The team then tested how well the system performed when several qubits were active together. With as many as three qubits operating simultaneously, fidelity remained above 99.99%. Even when all five qubits were controlled at once, accuracy stayed at approximately 99.9%, approaching the levels needed for practical fault-tolerant quantum computing.

Although the calibration method greatly reduced errors, it did not eliminate them entirely. The researchers found that the remaining loss of accuracy was linked to drive-induced decoherence, an effect caused by the greater microwave power required to operate multiple qubits simultaneously.

Unlike the unwanted phase shifts corrected by the new technique, decoherence involves the loss of the delicate quantum coherence needed for reliable operations.

According to Camenzind, "This is a limitation, but further improvements to device design could help mitigate this source of error."

A New Path Toward Larger Quantum Processors

The findings show that controlling several silicon spin qubits at the same time does not necessarily require a major sacrifice in accuracy. By correcting a key source of errors, the researchers have demonstrated a promising approach for operating more qubits through shared microwave control lines.

This is especially important for the development of large quantum processors. Giving every qubit its own independent control system would make increasingly complex devices difficult to manage. Shared control lines could offer a more practical way to expand these systems while maintaining the precision needed for quantum calculations.

"By addressing a key source of control errors through pairwise calibration," says Camenzind. "Our study supports the use of shared control lines as a practical approach to scaling up quantum processors. Combined with silicon's compatibility with established semiconductor manufacturing, we believe that this advance could help pave the way toward larger quantum processors capable of performing increasingly complex computations."

The broader objective is to develop quantum computers that can reliably perform calculations despite the errors and disturbances that affect quantum systems. Such machines, known as fault-tolerant quantum computers, will require precise, dependable control over large numbers of qubits.

"Our long-term goal is to build a fault-tolerant quantum computer based on silicon spin qubits, and reliable parallel control is an essential step toward that goal," adds Seigo Tarucha, project manager of a silicon quantum computing project under JST's Moonshot Goal 6.

#760. Rare Lunar Meteorites Could Fill Gaps In Moon’s Evolution

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Lunar meteorites --- literally pieces of the Moonâs surface that have been displaced into Earth or solar orbit and have burned their way through our thick atmosphere --- arrive on our planetâs surface as often overlooked pieces of space rock. But they are extraordinarily valuable as scientific fodder since they offer lunar scientists a precious window onto the Moonâs earliest formation.

In some ways, they scientifically surpass the multitude of lunar rocks thus far returned to Earth by spacecraft. That's because they are thought to originate from literally all over the lunar surface, including the far side.

Even so, only 835 lunar meteorites have been verified as such, representing 0.7% of all meteorites that fall to Earth.

And the most pristine lunar samples still arrive here via robotic or crewed spacecraft.

Three Soviet Union Luna missions collectively returned some 300 grams, and Chinaâs Changâe 5 and 6 missions some 3.7 kg of samples, the authors of a 2025 paper appearing in the journal Elements note. The six Apollo landings collected some 382 kg of lunar samples, the authors note.

Yet lunar meteorites provide a more representative sampling of the Moon than Apollo, Randy Korotev, Research Professor Emeritus in the Dept. of Earth, Environmental, and Planetary Science at Washington University in St. Louis, told me via email. At the time of Apollo, we did not know that five of the six landing sites were in or near a geochemically anomalous part of the Moon, Korotev told me.

Lunar meteorites in contrast come from almost randomly distributed locations on the Moonâs surface, says Korotev. But we don't know for sure where any of them come from, he says.

Most lunar meteorites have been found in Antarctica, Oman and in the deserts of Northwest Africa, with a few from deserts in Australia and Botswana, the authors of a 2013 paper appearing the journal Astronomy & Geophysics. The first identified lunar meteorites, Yamato-791197 and Yamato-793169, were collected in Antarctica in 1979 by the Japanese meteorite-hunting program, they write.

Deserts and barren wastelands make good areas to look for such meteorites because desert playas and Antarctic ice make them easier to spot.

The Vernadsky Institute in Moscow has estimated that âseveral tens or a few hundred kilogramsâ of lunar rocks strike Earthâs surface every year, Washington University in St. Louis (WUSTL) reports.

Over a period of a few years to tens of thousands of years, those orbiting the earth eventually fall to the surface, says WUSTL. Those in orbit around the Sun may also eventually strike Earth up to a few tens of millions of years after they left the Moon, WUSTL notes.

Korotev has argued that our view of the Moon would be very different if we had studied its meteorites first.

From the point of view of a scientific study, Apollo was done wrong; we had no orbital data, except gray-scale photography by which to choose the landing sites, says Korotev. We did not have mineralogical or compositional data obtained from orbit, he says.

We now know that all of the Apollo and Chinaâs Changâe 5 missions visited a geochemically atypical region of the lunar near side --- nicknamed the Procellarum KREEP Terrane, the Elements paper authors write. Itâs an acronym for potassium, rare earth elements, and phosphorous, they write.

Apollo was also stymied by the fact that missions 14 through 17 took samples from a highly geochemically anomalous area of the Moon in the vicinity of the Imbrium Basin. That is, a very large, dark lava plain that covers the Moonâs northwestern quadrant.

Apollo was a political not a scientific program, says Korotev. Some forward-looking scientists had to argue with NASA to have the astronauts collect samples, he says.

One outstanding question is how the lunar highlands crust formed. Researchers still donât know if the near side crust was created in the same way as the far side crust.

Lunar meteorites are key to this story as they potentially provide us with pieces of the Moon's ancient crust and some of these must have come from the far side, Katherine Joy, professor of lunar and planetary science at the University of Manchester in the U.K., told me via email. We can use the chemistry and age of these types of lunar meteorites to test the lunar magma ocean (LMO) hypothesis, she says.

The LMO is a hypothetical global ocean of magma that is theorized to have covered the moon up to several hundred km deep very early in the Moonâs history.

As for whatâs most puzzling about these meteorites?

Each stone tells this amazing picture of the Moon's past, but not knowing exactly where the rock came from is like missing the center pieces of a puzzle, says Joy.

It would be incredible to be able to identify a sample from somewhere really geochemically unique on the Moon, like a highly evolved magmatic region like Aristarchus, or to have a sample that contains large pieces of the Moon's mantle, says Joy. These sorts of samples would tell us incredibly interesting things about the Moon's interior and thermal history, she says.

They include Astronomy Magazine, Sky and Telescope, Scientific American, Science, Nature News, National Geographic, and Universe Today. He is the author of the 2001 book "Distant Wanderers: The Search for Planets beyond the Solar System." He was also the host of the "Cosmic Controversy" podcast which is still available via Podbean.

#764. Scientists discover a hidden flaw that challenges 40 years of superconductor science

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Scientists at the University of Warwick have uncovered a surprising secret inside a superconductor that researchers have studied for 40 years. Long believed to have a uniform crystal structure, the material actually contains a complex patchwork of different atomic arrangements. Using an advanced 3D imaging technique, the team was able to see deep inside the crystal for the first time, revealing hidden features that challenge decades of scientific assumptions.

High-temperature superconductivity ranks among the most important scientific breakthroughs of the past four decades. Superconductors can carry electrical current without any resistance, eliminating the energy losses that normally occur as electricity travels through conventional materials. They also have the potential to support extraordinarily powerful magnets, making them promising for future power grids, medical scanners, and quantum computers.

The new findings, published in Physical Review Letters, come from researchers in Warwick's Department of Chemistry working with the European Synchrotron Radiation Facility (ESRF) in France. Their investigation focused on a class of copper-based superconductors known as cuprates, which can conduct electricity without resistance at unusually high (though still very cold) temperatures.

Advanced 3D Imaging Reveals a Hidden Crystal Structure

For four decades, scientists have generally built their theories of cuprate superconductivity around the assumption that these materials have the same crystal structure throughout their interiors. The new research shows that this fundamental assumption does not always hold.

To investigate, the team used scanning 3D X-ray diffraction, a sophisticated imaging method that reveals how atoms are arranged deep within a crystal. The approach is similar in principle to a medical CT scan, except that it maps atomic structures rather than tissues and organs.

This allowed the researchers to construct a detailed three-dimensional picture of the superconductor's interior, exposing structural variations that conventional techniques had not revealed.

Instead of finding a uniform crystal, they discovered a patchwork of regions containing two slightly different atomic arrangements. Even more surprising were the boundaries separating these regions, which were hundreds of times wider than expected.

These unusually broad transition zones were not simply narrow dividing lines between neighboring structures. They were extensive enough to behave almost like distinct structures themselves, potentially influencing how electricity moves through the material.

A 40-Year Assumption About Superconductors Overturned

Professor Mark Senn, who led the study at the University of Warwick's Department of Chemistry, explained why the findings could have major implications for superconductivity research.

"For forty years, the working assumption has been that these materials are the same all the way through, and nearly all the theory is built on that picture. We've shown it doesn't hold. The crystal is fundamentally patchworked and textured, with unusually wide boundary regions that likely work against superconductivity rather than just sitting alongside it.

"This might explain why some materials perform better than others and means some existing bulk measurements will need to be reinterpreted and future theoretical models built that incorporate this structural complexity."

The discovery suggests that the internal boundaries may actively interfere with superconductivity rather than simply coexist with it. If that is the case, the hidden structural differences could help explain why certain superconducting materials perform better than others.

It also means that some previous experiments, particularly those measuring the overall properties of these materials, may need to be reconsidered. Future theories will likely need to account for the complex structures inside real crystals rather than treating them as perfectly uniform.

What the Discovery Means for Future Superconductors

The researchers suspect that similar hidden structural patterns may be widespread among other cuprate superconductors. They could also occur in related materials being investigated for their ability to become superconducting under extreme pressure.

Beyond challenging existing theories, the study demonstrates the power of a new generation of imaging technology.

The advanced measurements were made possible by a recent 150 million euro upgrade to the European Synchrotron Radiation Facility, which provides extremely intense X-ray beams for studying materials in extraordinary detail.

This technology now allows scientists to examine the three-dimensional interiors of many different materials with a level of precision that was previously unavailable. By revealing how hidden atomic structures affect a material's behavior, the technique could provide valuable insights across materials science.

For superconductivity research, the implications are particularly important. Understanding how internal crystal structures influence electrical performance could eventually help scientists identify ways to improve superconducting materials, bringing more advanced energy systems, powerful magnets, medical imaging devices, and quantum technologies closer to reality.

#741. NASA’s asteroid Bennu samples reveal a 4.5-billion-year-old secret

📅 Oct 24, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Samples collected from asteroid Bennu by NASA are revealing surprising new details about how our Solar System formed approximately 4.5 billion years ago. A new chemical analysis challenges earlier theories about the asteroid's birthplace and suggests that Jupiter played an important role in shaping the materials from which Bennu and other ancient objects formed.

Bennu completes one orbit around the Sun every 1.2 years and makes a relatively close approach to Earth about every six years, coming within roughly 300,000 kilometers (186,000 miles). NASA's OSIRIS-REx mission traveled to the asteroid, collected material from its surface, and successfully delivered the samples to Earth in 2023.

On September 23, 2023, a capsule carrying approximately 120 grams of asteroid material landed in the Utah desert. Scientists around the world subsequently received small portions of the precious cargo for detailed study.

Among them was Maria Schönbächler, Professor of Isotope Geochemistry at ETH Zurich, whose laboratory received half a gram of Bennu material. Her team immediately began examining the samples for clues about the asteroid's history.

The research, now published in Science Advances, has uncovered a distinctive chemical signature that not only helps identify Bennu's origins but also provides new evidence about the processes that shaped the early Solar System.

Bennu's Chemical Fingerprint Reveals Its Cosmic Relatives

To investigate Bennu's origins, the ETH Zurich researchers examined isotopes of three elements: iron, titanium, and chromium.

Isotopes are different forms of the same chemical element that contain varying numbers of neutrons, giving them slightly different masses. By measuring the relative amounts of these isotopes, scientists can identify a material's chemical fingerprint. These patterns provide clues about where an asteroid's ingredients originated and, in some cases, when they formed.

The analysis revealed that iron and titanium are distributed remarkably evenly throughout Bennu's material. It also uncovered a close chemical relationship between Bennu, the asteroid Ryugu, and a rare group of primitive meteorites known as CI meteorites.

CI meteorites are carbon-rich remnants of the early Solar System that have undergone relatively little alteration over billions of years. They are exceptionally uncommon among meteorites recovered on Earth.

Bennu, Ryugu, and CI meteorites share strikingly similar isotopic fingerprints, suggesting that their original materials came from the same reservoir of ancient cosmic dust. Their chemical signatures also differ substantially from those of other known asteroids, meteorite groups, and planets.

These similarities offer an important clue about where these objects formed and what conditions existed during the Solar System's earliest stages.

Scientists Discover Bennu's Unexpected Birthplace

For years, researchers generally believed that primitive asteroids like Bennu originated in the outer Solar System, perhaps in the same distant regions where comets formed. They also suspected that Bennu's original material came together relatively late in the Solar System's development.

The new findings challenge both assumptions.

Instead, the researchers propose that the material that became Bennu, Ryugu, and CI meteorites accumulated near a boundary known as the water-ice line.

This boundary marks the region in the disk surrounding the young Sun where temperatures were low enough for water vapor to freeze into ice. Around 4.5 billion years ago, it may have been a particularly important meeting place for material traveling from different parts of the developing Solar System.

Dust originating in both the inner and outer regions mixed near this boundary. Water ice helped the smallest particles stick together, allowing them to gradually accumulate into larger bodies.

"Bennu is a hybrid: the material does not clearly match either the inner or the outer Solar System," says Schönbächler.

Its unusual chemical composition suggests that Bennu formed from a combination of materials originating in both regions rather than from a single, isolated source.

Jupiter May Have Played a Crucial Role in Bennu's Formation

The researchers also developed a scenario explaining how this mixing could have occurred, with Jupiter emerging as a key player.

Jupiter began forming extraordinarily early in Solar System history, within approximately one million years of the Sun's birth. At that time, the young Sun was surrounded by a rotating disk of gas, dust, and ice.

Within this disk, countless small particles gradually collided and stuck together, building larger objects through a process called accretion. Over time, some of these growing bodies became asteroids and planets.

As Jupiter rapidly increased in mass, its immense gravitational influence began reshaping the distribution of material throughout the disk.

The researchers suggest that the growing gas giant acted somewhat like a structural barrier, preventing most larger particles from crossing its orbital region while still allowing finer dust to move around it.

This fine material, originating from different parts of the disk, could then mix extensively near the water-ice line.

According to the proposed scenario, the parent bodies of Bennu and Ryugu, along with the objects that produced CI meteorites, formed from this thoroughly mixed material.

Jupiter may therefore have helped determine not only where these ancient asteroids formed but also which ingredients became incorporated into them.

Ancient Dust and Ice Reveal the Ingredients That Built Earth

The proposed formation scenario could also explain two of Bennu's most intriguing characteristics: its abundance of water-bearing material and its chemical resemblance to the Sun.

Near the water-ice boundary, ice could evaporate as temperatures changed, releasing water vapor that later condensed again in cooler regions. This process would have helped concentrate water in the material from which Bennu formed.

Meanwhile, Jupiter's influence may have limited the movement of larger particles, leaving the asteroid's parent material dominated by fine dust.

Because these tiny particles could circulate and mix efficiently throughout the disk, they preserved a chemical composition that closely resembles the overall elemental mixture found in the Sun.

The process is somewhat similar to the way fine household dust spreads and settles across many different surfaces rather than remaining in one location.

This extensive mixing could explain why Bennu's material retains such a representative sample of the ingredients present during the Solar System's earliest history.

"Bennu may offer our best glimpse of the original mix of chemical elements from which the terrestrial planets were ultimately built," as Schönbächler states.

Bennu is considered an exceptionally primitive asteroid because its material formed approximately 4.5 billion years ago and has experienced relatively little chemical alteration since then.

That makes its samples particularly valuable for reconstructing the conditions that existed before Earth and the other rocky planets finished forming.

By studying the asteroid's chemistry, scientists can better understand how the Solar System developed and which processes determined the composition of its planets.

Bennu's water-bearing minerals and organic compounds also make it important for investigating how early Earth obtained some of the chemical ingredients necessary for life.

Although these findings do not establish exactly how those materials reached our planet, they provide valuable evidence about where such ingredients originated and how they were distributed throughout the young Solar System.

A New Mission to Mars' Moon Could Reveal More

Despite the new discoveries, several questions remain unanswered.

"We are now wondering whether other asteroids have the same isotopic signature as Bennu and Ryugu," Schönbächler goes on to state.

Researchers also want to determine more precisely how Jupiter influenced the movement of dust and whether its gravitational effects were responsible for favoring the accumulation of particularly fine particles.

Additional asteroid samples could help scientists test the proposed formation scenario and establish whether Bennu's chemical signature is shared by other ancient bodies.

With the Bennu analysis now complete, Schönbächler is looking ahead to another opportunity to examine material from the early Solar System.

Japan's space agency, JAXA, is preparing a sample-return mission to Phobos, one of Mars' two moons. The mission is scheduled to launch at the end of October 2026, with the goal of collecting material from the Martian moon and bringing it back to Earth.

"It would be exciting to also obtain this material to analyse it in my laboratory," she says.

Schönbächler plans to apply to JAXA for access to the samples. However, the scientific community will need to be patient. The capsule carrying material from Phobos is not expected to return to Earth until 2031.

Those future samples could provide another important piece of the puzzle, helping researchers determine how different regions of the early Solar System exchanged material and ultimately produced the planets, moons, and asteroids we observe today.

Materials provided by ETH Zurich. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#767. White Dwarfs Are Actually…Yellow, Green, Pink, and Purple?

📅 Oct 24, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

The universe hosts a myriad of star types, including supergiants, giants, main sequence, red dwarfs, brown dwarfs, white dwarfs, and neutron stars. What makes these stars stand out are just their sizes, but their colors, as each one hosts a distinct color based on their surface temperature, specifically orange, red, blue, and white based on cooler to hotter temperatures. While the correlation between star colors and surface temperatures has long been known throughout astronomy, what if star colors could change based on specific criteria, including their atmosphere?

Now, a team of researchers led by the University of Warwick might have introduced a new way to literally look at stars, as they revealed new evidence for white dwarfs to exhibit colors other than what their name implies. These findings were recently published in RAS Techniques and Instruments and could provide new insights into the formation and evolution of stars throughout the cosmos. This is because stars are referred to as âperfect radiatorsâ, also called blackbody objects, meaning they absorb all electromagnetic radiation and reflect nothing. In a nutshell, their glow is entirely dependent on temperature.

âAs an object gets hotter it will emit more light at optical wavelengths, changing from red to orange (think of a blacksmith working on a piece of hot metal),â Dr. Mark Hollands, who is a postdoctoral researcher at the University of Warwick and the studyâs lead author, tells Universe Today. âEventually these orange hues will become paler, and by 6000K a black body will appear will just appear white as most of its radiation will spread across optical wavelengths. For even hotter temperatures an object will start to glow pale blue as the majority of its light will become emitted in the ultraviolet. This set of red/orange/white/blue are the only colors that are possible for a true blackbody.â

For the study, the researchers used a series of computer models to study 151 white dwarfs with 20 parsecs (65 light-years) from Earth and how white dwarfs might challenge the longstanding understanding of stars being blackbodies. The reason white dwarfs were chosen was due to their extreme characteristics compared to main-sequence stars like our Sun, most notably white dwarfs having much denser atmospheres. The researchers aspired to learn if the presence of certain elements like carbon, calcium, or sodium in their dense atmospheres could influence the glow of white dwarfs.

In the end, the researchers found that a total of five white dwarfs of the 151 sampled showed that a combination of a white dwarfâs dense atmosphere with certain elements could cause these five white dwarfs to glow pink and yellow, specifically two yellow and three pink. This indicates about 3.3 percent of white dwarfs in the cosmos could exhibit different colors aside from white.

A white dwarf whose atmosphere is comprise of carbon appearing pink. (Credit: Mark A. Garlick/University of Warwick)

âOne in thirty might not sound like a huge fraction, but across the entire population of white dwarfs in the Universe, that is still a lot of oddly colored stars out there,â said Dr. Boris GÃ¤nsicke, who is a professor in the Department of Physics at the University of Warwick and a co-author on the study. âYet if we take the median across a representative population of white dwarfs, we found that the average white dwarf really is white."

Regarding the specific elements responsible for altering the white dwarfâs colors, the researchers found that carbon causes white dwarfs to appear pink or purple while cooler white dwarfs with hydrogen and helium tend to be cyan or blue. Additionally, the researchers found that white dwarfs whose atmospheres have debris from their original planetary system are typically green.

As noted, star colors are typically determined by their surface temperatures, with hotter stars to cooler stars ranging in several colors, including blue, blue-white, white, yellowish white, yellow (our Sun), orange, and red. The larger the star, the hotter and bluer it is. But while this study demonstrates there could be exemptions to this longstanding notion, could other stars also exhibit unexpected colors?

âThe other class of object that can disobey the rule are brown dwarfs," Dr. Hollands tells Universe Today. "These are substellar objects that are too cool to fuse hydrogen in their cores, but can fuse deuterium instead. Because these are quite cold objects (compared to normal stars) most of their radiation is in the infrared, but some still comes out at optical wavelengths. And because brown dwarfs also can have dense atmospheres, absorption from sodium, potassium, and molecules like titanium oxide can also alter their colors away from blackbody colors. Some astronomers who study brown dwarfs have looked into their appearance in the past and concluded that they can appear violet in color!â

What new insights into white dwarfs and other star colors will researchers make in the coming years and decades? Only time will tell, and this is why we science!

As always, keep doing science & keep looking up!

#776. 27 Exoplanet Targets for the World's First Private Space Telescope

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

The Lazuli Space Telescope will be the first private space telescope. It's the scientific child of Eric Schmidt, Google's former CEO. It will have a 3-meter mirror, larger than the Hubble's 2.4 meter mirror, and will be launched by 2029.

Lazuli will have three instruments: a wide-field optical imager, an integral field spectrograph, and the ExtraSolar Coronagraph (ESC).

New research proposes a list of targets for the ESC. It's titled "The first selection of optimal targets for the Lazuli ExtraSolar Coronagraphâs reflected starlight exoplanet campaign," and is available at arxiv.org. The authors are Logan Pearce from the Department of Astronomy at the University of Michigan, and Ewan Douglas from the Steward Observatory at the University of Arizona.

"Direct detection and characterization of exoplanet atmospheres via reflected starlight enables probes of composition of mature exoplanet systems (since flux is decoupled from age) at closer separations than via thermal emission (due to observations in visible wavelengths), and even probes of biosignatures," the authors write.

An illustration of the Lazuli Space Telescope. It will have a 3-meter mirror and three instruments. One of them is the ExtraSolar Coronagraph (ESC). The ESC will allow observations of exoplanets and their atmosphere with reflected starlight, rather than by their thermal emissions. Image Credit: Schmidt Sciences

The telescope's ESC will be especially important in this context. Characterizing exoplanet atmospheres in mature exoplanet systems with reflected starlight means the observations will not depend on an exoplanet's leftover heat from formation. Since older planets have aged, their leftover heat makes them dimmer over time. By using reflected starlight, the ESC will bypass that effect. It will also allow observations at closer separations than an infrared telescope, meaning it can observe planets closer to their stars, because planets closer to their stars reflect more light. (That's a simplified picture that ignores some details.)

"To date all exoplanets have been imaged through their self-luminous thermal emission, but direct, spatially resolved imaging of planets via reflected starlight is on the near horizon," the authors write. "Reflected light imaging enables detection and characterization of planets decoupled from age (since reflected starlight luminosity does not decrease with age), and the smaller resolution limits provided by imaging shorter wavelengths (â¼0.5â2Î¼m), where the star luminosity peaks, allows access to closer-in planets compared to thermal emission."

The researchers present a list of 27 targets for observations with the ESC, and all of them are giant exoplanets. "Our target list spans ages and temperatures of planets not probed before by direct imaging," the authors write, adding that some of them will also be accessible to the ELT's METIS thermal imager and the Nancy Grace Roman Space Telescope's coronagraphic instrument. The targets will benefit from synergies created by multiple observations, and that will "provide comprehensive atmosphere characterization."

The panel on the left shows a Gaia color-magnitude diagram showing the effective temperature (colormap) and spectral type of the host stars for the 27 planets on the list. Only one is an M-dwarf. The panel on the right shows the sky locations of the stars. Image Credit: Logan Pearce and Ewan Douglas 2026.

While observations with the ESC will be valuable, and open a new pathway to observing potential biosignatures, there are some restraints. In particular, the required exposure times. "The high contrasts involved in reflected light detection (ðªâ10â7â10-9) and subsequent long exposure times (10sâ100s of hours) make uninformed searches challenging for a 3m class observatory," the authors explain. "Campaign efficiency can be dramatically improved by targeting known planets and leveraging well constrained orbital priors in order to prioritize targets and time observations for the maximum likelihood of detection."

The list of targets is based on exoplanets with well-known orbits, since that will make the best use of observing time.

Those interested in exoplanets may recognize some of the 27 giant exoplanets on the list:

This artist's illustration shows Beta Pictoris b, one of the most deeply-studied exoplanets. It was one of the first directly-imaged exoplanets, and orbits a young star that's also well-known because of its debris disk, shown in the illustration. Image Credit: By ESO, CC BY 4.0, https://commons.wikimedia.org/w/index.php?curid=32625365

A private space telescope is something not seen before. It remains to be seen how effective it will be. It also remains to be seen what private ownership might mean, and if that makes it more efficient, or if it introduces unanticipated problems, or both.

Ultimately, it's all about the contribution it makes to exoplanet science.

"Our target list expands direct imaging of gas giant planets into regimes colder than previously imaged, probing mature exoplanet systems, with some overlap with Roman CGI and ELT-METIS imaging, providing more comprehensive atmosphere characterization through synergies with those platform capabilities," the authors conclude.
