#793. The Largest Canyon in the Solar System is Still Tectonically Active

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

For many decades, scientists assumed that Mars was geologically inactive. While its surface is covered in features that attest to its volcanic past, all evidence seemed to indicate that Mars was geologically dead. This is consistent with the fact that Mars' crust has no tectonic plates and instead consists of a single plate, making it a "Stagnant Lid" planet. However, with the arrival of NASA's InSight (Interior Exploration using Seismic Investigations, Geodesy and Heat Transport) lander, indications that the planet was still "alive" began to accumulate.

Beyond "marsquakes" caused by impactors, InSight detected evidence of tectonic activity from within. In a recent study, scientists at Imperial College London and Columbia University presented seismic evidence that a marsquake originated beneath Valles Marineris. According to InSight's data, the event (S0976a) was caused by rocks slipping along fault lines deep within the crust. These findings suggest that faults formed billions of years ago, when Mars was much more active, are unstable enough to rupture under stress.

The research was led by Jung-Hun Song, an Assistant Professor at Imperial College London's Department of Earth Science and Engineering. He was joined by researchers from Columbia's Department of Earth and Environmental Sciences and the Department of Earth Science and Environmental Change at the University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign. The study describing their findings was published in Nature Communications.

One of two Mars InSight's 7-foot (2.2 meters) wide solar panels was imaged by the lander's Instrument Deployment Camera. Credits: NASA/JPL-Caltech

Like Olympus Mons and the Tharsis region, Valles Marineris is the Solar System's largest geological feature of its kind, dwarfing similar features on Earth. The vast canyon system spans 3,900 km (2,423 mi) long, up to 600 km (373 mi) wide, and more than 9 km (5.5 mi) deep. Meanwhile, the largest Canyon system on Earth, the Yarlung Tsangpo Grand Canyon, stretches roughly 505 km (313.5 mi) long, up to 113 m (371 ft) wide, and reaches a maximum depth of 6 km (3.7 mi). As Song explained:

Valles Marineris is one of the most spectacular geological structures anywhere in the Solar System, but until now we didn't know for certain that its faults are still active. Our study opens up exciting possibilities for discovering ongoing seismic activity on other planetary bodies that we might otherwise consider geologically inactive.

The most widely accepted theory is that the valley was formed by rift faults created by tensional tectonic forces that pulled the crust apart. This process is believed to be related to the intense volcanic activity that formed the Tharsis Bulge during the Noachian to Late Hesperian periods (4.1 to 3.0 billion years ago). It is also theorized that the canyon was enlarged by subsequent erosion and the collapse of the rift walls, similar to the East African Rift.

For their study, the team analyzed the S0976a seismic event that was recorded by InSight on August 25th, 2021. This was the only marsquake detected in Mars's western hemisphere, and it originated on the opposite side of the planet. Previous studies placed the marsquake near the western part of Valles Marineris. However, its cause was unclear, with possibilities ranging from meteoroid impacts to landslides and other forms of mass movement common to Mars.

Tharsis Montes, with Olympus Mons (upper left) and Valles Marineris (right). Credit: NASA/JPL-Caltech

The team built models to test these explanations, but they couldn't reproduce the observed seismic waves. The researchers also consulted satellite images of the region taken by NASA's Mars Reconnaissance Orbiter (MRO) after the event, but found no evidence of an impact crater or landslides. The best fit was a strike-slip marsquake, in which blocks of crust move horizontally past each other along a steep fault about 20 to 40 km (12 to 24 mi) beneath the valley. Said Song:

What makes the result compelling is that several completely different types of evidence point in the same direction. The seismic waves tell us that there was a sideways sliding motion on a fault well below the surface, while the geology seen from orbit shows a region crossed by long-lived fault systems.

The results also match what scientists would expect if pre-existing fault structures slipped due to long-term cooling, volcanic stresses, and local effects associated with the canyon itself. They also add to the list of regions where InSight detected evidence of active faulting on modern-day Mars. This includes the Cerberus Fossae fissures and the southern part of Elysium Planitia, which suggest that stresses in the Martian crust do not follow a single planet-wide pattern.

Lastly, the results show that four years after the mission ended because of dust buildup on its solar panels, InSight continues to provide valuable insight into the Red Planet. Said Dr. Doyeon Kim, an Assistant Professor in Earth and Planetary Science at Imperial and a senior author on the study:

S0976a has turned out to be an extraordinary event, playing an important role in our understanding of the Red Planet. Its waves traveled through the core and helped us investigate the deepest part of Mars, and now they are telling us what happened at the source itself. From a single marsquake, we can learn both about the center of the planet and about an active fault beneath one of its most remarkable landscapes.

Further Reading: Imperial College London, Nature Communications.

His work is featured in The Ross 248 Project and Interstellar Travel edited by NASA alumni Les Johnson and Ken Roy. He also hosts the podcast series Stories from Space on Patreon. #801. Saudi Arabia Joins the Search for Alien Civilizations

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

The Kingdom of Saudi Arabia is joining in the technological effort to detect signals from other civilizations, called SETI. The SETI (Search for Extraterrestrial Intelligence) Institute, a non-profit body, uses the Allen Telescope Array and other ground-based facilities to search for alien signals. They've also used the Hubble and other space telescopes.

LaserSETI is a new network of optical detectors under development since 2024. AlUla, in Saudi Arabia's northwestern region, is the fifth LaserSETI site, and the first one in Asia. The others are in California, Hawaii, Puerto Rico, and Arizona.

LaserSETI brings new capabilities to the SETI endeavour. It can watch the entire sky continuously, catching rare transient events. Most of SETI's other efforts are slower, or target specific targets in the sky.

Most of what LaserSETI detects will just be flashes of light from events in the cosmos. But, the hope is, it will one day detect a laser originating from another ETI. The chances of it doing that increase with sky coverage, which is why LaserSETI is expanding. As the first one in Asia, Saudi Arabia's two detectors are important.

âAlUla gives LaserSETI an important new geographic vantage point and connects this remarkable astronomical site to a growing global network." - Dr. Franck Marchis, SETI.

They're placed in Alula Manara, which is being developed as Saudi Arabia's first major astronomical observatory. Alula is also the kingdom's first UNESCO World Heritage Site, and has hosted multiple civilizations from the Neolithic through the Roman times and the Ottoman times. Alula is also a Dark Sky Park, and will eventually host a 4-meter telescope and a pair of 2-meter telescopes, if plans are successful.

Alula is about 380 kilometers from the holy city Medina (Madinah). Alula Manara, the astronomical site where the LaserSETI devices are situated is on a remote plateau about 74 km north of Alula. Image Credit: Google Earth/LandSat/Copernicus. Data SIO, NOAA, US Navy, NGA, GEBCO, Mapa GISrael.

âTwo years ago, we announced our intention to bring LaserSETI to AlUla. Today, the instruments are installed, commissioned and observing the sky,â said Dr. Franck Marchis, Senior Planetary Astronomer and Director of Citizen Science at the SETI Institute. âAlUla gives LaserSETI an important new geographic vantage point and connects this remarkable astronomical site to a growing global network. This is exactly what LaserSETI needs: instruments distributed around the world, observing continuously and together.â

The LaserSETI detectors are not overly-complicated devices. They use cameras with commercially-available lenses that can image about 75 degrees of the sky. The solid-state detectors are also off-the-shelf units. A grating behind the lens will differentiate between starlightâwhich produces a complete light spectrum from blue to redâand lasers, which only show up as a characteristic wavelength, like laser pointers do.

Cost is a critical factor in LaserSETI, and along with using commercially available components, parts of the detectors are also 3-D printed.

Only LaserSETI's transmission grating and stainless-steel enclosure are custom made, as shown in this image. Image Credit: SETI/LaserSETI

Each LaserSETI will also have a powerful computer that will analyze incoming data in real-time. Not only will that eliminate expensive data storage, it will allow real-time alerts.

The pair of detectors in AlUla were installed August 19, 2026 and are now fully operational.

âLaserSETI was designed from the beginning as a global observatory rather than a single telescope,â said Eliot Gillum, Director of LaserSETI at the SETI Institute. Gillum also designed and builds the LaserSETI units. âAdding AlUla extends the network into an entirely new geographic region. Every new location improves our ability to monitor the sky continuously and to compare observations of unusual events from different parts of the world.â

âThe commissioning of LaserSETI at AlUla Manara marks an important milestone in our collaboration with SETI Institute, translating our shared ambition into scientific capability on the ground,â said Naif Al Malik, AlUla Manara Executive Program Head. âBy connecting AlUlaâs exceptional dark skies to a global observation network, we are creating new opportunities for scientific research, international collaboration and knowledge exchange, while strengthening AlUlaâs growing role in astronomy and scientific discovery.â

For most of its history, Saudi Arabia has been known as a closed, conservative society with no separation between church and state. A place where science took a backseat to pretty much everything else. It hosts Islam's two holiest cities, Mecca and Medina, the site of pilgrimages by the Islamic faithful. Its weath, and its influence, comes entirely from oil.

But in recent decades and years, the desert kingdom has made a number of changes. It's sought to host international sporting and cultural events. It's also moved to use some of its extraordinary wealth in scientific endeavours. In 2009, it founded the King Abdullah University of Science and Technology (KAUST). Among other things, it hosted the fastest supercomputer in the Middle East. KAUST is also the highest-ranked university in the Arab World.

Hosting LaserSETI is part of a new direction for Saudi Arabia, and the upcoming telescopes that will join it at Alula will be part of its scientific future. As Universe Today readers likely agree, more science can only be good for Saudi Arabia. If, one day, Alula's LaserSETI were to play a role in detecting an ETI, that would be a culture-changing triumph.

Interested readers can check out the live status of each LaserSETI observatory here.

#729. Treasure Is Finally Rising From the ‘Holy Grail of Shipwrecks.’ Someone May Have Gotten There First.

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

The hauling up of the world’s most valuable shipwreck had to start somewhere. So crews brought to the surface three small gold and bronze coins from the 1700s, pulled from the Spanish galleon the San José, considered the Holy Grail of all shipwrecks. But there’s plenty more still sitting 1,970 feet below the ocean’s surface off the coast of Colombia—as much as $17 billion worth.

And now, nearly a year after those first pieces broke the surface, more questions than answers are emerging from the wreck. The biggest one is: has somebody been down there already?

The San José, sunk by the British Royal Navy in the Caribbean during the War of Spanish Succession in 1708, was full of value. The ship was laden with 10 years’ worth of gold, silver, and gems—tribute from Spanish colonies in Latin America headed back to the Spanish king. The Colombian Ministry of Culture, Arts, and Knowledge used a robotic underwater vehicle to secure the first samples of that cargo in late 2025, picking up three coins, a bronze cannon (along with fragments of wood, rope, and sediment), and two porcelain cups—each with a different style—without disturbing the structure of the galleon itself.

Alhena Caicedo Fernández, director of the Colombian Institute of Anthropology and History (ICANH), said in a translated statement that collecting the objects “opens the possibility that citizens can approach, through material testimony, the history of the San José galleon.”Not everyone sees it that way.

Earlier this year, a Colombian oversight group dedicated to the country’s submerged cultural heritage issued an open letter to the nation’s attorney general, alleging looting and unauthorized interventions at the wreck site in 2016 and 2022—and claiming the site’s coordinates, officially a state secret since the government announced its discovery in 2015, have been disclosed. The group’s suspicions trace partly to an analysis by a University of Southampton deep-sea archaeology expert, who compared imagery of the wreck from 2015 and 2016 and found “selective areas with sediment removal.” Even the rope fragment recovered with the cannon sparked a fight: the watchdog’s director called it “most likely modern,” while the research team countered that rope has survived on far older wrecks, including a 12th-century Viking vessel. ICANH has repeatedly denied any looting.

Other experts questioned why the extraction happened at all. One Colombian archaeologist who has challenged the recovery plan since 2018 said “no scientific reasons were given” for pulling up the artifacts, while the research team maintains the objects help determine the site’s conservation through physicochemical studies.

Then there’s the custody battle—or battles, really. Current estimates of the on-board value have ranged from $10 billion to $17 billion in today’s currency, sparking competing claims from the Spanish government, which considers the galleon a sovereign warship and war grave, and Bolivia’s Indigenous Qhara Qhara nation, whose ancestors mined much of the silver aboard. The biggest legal fight is happening at the Permanent Court of Arbitration in The Hague, where U.S.-based salvage company Sea Search Armada—which claims it located the galleon back in the early 1980s—is seeking $10 billion, roughly half the cargo’s value. Colombia disputes that the company ever found the real site. A ruling against Colombia could mean the most expensive treasure recovery in history will end up costing the country more than it winds up getting from the wreck.

But whoever gets custody of the San José probably won’t come up totally empty-handed. It’s estimated that 200 tons of gold, silver, and uncut gemstones were aboard the ship, the result of 10 years of taxation saved up before the fleet’s planned voyage back to Spain. The macuquinas—known as cobs in English—were the main currency in the Americas at the time, typically cut from gold or silver ingots, and were the primary way the Spanish transported large volumes of wealth from the Americas to Europe. These coin hoards likely formed part of the royal treasure dispatched from Peru.

But when the fleet of 18 ships left Cartagena bound for Spain on June 8, 1708, it was attacked by five British warships. The cannon battle resulted in the explosion of the San José’s gunpowder stores, sinking the 150-foot-long ship and nearly 600 sailors with it.

The shipwreck—and its treasured cargo—was thought to have sat untouched on the ocean floor ever since. Now, bit by bit, what’s left of it is reaching the surface once more. But as the legal fight over its ownership rises faster than the artifacts themselves, the real tragedy may be that the world’s greatest sunken treasure has already been plundered.

Tim Newcomb is a journalist based in the Pacific Northwest. He covers stadiums, sneakers, gear, infrastructure, and more for a variety of publications, including Popular Mechanics. His favorite interviews have included sit-downs with Roger Federer in Switzerland, Kobe Bryant in Los Angeles, and Tinker Hatfield in Portland.

#808. Magnetic Field Reversals and Extinctions: The Correlation Won't Go Away.

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Is there a link between Earth's geomagnetic reversals and extinctions? Nobody has proven it, despite their efforts. But the idea just won't go away.

The Earth's magnetic poles haven't remained consistent over the planet's 4.5 billion year history. It's experienced multitudes of pole flips, or geomagnetic reversals, where its north and south poles swap positions.

Scientists rely on paleomagnetism to detect them. Paleomagnetism detects the orientiation of Earth's poles across geological time in lava and sediments. The orientation of magnetic minerals in lava and ancient sediments record the locations of the poles at the time they solidified or accumulated.

Scientists think reversals are triggered by changes in the heat that flows across Earth's core-mantle boundary, but finding a pattern in geomagnetic reversals is a daunting task. They seem to occur about once every 450,000 years; evidence shows 183 of them in the last 83 million years. The last one was about 780,000 years ago, so by that measure, we're overdue for the next one But the 450,000-year average is kind of a mathetmatical abstraction. There's no indication of a periodic cycle.

Determining when a reversal happened can be difficult, and determining how long the reversal took to complete is likewise challenging. Predicting one is next to impossible.

Lava and sediments present a tangled history that needs to be painstakingly untangled. Deciphering the history of these reversals is made more difficult because sometimes there are long periods of stability punctuated by shorter periods of rapid multiple reversals. To add to that, individual reversals are chaotic events.

These images are form a NASA simulation of geomagnetic reversals. Simulations show that reversals aren't simple, tidy affairs. Instead, they can be a chaos of magnetic field lines. Image Credit: Public Domain, https://commons.wikimedia.org/w/index.php?curid=1546763

New research in the National Science Review presents evidence of one period of geomagnetic chaos that the authors say could be tied to an extinction. It's titled "Geomagnetic instability as a potential deep-Earth forcing factor in the Permian environmental and biotic crisis," and the lead author is Min Zhang. Zhang is from the Key Laboratory of Planetary Science and Frontier Technology at the Institute of Geology and Geophysics, at the Chinese Academy of Sciences in Beijing.

"The Permian witnessed profound geomagnetic, environmental, and biotic upheavals, yet high-resolution paleomagnetic constraints across critical transitions remain sparse," the authors write. In this work, the authors present a magnetostratigraphic record from what's called the Fengshan section in South China that covers 3.75 million years. The record spans what's called the Guadalupian-Lopingian transition (end-Guadalupian extinction), which occurred from 262.5-258.75 Mya.

"It yields extreme geomagnetic instability, characterized by 29 magnetozones and an unprecedented reversal frequency of 11 reversals/Myr â one of the highest records in Earthâs history," the researchers explain.

"These features are temporally correlated with the end-Guadalupian extinction (â¼261 Ma), suggesting a potential deep-Earth force contributing to surface ecosystem collapse, though the causation remains unproven," the authors write. It's tempting to tie geomagnetic reversals in with extinctions. So far, that's unproven, but the implication has staying power. "This implies that, together with other global forcing factors, geomagnetic instability might have influenced late Permian climatic and biotic upheavals," the researchers write.

Not only did this period see frequent reversals and an extinction, but the appearance of Large Igneous Provinces (LIP) were also altering Earth's atmosphere. LIPs are associated with mantle plumes and coincide with extinctions and rapid changes in Earth's climate. LIPs are wholly unlike any modern type of volcanic activity, supporting the idea that they're causal to extinctions.

Adding to the idea is that the Earth's magnetic shield may weaken during pole reversals. This could let in more radiation, but that isn't clear. "The geomagnetic instability may have amplified upper atmospheric ionization through increased flux of galactic cosmic rays and solar wind particles, potentially promoting ozone depletion, increasing atmospheric oxygen loss, and elevating ultraviolet radiation (UVR) on Earthâs surface," the authors write. But most scientists seem to question this idea.

There's a lot of information packed into this figure from the research, but it merits a careful look. The x-axis on the bottom shows age across all of the individual figures. In (a) Polarity reversal frequency is shown with a blue line and Paleointensity data is also shown. The y-axis shows the frequency of geomagnetic reversals. (b) shows volcanic activity, with dark red denoting the major phase of large igneous provinces (LIPs). (c) shows sea-level fluctuations, along with the configuration of the supercontinent Pangaea. The blue line again shows polarity reversals. (d) shows the profile for atmospheric carbon. (e) shows species diversity trends and the End-Guadalupian mass extinction and End-Permian mass extinctions. It's hard to ignore the correlations. Image Credit: Zhang et al. 2026. National Science Review, nwag647.

If geomagnetic reversals, LIPs, and extinctions all roughly coincide, there could be a causal link. The period of frequent reversals identified in the research could be the key to it.

11 geomagnetic reversals per million years indicates that something remarkable was going on inside Earth. "This observation indicates a period of geodynamo instability, which may have been associated to various deep Earth processes. The temporal association of deep Earth processes, geomagnetic instability with major environmental and biotic changes is intriguing," the authors write.

The correlation between these factors certainly deserves more study, that much is obvious. But is it too soon to declare causality? Yup.

"Our findings highlight the potential strong influence of deep-Earth processes, and geomagnetic variations, on Permian surface conditions and biotic crisis," the authors conclude.

#785. Reality Might Be ‘Leaking’ Into a Fifth Dimension—And Dark Energy Could Be the Smoking Gun, Scientists Say

📅 Oct 22, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

If we find that gravity starts acting weird at the scale of a micron, it could trigger a process of discovery that could finally explain the biggest mysteries of the cosmos.

Here’s what you’ll learn when you read this story:

Imagine you are standing in a dark, quiet kitchen at midnight. You’re reaching for a glass of water, confident that the counter is where it’s always been, that the floor is solid, and that the air around you is, well, just air. You feel like you have a pretty good handle on how the room—and the world—works.

But what if there was a trapdoor? Not a physical one in the linoleum, but a hidden dimension tucked into the very fabric of space, no larger than a speck of dust, yet governing everything you see.

That’s a good analogy for a cosmic puzzle that scientists have been trying to solve for decades. It stems from the fact that our best approximation of how the universe works just doesn’t fit with reality. On one hand, we have the standard model, our remarkably successful “instruction manual” for subatomic particles. On the other, we have dark energy—that mysterious, ghostly pressure pushing the universe to expand faster and faster. The problem is that our math says dark energy should be enormous, but our telescopes show it’s incredibly tiny. It’s a mismatch of epic proportions.

So, how do we fix a universe model that doesn’t add up?

In a paper published on arXiv in September 2024, researchers Ignatios Antoniadis, PhD, from Sorbonne University and Dieter Lüst, PhD, from the Max Planck Institute have proposed a radical “top-down” explanation. They aren’t just tweaking the dials; they are suggesting that our entire four-dimensional reality is actually “leaking” into a fifth dimension.

They call it the “Dark Dimension” scenario.

In the world of string theory—our best guess at a “theory of everything”—we usually imagine extra dimensions as being curled up so tight they are practically invisible, like the thickness of a thread viewed from miles away. But Antoniadis and Lüst suggest something different. Using a framework called “weakly coupled string theory,” they’ve derived a model where one of these extra dimensions is actually quite large.

That might sound small, but in the world of particle physics, a micron—the size of a single bacterium—is gargantuan. It’s a mesoscopic scale, a middle ground between the quantum and the cosmic. If this dimension exists, it’s like finding out your studio apartment actually has a massive, hidden ballroom just behind the wallpaper.

This is called “UV-IR mixing.” Usually, we assume that the physics of the very small (the ultraviolet) and the physics of the very large (the infrared) don’t talk to each other. They stay in their own lanes. But the authors argue that in quantum gravity, these scales are tethered together. The tiny value of dark energy we observe is actually a signal—a breadcrumb trail—pointing directly to the existence of this hidden, micron-sized dimension.

It is a bold claim, but the math is compelling. By looking at how strings “slosh” around on a worldsheet (the two-dimensional surface a string traces through space), the researchers found that if you want to keep the universe stable and explain dark energy without “fine-tuning”—which is physicist-speak for "fudging the numbers"—you almost inevitably end up with this dark dimension.

If this fifth dimension is real, it changes everything about our future in the stars. It means that gravity isn’t just a weak force; it’s a force that is “diluted” because it’s spreading out into that extra space. It also suggests that there is a “tower” of light particles—a cosmic ladder of new physics—waiting for us just beyond our current reach.

Experimentalists are already looking for “short-range” gravity deviations. If we find that gravity starts acting weird at the scale of a micron, we’ll know Antoniadis and Lüst are onto something. We would be looking at the first potential physical proof of string theory.

But for now, it remains a beautiful, mathematically rigorous hypothesis. We are still peering through the keyhole, trying to see the shape of the room beyond.

And that’s the thrill of it. We are finally learning how to ask the right questions. The dark dimension might be a mirage, or it might be the foundation of everything. Who knows what we’ll see when we keep peeking around corners?

Paul M. Sutter is a science educator and a cosmologist at Johns Hopkins University and the author of How to Die in Space: A Journey Through Dangerous Astrophysical Phenomena and Your Place in the Universe: Understanding Our Big, Messy Existence. Sutter is also the host of various science programs, and he’s on social media. Check out his Ask a Spaceman podcast and his YouTube page.

#953. Birds are more afraid of women than men. Scientists have no idea why

📅 Oct 22, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Birds living in cities appear to react differently to men and women, and scientists are struggling to explain why. An international research team discovered that urban birds, including great tits, house sparrows, and blackbirds, tend to fly away sooner when approached by women than by men. The surprising behavior raises new questions about how birds perceive the people around them.

The study took place in five European countries, where male and female participants (matched for height and clothing) walked directly toward birds in city parks and other green spaces.

On average, men were able to approach about one meter closer than women before the birds flew away. The researchers measured this response by recording how close a person could get before a bird took flight (termed flight initiation distance).

Published in the British Ecological Society journal People and Nature, the findings showed the same pattern in Czechia, France, Germany, Poland, and Spain. The difference also appeared across all 37 bird species examined, including easily startled magpies and pigeons that typically allow people to approach much more closely.

The researchers believe their findings indicate that city birds can distinguish between male and female humans. However, they have yet to determine which characteristics the birds recognize or why they appear more cautious around women.

Professor Daniel Blumstein of the University of California, Los Angeles, who helped conduct the research, said the results were convincing despite the lack of an explanation.

"I fully believe our results, that urban birds react differently based on the sex of the person approaching them, but I can't explain them right now. We used bleeding-edge comparative analysis techniques that showed our findings were consistent across cities and species, but we simply don't have a conclusive explanation yet."

Dr. Yanina Benedetti of the Czech University of Life Sciences Prague, another member of the research team, said the discovery challenged her expectations about how birds respond to human observers.

"As a woman in the field, I was surprised that birds reacted to us differently. This study highlights how animals in cities 'see' humans, which has implications for urban ecology and equality in science. Many behavioral studies assume that a human observer is neutral, but this wasn't the case for urban birds in our study."

Scientists have proposed several possible explanations for the difference. Birds might be responding to chemical signals such as pheromones, variations in body shape, or differences in how men and women walk. For now, however, these ideas remain speculative, and additional experiments will be needed to identify the actual cause.

Dr. Federico Morelli of the University of Turin, a co-author of the study, said the unanswered question is one of the discovery's most fascinating aspects.

"This is maybe the most interesting part of our study," said Dr. Federico Morelli from the University of Turin, a co-author of the study. "We have identified a phenomenon, but we really don't know why. However, what our results do highlight is the birds' sophisticated ability to evaluate their environment."

The findings suggest that urban birds may be sensitive to human characteristics that people themselves rarely notice. Identifying those signals could help researchers better understand how wildlife assesses potential threats in environments shared with humans.

Dr. Benedetti said: "Urban birds clearly react to subtle cues that humans do not easily notice. Follow-up studies could focus on individual factors such as movement patterns, scent cues, or physical traits, testing them separately rather than grouping them under observer sex. This approach would help identify the specific cues birds detect."

More Research Is Needed to Explain the Bird Behavior

Although the investigation included 2,701 observations, the researchers emphasize that their findings are still preliminary. Further studies will be necessary to establish whether the difference between birds' responses to men and women consistently appears in other settings and populations.

For now, the discovery points to an intriguing possibility: Birds sharing our cities may be paying attention to subtle differences between people that scientists have yet to fully understand.

#3929. Scientists discover a surprising secret behind the human brain’s wrinkles

📅 Oct 22, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Most primates have relatively large brains covered in distinctive folds and grooves. However, common marmosets (Callithrix jacchus) are an unusual exception. These small monkeys have brains that are almost entirely smooth, with very little folding. Scientists at the German Primate Center (DPZ), the Leibniz Institute for Primate Research in Göttingen, have now identified several cellular processes that help explain this difference.

Using miniature brain structures known as organoids, the researchers discovered that certain neural progenitor cells, which produce neurons, develop differently in marmosets than in humans. These differences involve the cells' structure, the speed at which they divide, and the timing of their development. Together, these changes reduce the number of neurons produced, contributing to the smaller size and smoother surface of the marmoset brain. Published in Science Advances, the findings provide new insights into primate brain evolution and could eventually improve our understanding of human brain development disorders.

Why Some Primate Brains Are Smooth Instead of Folded

Brain size and the extent of surface folding are important features that distinguish different primate species. The human brain, for example, has a highly folded surface resembling the ridges and grooves of a walnut. This arrangement greatly expands the brain's surface area, creating room for billions of neurons that support complex thinking and other advanced mental abilities.

Scientists believe that the common ancestor of modern primates already possessed a moderately sized brain with at least some folding. Yet not all primates living today retain these characteristics.

Common marmosets (Callithrix jacchus), small monkeys native to South America that are becoming increasingly important in biomedical research, have brains with almost no visible folds. Researchers at the German Primate Center set out to determine why their brains develop so differently from those of humans and other primates.

"We wanted to understand which changes at the cellular level cause the marmoset brain to grow less and form fewer folds," explains Lidiia Tynianskaia, one of the study's two first authors and a PhD student in the Junior Research Group Brain Development and Evolution at the DPZ. "At the beginning of development, the common marmoset brain exhibits the typical structure and composition of a large, folded primate brain. As development progresses, processes must therefore occur that effectively slow down the production of nerve cells."

Scientists Discover What Slows Brain Growth

To investigate these developmental differences, the team compared brain organoids grown from human and common marmoset cells. Their attention centered on neural progenitor cells, the cells responsible for generating neurons during brain development.

These progenitor cells play a major role in determining the eventual size and shape of the brain. The more neurons they produce, the greater the potential for the cerebral cortex to expand and develop folds.

The researchers found that the marmoset's smaller, smoother cerebral cortex is not the result of a single developmental change. Instead, several differences involving distinct types of progenitor cells work together to limit neuron production and brain folding.

"Our investigations have shown that certain progenitor cells in the common marmoset divide significantly more slowly than in humans," explains César Mateo Bastidas Betancourt, also a first author of the study and a PhD student in the Junior Research Group. "Other progenitor cells have a simpler structure than their human counterparts, with fewer processes, and are therefore less proliferative. Both of these factors ultimately result in fewer nerve cells, which contributes to a smaller size and less folding of the cerebral cortex in marmosets."

The scientists also identified important differences in when certain progenitor cell characteristics and behaviors emerge during development. Compared with humans, marmosets have a shorter overall period during which these cells can multiply rapidly.

Together, the slower rate of cell division, simpler cell structures, and shorter window for rapid growth help explain why the marmoset brain produces fewer neurons and develops far fewer folds.

Miniature Brains Reveal Important Developmental Clues

Much of the research relied on brain organoids, small, three-dimensional structures grown from cells that reproduce important features of early brain development. These miniature models allowed scientists to investigate developmental changes across multiple samples and compare the timing of key processes.

"The study combines the advantages of in vivo and in vitro methods," says Michael Heide, head of the Junior Research Group Brain Development and Evolution. "Organoids are well-suited for obtaining statistically robust results because such sample sizes are not feasible in primates. We subsequently repeated some key experiments in fetal brain tissue to confirm the results from the organoids."

One particularly important finding involved the relationship between development in organoids and development in actual marmoset brains. The researchers determined that a marmoset brain organoid grown for 50 days closely matches the developmental stage of a natural marmoset brain at day 90.

Establishing this relationship was essential because brain organoids and actual brains do not necessarily develop at the same rate. By identifying corresponding developmental stages, the scientists could make meaningful comparisons and interpret their experimental findings more reliably.

What Smooth Monkey Brains Reveal About Human Brain Evolution

The findings help explain how relatively small differences in the behavior and developmental timing of brain-building cells can contribute to major differences in brain size and folding among primates.

They also offer a potential explanation for how the wide variety of primate brain shapes and sizes emerged over evolutionary time, including why some species developed relatively smooth brains while others possess much larger, heavily folded ones.

Beyond their evolutionary significance, these discoveries provide a foundation for investigating how the human brain develops and what happens when those processes are disrupted. A better understanding of the cellular mechanisms that control neuron production and brain growth could ultimately help researchers study developmental disorders affecting the human brain.

#2373. Renovation Diaries: The House Envelope and Other Lessons in Efficiency

📅 Oct 22, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

From house tours and expert advice to favorite finds and DIYs.

Design-forward shops, hotels, and restaurants for your itinerary.

Renovating? Start here for expert advice, deep dives, and helpful tips.

Our catalog of more than 10,000 products featured on Remodelista.

Remodelista’s members-only directory of architects, designers, and landscape professionals.

Editor’s note: This is the latest in a series of posts by Sally Kohn— journalist and CNN political commentator, TED talk giver, and design aficionado—chronicling her adventures in remodeling.

Let’s level set. I live in a 1780s barn that was turned into a house in the 1950s, and as far as I can tell the windows and the siding haven’t been changed since. The fact is, many parts of our money pit farmhouse need updating. Some, like the 1950s asphalt roof built directly on top of a much older shake shingle roof, we replaced last year. Other things we’re still waiting on, like the vintage 1950s bathroom tile with vintage crumbling grout. But since we can’t close many of our windows without pushing on them from the outside, and we have an inexplicable leak behind our very ancient siding, we put off the inevitable long enough. It is time for some grand-scale renovations.

Stay tuned for some of the other things we’ll be changing, including a kitchen refresh. But first up, if we’re going to go to all the trouble—and, ahem, expense—of replacing all of our siding and windows, can we make our old home more environmentally responsible in terms of both materials and energy efficiency? Let’s find out.

My guide on this journey is Shannon Pendleton, principal of Sanderson Sustainable Design and co-host of Passive House Accelerator. In addition to being a friend and neighbor, Shannon is a leading expert on how to make homes more sustainable and energy efficient. And while those concepts might immediately make you think of solar panels and the like, Shannon explains that in energy efficiency, an ounce of prevention is worth a pound of cure. In other words, using green energy to power your home is great. But designing your home to need less energy in the first place is even better.

But with apologies to Shannon, when she explained the science of the “building envelope”, all I could think about is that envelope feels like the wrong word. Maybe it should be “building envelop”—as in, how enveloped is your building. Because your “building envelop” is what stops the outside environment from getting in and the inside environment from getting out. That means everything from bugs and dust to wind to heat and cold.

So I’m making two big changes to my “building envelop”, both of which create an opportunity for more energy efficiency. Let’s start with the siding. We have very old, very stained, very falling-apart cedar. I could just replace it with cedar, maybe even sustainably harvested cedar. But the fact is, our old cedar is cupping and splitting. Yes, more maintenance would help, but that’s hard to do with second-story wood. Also I’m lazy. Alternatively, I could have gone with some plastic product or chemically treated wood but, like I said, we care about the environment and also want to preserve the look and feel of our historic home. So we’re going with Thermory siding.

Thermory takes sustainably forested wood—in our case, we’re using pine—and puts it through an intense heat-treating process that makes the wood more stable and rot-resistant. It’s an updated twist on the Japanese shou sugi ban process, where wood is charred to make it water and rot resistant. Thermory is that, without the blackening (though they actually do have a product that mirrors the shou sugi ban look, but with the added benefits of Thermory’s advanced thermal modification). In other words, Thermory gives us everything we like about natural wood plus all these added benefits.

Using a more stable rot-resistant siding definitely envelops our house better (take that, flying squirrels), but the energy efficiency upgrades come from what will be behind the siding. Our contractor—the wonderful, lovable, fantastic-to-work-with John Gemmi and his company, Gemmi Construction—were already planning to weather-seal behind the siding, and of course put new insulation behind that where we find any lacking. But Shannon suggested installing “a rain screen, a continuous air barrier, and continuous exterior insulation” to give us the most enveloped envelope possible. Ultimately, we settled on Zip R sheathing, which does all three of those things in one system. So that’s our plan.

As for the windows, well….The most energy-efficient option is triple-pane glass, but in our case, since we’re replacing a gajillion windows, we just couldn’t make it work in our budget. We have two walls of windows in our great room, most of which are currently fixed panes that we’re excited to replace with operable awning windows. We looked into doing triple-pane for just those windows, but that would have made the windows too heavy to open. So yes, we’re choosing a little less efficiency there but counting on the fact that having new windows that don’t require a screwdriver and grunts to close will nevertheless up our efficiency. Plus, thanks to Ambiance Design, we’re adding retractable Duette Honeycomb shades over those large window walls, which will give us some extra energy efficiency on super hot days.

Niece Lumber helped us pick out Lincoln Windows, a family run U.S.-manufactured brand with two advantages. First, we could get retractable screens which we’re very excited about. And second, they offer custom colors on their exteriors. You’ll have to stay tuned to figure out what color choice we’ve made. It’s gonna be…dramatic.

Work on the siding and window part of the renovation is starting soon. I’ll be posting updates on my Instagram (@sallykohn), plus more updates along the way here as we get into our kitchen renovations, including the most sustainable and cool countertop and our very locally made and custom color stove. Watch this space!

#3957. Why some people escape Alzheimer’s despite carrying a high-risk gene

📅 Oct 19, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Some people carry one of the strongest genetic risk factors for Alzheimer's disease yet never develop it, remaining mentally sharp well into their 80s. For years, scientists have wondered what protects these individuals despite their increased genetic vulnerability.

Now, researchers at Columbia University have uncovered a biological mechanism that could help explain this remarkable resistance. Their findings point to a rare genetic mutation that protects the brain's blood vessels from damage, potentially opening a new path toward Alzheimer's treatments.

The discovery builds on research the team published two years ago involving APOE ε4, a gene variant strongly associated with Alzheimer's risk. The scientists found that some people carrying APOE ε4 also possess a rare protective mutation in the fibronectin (FN1) gene.

Fibronectin is a protein that helps support the structure of tissues. However, in people with APOE ε4, excessive amounts can accumulate around blood vessels in the brain, potentially compromising a critical protective system known as the blood-brain barrier. The rare FN1 mutation appears to limit this buildup and the damage associated with it.

That earlier discovery suggested an intriguing possibility: What if scientists could develop a treatment that recreates the protection provided by this rare mutation?

A new study published in Nature Aging brings that possibility closer by identifying how excess fibronectin damages the brain's protective barrier and revealing potential ways to interrupt the process.

"This new study takes us from a genetic clue to a disease mechanism," says Caghan Kizil, the study's corresponding author and professor of neurological sciences in the Department of Neurology and the Taub Institute for Research on Alzheimer's Disease and the Aging Brain at Columbia University Vagelos College of Physicians and Surgeons.

"We knew that changes in fibronectin could protect against Alzheimer's, but we didn't know why excess fibronectin was harmful in the first place. This study gives us that detailed mechanism -- and with it, ideas for how to reproduce that natural protection."

How an Alzheimer's Risk Gene Weakens the Blood-Brain Barrier

The blood-brain barrier serves as a protective boundary between the bloodstream and the brain. Made up of tightly connected cells surrounding the brain's blood vessels, it carefully regulates which substances can enter brain tissue while helping maintain conditions necessary for removing potentially harmful waste.

When this barrier becomes damaged or excessively permeable, substances that would normally be kept out can enter the brain more easily. Such disruptions may contribute to inflammation and other processes involved in neurological diseases, including Alzheimer's.

To investigate the role of fibronectin, the Columbia team combined several advanced research approaches. They examined human brain tissue and cerebrospinal fluid, the clear liquid surrounding the brain and spinal cord, using genetic and epigenetic analyses to explore changes in DNA and gene activity.

Their investigation also included single-cell sequencing, which reveals the activity of individual cells, and spatial imaging to examine where biological changes occur within tissues. Additional experiments involved human stem-cell models, three-dimensional cultures of blood vessels, and studies in zebrafish and mice.

Despite the variety of methods, the findings consistently pointed to the same mechanism: APOE ε4 promotes the production and accumulation of fibronectin around the brain's blood vessels.

In mice engineered to carry human APOE ε4, researchers measured nearly twice as much fibronectin in the brain as in mice carrying APOE ε3, another common version of the gene. The greater accumulation was accompanied by evidence that the blood-brain barrier had become leaky.

Scientists Discover How Fibronectin Causes Brain Damage

One experiment provided especially important evidence that fibronectin does more than simply accumulate in damaged blood vessels.

The researchers increased human fibronectin specifically in astrocytes, specialized support cells that help maintain the blood-brain barrier and keep the brain functioning properly.

Increasing fibronectin in these cells was enough to disrupt the barrier, allowing it to become more permeable. Conversely, reducing fibronectin improved the barrier's ability to function normally.

These results provided experimental evidence that excess fibronectin can directly contribute to blood-brain barrier damage rather than merely appearing alongside it.

"The animal experiments allowed us to move beyond association," says co-first author Prabesh Bhattarai, an associate research scientist in the Kizil lab. "When we increased fibronectin in astroglia, the barrier became permeable. When we reduced fibronectin, barrier function improved."

The team then investigated the molecular events responsible for the damage.

They found that excessive fibronectin interferes with communication between cells by activating abnormal signals through integrins, proteins that help cells sense and respond to their surroundings, and an enzyme known as focal adhesion kinase.

These abnormal signals disrupt a network of growth factors called VEGF, HBEGF, and IGF1. Although growth factors are often associated with cell development and repair, these particular signaling molecules also help astrocytes and blood vessel cells coordinate their activities to maintain a healthy blood-brain barrier.

When their communication becomes disrupted, the barrier can lose its integrity, potentially leaving brain tissue more vulnerable to damage.

"Single-cell analysis allowed us to examine this pathway in the different cells that maintain the brain's blood vessels," says co-first author Elanur Yilmaz, an associate research scientist in the Kizil lab. "We observed the same biological pattern across experimental models and human Alzheimer's tissue, making the mechanism especially compelling."

Evidence From Human Brains Strengthens the Alzheimer's Connection

Importantly, the researchers found signs of similar biological disturbances in human samples, suggesting that the process identified in laboratory experiments may also contribute to Alzheimer's disease in people carrying APOE ε4.

Analyses of brain tissue and cerebrospinal fluid showed that elevated fibronectin levels were associated with inflammation in astrocytes.

Genetic and gene-regulation analyses also connected FN1 to blood vessel disease and changes involving VEGFA, a growth factor that plays an important role in maintaining healthy brain blood vessels.

Together, these findings provide multiple lines of evidence linking excess fibronectin to the vascular damage associated with APOE ε4.

"The convergence of these independent human datasets strengthens the case that fibronectin is part of the APOE ε4-related vascular disease process," says Badri Vardarajan, associate professor of neurological science in the Department of Neurology, Gertrude H. Sergievsky Center, and Taub Institute, who collaborated on this discovery of the FN1 mutation.

A Rare Genetic Defense Could Inspire New Alzheimer's Treatments

The potential implications are significant because APOE ε4 is the strongest common genetic risk factor for Alzheimer's disease. Approximately one in five people carries at least one copy of this gene variant.

Although carrying APOE ε4 does not guarantee that someone will develop Alzheimer's, it increases their likelihood of developing the disease. Understanding why certain carriers remain unaffected could reveal new opportunities for prevention and treatment.

The latest findings suggest several possible approaches.

One strategy would be to prevent harmful amounts of fibronectin from accumulating around the brain's blood vessels. Another would involve blocking the abnormal cellular signals triggered by excess fibronectin. A third possibility would be to restore the growth factor signaling that allows astrocytes and blood vessel cells to maintain the blood-brain barrier.

Researchers are already investigating these possibilities through laboratory experiments.

However, translating the discovery into a treatment will require considerable care. Fibronectin performs essential functions throughout the body, including supporting tissue structure and helping damaged tissues heal. Any future therapy would therefore need to target its harmful accumulation or effects without disrupting these beneficial activities.

By identifying the molecular mechanisms involved, scientists now have a more precise framework for designing such treatments. The findings may also help researchers determine when interventions would be most effective and how to measure whether they are successfully protecting the brain.

"By showing how fibronectin contributes to early vascular damage, this study gives us new therapeutic targets and potential ways to measure whether treatments are working," says Richard Mayeux, chair of the Department of Neurology and a collaborator on the project. "Clinically, it connects a major genetic risk factor for Alzheimer's to an early disease process that may be possible to modify."

Although these potential therapies remain experimental, the discovery offers a promising new direction in Alzheimer's research. Rather than focusing only on the damage that develops as the disease progresses, scientists may eventually be able to reproduce a natural genetic defense that helps protect vulnerable brains before significant damage occurs.

Materials provided by Columbia University Irving Medical Center. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#3663. Mission Accomplished: Astronaut Luca Parmitano Earns Kona Finish Line

📅 Oct 13, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Astronaut Luca Parmitano crosses the finish line at the 2026 Ironman World Championship. (Photo: Brian Metzler/Triathlete)

Journalist Brian Metzler was on the ground in Kona for the 2026 Ironman World Championship, where he watched astronaut Luca Parmitano race and spoke with him before and after. This is Parmitano’s second Ironman World Championship, and first time as a competitor who qualified for his spot on the start line.

Sometime next year, Luca Parmitano will navigate his way through space for a few million miles in a two-week low Earth orbit as the pilot of NASA’s Artemis III mission. Flying at Mach 25 (the fastest he’s flown), he will cover 140.6 miles in 26.7 seconds.

On Saturday, the Italian astronaut maneuvered his way through the 140.6-mile heaven-on-earth course of the 2026 Ironman World Championship in 10 hours, 27 minutes, and one second.

Parmitano, 50, has been an astronaut with the European Space Agency since 2009 and has been primarily based at the Johnson Space Center in Houston since 2010. He was the first Italian and third European to command the International Space Station in 2019-2020.

Competing in his eighth Ironman and second world championship, Parmitano had what he described as a near-perfect day. Despite particularly tough conditions, even for Kona, he accomplished his goal of finishing before the sun set and stars appeared in the skies overhead, crossing the finish line at 6:02 p.m. local time amid golden hues on the western horizon line.

“It was absolutely fantastic,” Parmitano told Triathlete after finishing with an Italian flag held high over his head. “I could not have asked for a better day. The weather was great. The people were great. I could feel the vibe of the island and the people the entire day. The entire day was beautiful.”

Parmitano started with a 1:14 swim split in the choppy waters of Kailua Bay, then turned in a strong 112-mile bike effort (5:29) on the windy bike course on the Queen K Highway to Hawi and back, before concluding his day with a stellar 26.2-mile run. His 3:32 run split ranked him 27th out of 247 in his age group.

His efforts combined for 56th place in the men’s 50–54 age group and a two-hour personal best on the Kona course. But he said his day was less about his specific performance and more about proving to himself that he belonged.

In 2014, Parmitano participated in the Ironman World Championship in Kona as an invited participant along with NASA astronaut Chris Cassidy. Between doing spacewalks and conducting experiments together, they trained while aboard the International Space Station and later back in Houston. Parmitano finished the race that year in 12:30:30, but he said he left feeling like a bit of an imposter at someone else’s party.

Amid other space station missions and astronaut work, Parmitano continued immersing himself in triathlon as a way to stay physically fit and mentally sharp. He spent the past several years based in Houston trying to earn his way back to Kona as confirmation he can do it.

On Saturday evening, with his wife, Marta, and their young daughter, Maia, to watch him finish, it was clear that the mission was accomplished.

“Today is deliverance,” he said. “Twelve years ago, I was invited. I did not qualify. I didn’t earn my spot. I was invited because I’m an astronaut. And when I left, I felt like I didn’t belong. Today, being able to finish the Ironman World Championship like everybody else, suffering under the same sun, the same wind, the same waves, I feel like I belong.”

Although he had trained for triathlons consistently through the years under the guidance of coach John Mayfield, his road to Kona began in earnest when he completed the 2022 Ironman Frankfurt in 10:42:14, a one-hour personal best.

In 2023, he unexpectedly qualified for the world championship with a third-place age-group finish (9:56:53) at Ironman Texas. However, he declined the entry, in part because he was getting married later that year. Also, he really wanted to return to Kona, and 2023 was the first year of the split-gender championships with the men’s race in Nice, France.

He returned to Ironman Texas in 2024, but a crash on the bike took him out of contention for a qualifying slot even after he rebounded with a strong run to finish in 10:08:00. In 2026, he raced Ironman Texas again, finishing third in his age group in 9:50:30, finally earning the return ticket to Kona that he so coveted.

Although he wasn’t intent on chasing high-level race goals in Kona, Parmitano arrived as fit and focused as he has ever been. He wore a bright blue Sanini race kit with the colors of the Italian flag and his nickname “Astro Luca” emblazoned on it, rode an all-black Ventum One bike with a chrome Rudy Project aero helmet, and ran in a pair of bright red Nike Vaporfly 4 carbon-plated super shoes.

The varying weather leading up to the race might have been nerve-racking to other triathletes, but not to Parmitano. The weather forecast was full of unknowns, and on race day there was rain in the morning when he arrived, overcast skies during the swim and a mix of clouds, and wind and sun on the bike and run.

“Because I’ve been to space, I learned to enjoy the weather and to think the weather is perfect, whatever the weather is,” he said. “The sky’s perfect, but it’s always changing. Perfection is not fixed. It’s ever-changing. And today, the day was always changing, so it was a perfect day.”

Training for astronaut work usually takes eight to 10 hours per day during the week, leaving Parmitano about two hours per day for triathlon training. He says there are similarities to his hardest moments of training and racing and the challenges of space missions in that both make him feel alive. In both cases, he says everything goes quiet and falls away except himself, and he believes that skill of capturing the present moment carries into the rest of life.

Parmitano says pushing himself to his perceived human limits swimming, biking, and running while on Earth and the curiosity and wonder of looking down at the planet from space have given him an awareness that the planet doesn’t belong to us; all of humankind belongs to it.

“I have come to the conclusion our purpose is to leave this place a little better,” he said. “Everybody has their own way to contribute. This is our only planet, and we have to take care of it. It doesn’t belong to us. We belong in it, but it belongs to the future.”

#4192. Brain scans reveal which disorders are linked to faster brain aging

📅 Sep 29, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

People with dementia, mild cognitive impairment, alcohol addiction, and psychiatric conditions such as schizophrenia may show signs of accelerated brain aging, according to a study published in the open access journal PLOS Medicine. The research, led by Shile Qi of the Nanjing University of Aeronautics and Astronautics, China, found that different conditions appear to be associated with distinct patterns of aging across the brain.

Researchers can estimate whether a person's brain appears older or younger than expected for their chronological age. One way to do this is with predictive age difference (PAD), which compares a person's actual age with an age estimated from brain imaging. A positive PAD means the brain appears older than would typically be expected.

To investigate how different brain conditions might influence this measure, the researchers analyzed structural magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) data from 45,900 controls drawn from several brain imaging databases. They compared those scans with data from 2,698 people with a range of conditions and differences, including attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD), autism spectrum disorder (ASD), alcohol or tobacco addiction, Alzheimer's disease (AD), mild cognitive impairment (MCI), schizophrenia, bipolar disorder or major depressive disorder.

Alzheimer's and MCI Show the Strongest Associations

Among the conditions examined, the neurodegenerative disorders AD and MCI showed the strongest associations with higher PAD values, suggesting the greatest degree of accelerated brain aging.

Addiction and psychiatric disorders were also associated with increased PAD. By contrast, the researchers found no overall differences in PAD between controls and people with ADHD or ASD.

Different Disorders Affect Different Brain Regions

The team also examined PAD across individual brain regions and looked at patterns of gene expression associated with the different conditions.

One area that stood out was the prefrontal cortex, which showed higher PAD across multiple brain disorders. Psychiatric disorders were associated with elevated PAD in the frontal and temporal lobes, while dementia was linked to higher PAD in the frontal and occipital cortex.

Addiction showed a different pattern. Higher PAD was observed in the default mode network, as well as in the salience network, putamen and thalamus.

The researchers also identified differences in gene transcription associated with particular conditions and divergences, suggesting that the patterns of brain aging may be linked to distinct underlying biological processes.

The findings do not show that these conditions directly cause accelerated brain aging. The study is correlational, and some conditions, particularly psychiatric disorders and addiction, frequently occur together, which can make their individual effects difficult to separate.

Even so, the researchers suggest that studying PAD more closely could eventually help identify biomarkers associated with common brain disorders and provide new clues about the biological pathways involved.

The authors add, "Different neurological disorders appear to leave different signatures on the brain aging clock, which may help researchers better understand the neural and biological pathways involved in these conditions."

This work was supported by the Key Research and Development Plan of Jiangsu Province, China (BE2023668) to S.Q., and the National Natural Science Foundation of China (62376124) to S.Q. The funders had no role in study design, data collection and analysis, decision to publish, or preparation of the manuscript.

Materials provided by PLOS. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#4239. Scientists create a needle-thin brain implant that can do three jobs at once

📅 Sep 21, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

A new type of brain implant could give scientists a more precise way to study the brain and may eventually contribute to treatments for neurological conditions such as epilepsy.

Researchers from DTU, the University of Copenhagen, University College London, and other institutions have developed a long, needle-thin brain electrode equipped with microscopic channels. Known as the microfluidic Axialtrode (mAxialtrode), the device is designed to provide multiple functional points along the length of the implant. This allows researchers to record neural activity and deliver medication to specific locations across different parts of the brain.

The findings were published in the journal Advanced Science.

For now, the technology is primarily intended as a research tool. Scientists could use it to investigate how signals travel through different layers of the brain during processes involving epilepsy, memory, and decision-making.

Over the longer term, the researchers say the mAxialtrode could also have therapeutic applications. One possibility would be using the device to deliver drugs to precise locations while simultaneously applying electrical stimulation or light stimulation to selected areas of the brain.

Postdoc Kunyang Sui, who developed the mAxialtrode concept together with Associate Professor Christos Markos, says one of the main advantages is that several capabilities can be combined within a single implant. That could allow researchers to perform more precise experiments while reducing the need for multiple devices inserted into the brain.

"Most current brain implants are based on hard materials such as silicon, which can irritate the brain and trigger inflammatory reactions in the tissue. The new implant differs in that it is made of soft, plastic-like optical fibers and has a specially angled tip that makes it smaller and reduces the damage caused when it is placed in the brain," says Kunyang Sui.

Sui cautions that the technology is still far from routine clinical use. Extensive testing, additional development, and regulatory approvals would be required before it could be used to treat patients.

Moving Beyond Conventional Optical Fibers

Brain researchers currently use flat-ended optical fibers in many experiments. These thin fibers, made from glass or plastic, can carry light into deep regions of the brain. They are frequently used in optogenetics, a technique in which specific nerve cells are activated using light.

Conventional fibers, however, have an important limitation. They typically interact with brain tissue only at the end of the fiber, meaning researchers can stimulate or monitor just one location at a time.

The outermost end is called the distal tip, or the "nose" of the fiber. Light is emitted and contact with brain tissue occurs only at this point. As a result, scientists may be limited to measuring or stimulating one brain layer at a time, even though many brain functions depend on communication among several layers and deeper structures.

The needle-thin mAxialtrode begins as a much larger polymer rod. Researchers heat the material and draw it into an extremely thin fiber, somewhat like producing a very fine strand of sugar, but with far greater precision.

A light-conducting core runs through the center of the fiber. Surrounding it are eight microscopic channels that can transport liquids. Those channels can also hold extremely thin metal wires used to measure electrical activity in the brain.

The finished fiber measures less than half a millimeter across. It is also highly flexible, allowing it to move along with brain tissue rather than pressing rigidly through it. This difference in stiffness could be important because harder implants can trigger inflammatory responses when they remain in the brain for long periods.

The researchers tested the system not only in the laboratory but also "in vivo," meaning in living mice. The electrode was implanted in the animals' brains and connected to light sources, recording equipment, and small pumps used to deliver fluids.

The experiments showed that the device could stimulate nerve cells using both blue and red light. At the same time, researchers were able to record electrical activity from shallow and deeper brain regions, including the cerebral cortex and hippocampus.

They were also able to inject different substances at separate depths, with delivery points spaced almost three millimeters apart. All of these measurements and forms of stimulation were carried out using a single lightweight fiber, which the mice were able to carry without any obvious signs of discomfort.

Potential Applications in Epilepsy and Neuroscience

The in vivo experiments and neurophysiological validation were performed in close collaboration with Associate Professor Rune W. Berg of the University of Copenhagen and Associate Professor Rob C. Wykes from University College London. Their contributions included expertise in analyzing neural circuits and models relevant to epilepsy.

The research team is now working to patent the technology behind the brain electrode. The scientists are also exploring what would be required to begin testing the device in patients within a clinical setting.

#4329. Your brain may not actually “make” decisions

📅 Aug 25, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

What we experience when making a decision may be very different from what actually happens inside the brain, according to Indiana University Professor Tom James.

For decades, both scientific theories and everyday intuition have tended to describe decision-making as a sequence. First we perceive something, then we think about it and make a choice, and finally we act. Each step is often assumed to correspond to a distinct brain function, moving from sensory processing to cognition and then to motor activity.

Many scientific approaches, particularly model-based cognitive neuroscience, are built around this same linear framework. It also matches how decision making feels from the inside. As James notes, "Our actions feel like they are caused by decisions based on desires, beliefs, and intentions."

Rethinking the Brain's Decision Making Process

James argues that this familiar explanation, sometimes called the "sandwich model," does not fit neatly with what scientists know about the brain. Sensation has identifiable sensory mechanisms, and action has identifiable motor mechanisms. But the supposed cognitive stage between the two does not appear to have a corresponding neural process that functions as a distinct decision maker.

Rather than proposing a dedicated decision-making system that directs behavior, James suggests that sensory, sensorimotor, and motor processes together produce what he calls 'action selection.' In this view, behavior emerges through ongoing interactions among the brain, body, and environment. These processes can occur simultaneously and feed back into one another rather than unfolding as a simple sequence.

That does not mean James believes decisions are unreal.

As James says, "Of course they do. We use this language all the time and it's very helpful in terms of describing behavior. The leap, I think, is to say that the brain works by having decision-making or control processes. It produces behavior that is well described in that way. But it doesn't need a process that does that to make it look that way."

James, a professor in the Department of Psychological and Brain Sciences in the College of Arts and Sciences, presents the argument in "Sensorimotor Mechanisms of Decisions and Actions," an article just published in the Journal of Cognitive Neuroscience.

What Happens in the Brain When We Make a Decision?

James develops his argument using a "physicalist" framework associated with philosophers such as Daniel Dennett. The approach makes explicit what James sees as a basic assumption underlying science: physical phenomena can cause both physical and nonphysical phenomena, while nonphysical phenomena cannot themselves cause physical events.

Sensory and motor processes, for example, are physical. Decisions, in this framework, are nonphysical. If that is the case, James argues, a decision cannot literally cause a physical action.

To make the idea easier to understand, he uses several analogies.

Drawing on Daniel Dennett's comparison between the self and a center of mass (CoM) or center of gravity, James suggests that decisions may function in a similar way. A center of mass is a useful mathematical concept, but it cannot independently exert a physical force. (i.e., you cannot move an object's center of mass without moving the object) In the same sense, James proposes that a decision may be an abstract description rather than a physical entity that directly causes something to happen.

Another analogy shows how useful concepts can become less informative when scientists need to understand events at a more detailed level.

We routinely use the phrase "the university" to describe the activities of an institution. The term conveniently represents a collection of people, buildings, departments, and processes. Saying that "the university took certain actions during a campus protest," however, tells us little about the specific events involved. A detailed explanation might instead require examining meetings between administrators, phone calls to state police, and other individual actions.

James argues that decisions present a similar problem for neuroscience. They may provide a useful high-level description of behavior without revealing the physical mechanisms producing it.

As James maintains: "As mental phenomena, they are defined on too abstract a level for the goals of cognitive neuroscience."

In other words, simply saying that someone made a decision does not explain what happened inside the brain.

James uses a third example to push the argument further.

He points to a robot constructed from a small number of sensory, motor, and sensorimotor modules. The machine demonstrates "wall-following" behavior that can appear purposeful. From the outside, it seems to have goals, strategies, and perhaps even something resembling intentions.

But the robot contains no system designed to make decisions.

"The robot does not have decisions built into it," James explains. "It just senses its environment and moves around accordingly. And based on the environment, wall-following turns out to be a good thing. It looks intentional. It looks strategic. It looks like the robot is making decisions. And yet, it is not. The reason we know it is not is that there are no systems built into it to do that."

That raises a provocative question. If a relatively simple machine can produce behavior that looks intentional without possessing a decision-making system, could human behavior also appear to result from centralized decisions even if no such central process exists?

James argues that this explanation is more parsimonious than assuming the brain contains what he calls "a higher-level, central controller that monitors and regulates sensory and motor processes."

The idea of a central controller also creates a philosophical problem that has been discussed since the time of Descartes.

If some higher-level entity inside the brain is responsible for observing information and deciding what to do, scientists still need to explain how that controller itself works.

"Explaining that the brain works by way of a central controller suggests that you haven't figured out how the brain works, because you've just put a person inside your brain," says James. "Dennett called this idea the Cartesian Theater. That person inside your brain would need another person inside its brain, which would need a person inside its brain and so on, in an infinite regress. So the problem is never solved. It's just passed on."

Instead of invoking an internal decision maker, James proposes focusing directly on the interacting sensory and motor systems that generate behavior.

If decision making emerges from continuous interactions among the brain, body, and environment, studying it will require experimental methods capable of capturing that complexity.

James acknowledges that this creates both exciting opportunities and difficult methodological challenges. Researchers would need to move beyond strictly linear models and examine processes that happen simultaneously, influence one another, and change as a person interacts with the surrounding world.

His own laboratory has begun exploring this direction by drawing on ideas from embodied cognition and ecological psychology.

James believes this approach could help cognitive neuroscience uncover the mechanisms that give rise to what we describe as decision-making. It may also offer new ways to investigate many other cognitive and mental phenomena that have traditionally been treated as distinct processes inside the brain.
